  2. For every \(\delta \in (0, 1)\), \(\theta \in (0, 1 - \delta)\), \(\ell, \ell' \geqslant 1\), we have the estimates

    \(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle \| \nabla_{Y_0} Y_t (Y_0) [h] \|_{C^{\theta}_{\ell'} (\mathbb{R}_+, C_{- \ell}^{2 - 2 \theta - 2 \delta} (\mathbb{R}^2)) \oplus L^{\infty} (\mathbb{R}_+, \tilde{B}_Y)}\)
    \(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle \lesssim_{g_{\varepsilon}}\) \(\displaystyle \mathfrak{P}_2 (\| f_{\varepsilon} : e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X_t)} : e^{\alpha P_t (g_{\varepsilon} \ast h)} \|_{L^{\infty}_{\ell'} (\mathbb{R}_+, L^{\infty}_{- \ell} (\mathbb{R}^2))}, \| h \|_{\tilde{B}_Y}), \quad h \in \tilde{B}_Y,\) (3.12)

    and

    \(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle \| \nabla_{X_0} Y_t (Y_0) [h] \|_{C^{\theta}_{\ell'} (\mathbb{R}_+, C_{- \ell}^{2 - 2 \theta - 2 \delta} (\mathbb{R}^2))}\)
    \(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle \lesssim_{g_{\varepsilon}}\) \(\displaystyle \tilde{\mathfrak{P}}_2 (\|f_{\varepsilon} : e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X_t)} : e^{\alpha P_t (g_{\varepsilon} \ast h)} \|_{L^{\infty}_{\ell'} (\mathbb{R}_+, L^{\infty}_{- \ell} (\mathbb{R}^2))}), \quad h \in \tilde{B}_X,\) (3.13)

    where \(\mathfrak{P}_2\) and \(\tilde{\mathfrak{P}}_2\) are two second degree polynomials.

  3. For every \(\ell, \kappa \geqslant 0\), we have that there exist \(\beta, \delta > 0\) such that

    \(\displaystyle \| \nabla_{X_0}^2 Y_t (Y_0) \|_{L (H^{- \kappa}_{\ell}, H^{\kappa}_{- \ell})} \lesssim \left( \int_{\mathbb{R}^2} \alpha^2 f_{\varepsilon} (z') : e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X_t) (z')} : e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast Y_t) (z')} e^{(\delta - m^2) t} (1 + | z' |^{\beta}) \mathrm{d} z' \right)^2 . \) (3.14)

The remainder of the present subsection is devoted to the proof of Proposition 3.5.

Proof of Proposition 3.5. Let us start by proving that \(G^{\lambda}_{\varepsilon}\) is a classical solution of the resolvent equation. We exploit Itô's formula appearing in Theorem 4.32 in [DZ14]. Notice that it can be applied to \(G^{\lambda}_{\varepsilon}\) since, by point i. in Proposition 3.8, \(G_{\varepsilon}^{\lambda}\) is a \(C^2\)-function with trace-class second derivative (continuity of the second derivative can be deduced with similar techniques as the ones adopted in Proposition E.7 and point i. in Proposition 3.5).

We use the notation \(X_t^{X_0}\) to denote a process \(X\) at time \(t\), with starting point \(X_0\) at time \(t = 0\). Recalling the definition of \(G^{\lambda}_{\varepsilon}\) in equation (3.2), we have

\(\displaystyle G_{\varepsilon}^{\lambda} (X_t^{X_0}, Y_t^{Y_0}) = \int_0^{+ \infty} e^{- \lambda s} \mathbb{E}_{\xi} [F (X_s^{X_t}, Y_s^{Y_t}) |\mathcal{H}_t] \mathrm{d} s,\)

where \((\mathcal{H}_t)_{t > 0}\) is the filtration generated by the white noise at time \(t\) and the initial conditions \(X_0\) and \(Y_0\). By Markovianity of the process, we get

\(\displaystyle G_{\varepsilon}^{\lambda} (X_t^{X_0}, Y_t^{Y_0}) = \int_0^{+ \infty} e^{- \lambda s} \mathbb{E}_{\xi} [F (X_{t + s}^{X_0}, Y_{t + s}^{Y_0}) |\mathcal{H}_t] \mathrm{d} s.\)

Since from now on \(X\) and \(Y\) always start at \(X_0\) and \(Y_0\), respectively, when \(t = 0\), we drop the dependence on the initial conditions. We have, by Itô's formula,

\begin{eqnarray*} & & e^{- \lambda t} G_{\varepsilon}^{\lambda} (X_t, Y_t) - G_{\varepsilon}^{\lambda} (X_0, Y_0)\\ & = & \int_0^t e^{- \lambda s} \mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon} (G_{\varepsilon}^{\lambda} (X_s, Y_s)) \mathrm{d} s + \int_0^t e^{- \lambda s} \nabla_{X_0} G_{\varepsilon}^{\lambda} (X_s, Y_s) \cdot \mathrm{d} X_s - \lambda \int_0^t e^{- \lambda s} G_{\varepsilon}^{\lambda} (X_s, Y_s) \mathrm{d} s. \end{eqnarray*}

On the other side of the equation, we have

\(\displaystyle \int_0^{+ \infty} e^{- \lambda (t + s)} \mathbb{E}_{\xi} [F (X_{t + s}, Y_{t + s}) |\mathcal{H}_t] \mathrm{d} s - \int_0^{+ \infty} e^{- \lambda s} \mathbb{E}_{\xi} [F (X_s, Y_s) |\mathcal{H}_t] \mathrm{d} s.\)

Notice that

\(\displaystyle \mathbb{E} \int_0^{+ \infty} e^{- \lambda (t + s)} \mathbb{E} [F (X_{t + s}, Y_{t + s}) |\mathcal{H}_t] \mathrm{d} s =\mathbb{E} \int_t^{+ \infty} e^{- \lambda s} \mathbb{E} [F (X_s, Y_s) |\mathcal{H}_t] \mathrm{d} s,\)

and therefore

\(\displaystyle \mathbb{E} \int_t^{+ \infty} e^{- \lambda s} \mathbb{E} [F (X_s, Y_s) |\mathcal{H}_t] \mathrm{d} s -\mathbb{E} \int_0^{+ \infty} e^{- \lambda s} \mathbb{E} [F (X_s, Y_s) |\mathcal{H}_t] \mathrm{d} s = -\mathbb{E} \int_0^t e^{- \lambda s} \mathbb{E} [F (X_s, Y_s) |\mathcal{H}_t] \mathrm{d} s.\)

Dividing by \(t\) and letting \(t \rightarrow 0\), we get

\(\displaystyle - \frac{1}{t} \mathbb{E} \int_0^t e^{- \lambda s} \mathbb{E} [F (X_s, Y_s) |\mathcal{H}_t] \mathrm{d} s \rightarrow - F (X_0, Y_0) .\)

Taking expectation also on the first side, we have the desired relation.

Proof of point i. When \(F \in \operatorname{Cyl}_{B_X \times B_Y}^b\), the derivative of \(G^{\lambda}_{\varepsilon} (X_0, Y_0)\) with respect to \(Y_0\) \(\)is given by

\(\displaystyle \nabla_{Y_0} G^{\lambda}_{\varepsilon} (X_0, Y_0) (h) =\mathbb{E}_{\xi} \left[ \int_0^{+ \infty} e^{- \lambda t} \left( \sum_{k = 1}^N \partial_k \tilde{F} \langle \nabla_{Y_0} Y_t (h), v_k \rangle \right) \mathrm{d} t \right] .\)

Therefore, we have

\(\displaystyle | \nabla_{Y_0} G^{\lambda}_{\varepsilon} (X_0, Y_0) (h) | \lesssim \sup_{k = 1, \ldots, N} \mathbb{E}_{\xi} \left[ \int_0^{+ \infty} e^{- \lambda t} \| \partial_k \tilde{F} \|_{L^{\infty}} | \langle \nabla_{Y_0} Y_t (h), v_k \rangle | \mathrm{d} t \right] .\)

Applying the estimate (3.12), we get

\begin{eqnarray*} | \langle \nabla_{Y_0} Y_t (h), v_k \rangle | & \lesssim & \rho_{\ell'} (t) \| \nabla_{Y_0} Y_t (Y_0) (h) \|_{C^{\theta}_{\ell'} (\mathbb{R}_+, C_{- \ell}^{2 - 2 \theta - 2 \delta} (\mathbb{R}^2)) \oplus L^{\infty} (\mathbb{R}_+, \tilde{B}_Y)} \| v_k \|_{\tilde{B}_Y^{\ast}}\\ & \lesssim & \rho_{\ell'} (t) P_2 (\|f_{\varepsilon} : e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X_t)} : e^{\alpha P_t (g_{\varepsilon} \ast h)} \|_{L^{\infty}_{\ell'} (\mathbb{R}_+, L^{\infty}_{- \ell} (\mathbb{R}^2))}, \|h\|_{\tilde{B}_Y}) \|v_k \|_{\tilde{B}_Y^{\ast}} . \end{eqnarray*}

Therefore,

\begin{eqnarray*} | \nabla_{Y_0} G^{\lambda}_{\varepsilon} (X_0, Y_0) (h) | & \lesssim & \sup_k (\| \partial_k \tilde{F} \|_{L^{\infty}} \| v_k \|_{\tilde{B}_Y^{\ast}}) \int_0^{+ \infty} e^{- \lambda t} \rho_{\ell'} (t) \\ & & \times \mathbb{E}_{\xi} [P_2 (\|f_{\varepsilon} : e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X_t)} : e^{\alpha P_t (g_{\varepsilon} \ast h)} \|_{L^{\infty}_{\ell'} (\mathbb{R}_+, L^{\infty}_{- \ell} (\mathbb{R}^2))}, \| h \|_{\tilde{B}_Y})] \mathrm{d} t. \end{eqnarray*}

We have that

\begin{eqnarray*} & & \mathbb{E}_{\xi} [P_2 (\|f_{\varepsilon} : e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X_t)} : e^{\alpha P_t (g_{\varepsilon} \ast h)} \|_{L^{\infty}_{\ell'} (\mathbb{R}_+, L^{\infty}_{- \ell} (\mathbb{R}^2))}, \|h\|_{\tilde{B}_Y})]\\ & \lesssim & R_{2, \varepsilon} \left( \|f_{\varepsilon} e^{\alpha | g_{\varepsilon} \ast X_0 |} \|_{L^{\infty}}, e^{\alpha^2 \sup_{t \geqslant 0} \mathbb{E} [(g_{\varepsilon} \ast X_t)^2]}, e^{\alpha \|h\|_{\tilde{B}_Y}} \right), \end{eqnarray*}

where \(R_{2, \varepsilon}\) is a suitable second degree polynomial. Taking the supremum over \(h \in \tilde{B}_Y\) with \(| h | \leqslant 1\), recalling that \(\tilde{B}_Y^{\ast} \subset B^{(2 - s) \wedge (\gamma (r - 1)) + \delta}_{l, l, - \ell} (\mathbb{R}^2)\), for some \(l \in (1, + \infty)\), and taking \(f_{G_{\varepsilon}^{\lambda}} (X_0)\) as in Definition 3.1 to be proportional to \(\| f_{\varepsilon} \exp (\alpha | g_{\varepsilon} \ast X_0 |) \|_{L^{\infty}}^2 + 1\), we get that \(G_{\varepsilon}^{\lambda}\) satisfies the first condition of Definition 3.1. For the remaining conditions of Definition 3.1, similar arguments with the application of inequalities (3.13) and (3.14) allow us to conclude that \(G_{\varepsilon}^{\lambda} \in \mathcal{F}\).

Since in our approximation we have that \(\mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon} (X, Y) = \alpha f_{\varepsilon} e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast (X + Y)) - \frac{\alpha^2}{2} c_{\varepsilon}}\), then the sum \(\varphi_t^{\varepsilon} = X^{\varepsilon}_t + Y^{\varepsilon}_t\) solves the SPDE

\(\displaystyle (\partial_t - \Delta + m^2) \varphi_t^{\varepsilon} = - \alpha f_{\varepsilon} e^{\alpha g_{\varepsilon} \ast \varphi_t^{\varepsilon} - \frac{\alpha^2}{2} c_{\varepsilon}} + \xi_t .\)

Therefore, if \(F \in \operatorname{Cyl}^b_{B_X \times B_Y}\) is of the form \(F = \bar{F} \circ P^{X + Y}\) for some \(\bar{F} \in \operatorname{Cyl}^b_E\), we have

\(\displaystyle G_{\varepsilon}^{\lambda} (X_0, Y_0) = \bar{G}_{\varepsilon}^{\lambda} (X_0 + Y_0) =\mathbb{E}_{\xi} \left[ \int_0^{+ \infty} e^{- \lambda t} \bar{F} (\varphi_t^{\varepsilon}) \hspace{1em} \mathrm{d} t | \varphi_0 = X_0 + Y_0 \right] .\)

Proof of point ii. In the following, for \(j = 1, 2\), we denote by \((X^{\varepsilon, j}_t, Y^{\varepsilon, j}_t)\) the solution to the system of equations (3.3)–(3.4) with initial conditions \((X_0, Y_0) \sim \mu_j\). Sometimes we also write \((X_0^j, Y_0^j)\) to indicate that \((X_0, Y_0) \sim \mu_j\), for \(j = 1, 2\). By definition of \(G_{\varepsilon}^{\lambda}\) (i.e. by equation (3.2)), we have

\begin{eqnarray*} & & \lambda \int G^{\lambda}_{\varepsilon} (X_0, Y_0) \hspace{1em} \mu_1 (\mathrm{d} X_0, \mathrm{d} Y_0) - \lambda \int G^{\lambda}_{\varepsilon} (X_0, Y_0) \hspace{1em} \mu_2 (\mathrm{d} X_0, \mathrm{d} Y_0)\\ & = & \lambda \int \int_0^{+ \infty} e^{- \lambda t} \mathbb{E}_{\xi} [F (X^{\varepsilon, 1}_t, Y^{\varepsilon, 1}_t)] \hspace{1em} \mathrm{d} t \hspace{1em} \mathrm{d} \mu_1 - \lambda \int \int_0^{+ \infty} e^{- \lambda t} \mathbb{E}_{\xi} [F (X^{\varepsilon, 2}_t, Y^{\varepsilon, 2}_t)] \hspace{1em} \mathrm{d} t \hspace{1em} \mathrm{d} \mu_2\\ & = & \lambda \int_0^{+ \infty} e^{- \lambda t} \mathbb{E} [F (X^{\varepsilon, 1}_t, Y^{\varepsilon, 1}_t) - F (X^{\varepsilon, 2}_t, Y^{\varepsilon, 2}_t)] \mathrm{d} t, \end{eqnarray*}

Notice that, for any \(\varsigma > 0\), there exist two compact sets \(K_1, K_2\), such that \(\mu_1 (K_1), \mu_2 (K_2) > 1 - \varsigma\). Therefore, the following inequality holds

\begin{eqnarray*} & & \left| \lambda \int_0^{+ \infty} e^{- \lambda t} \mathbb{E} [F (X^{\varepsilon, 1}_t, Y^{\varepsilon, 1}_t) - F (X^{\varepsilon, 2}_t, Y^{\varepsilon, 2}_t)] \mathrm{d} t \right|\\ & \leqslant & \left| \lambda \int_0^{+ \infty} e^{- \lambda t} \mathbb{E} [| F (X^{\varepsilon, 1}_t, Y^{\varepsilon, 1}_t) - F (X^{\varepsilon, 2}_t, Y^{\varepsilon, 2}_t) | \mathbb{I}_{(X^1_0, Y^1_0) \in K_1, (X^2_0, Y^2_0) \in K_2}] \mathrm{d} t \right| + 2 \varsigma \| F \|_{L^{\infty}} \lambda \int_0^{+ \infty} e^{- \lambda \tau} \mathrm{d} \tau . \end{eqnarray*}

Let us then focus on the case where \((X^1_0, Y^1_0) \in K_1\) and \((X^2_0, Y^2_0) \in K_2\). By Lagrange's theorem, we have

\(\displaystyle F (X^{\varepsilon, 1}_t, Y^{\varepsilon, 1}_t) - F (X^{\varepsilon, 2}_t, Y^{\varepsilon, 2}_t) = \int_0^1 \langle \mathrm{d} F_{\beta}, \mathfrak{D}_{\beta} \rangle \hspace{1em} \mathrm{d} \beta,\)

where, for \(\beta \in (0, 1)\),

\(\displaystyle \mathrm{d} F_{\beta} = \mathrm{d} F (X^{\varepsilon}_t ((1 - \beta) X^1_0 + \beta X^2_0), Y^{\varepsilon}_t ((1 - \beta) Y^1_0 + \beta Y^2_0)),\)

with \(\mathrm{d} F (X, Y) = \sum_{j = 1}^N \partial_j \tilde{F} \langle u_j, X \rangle + \sum_{j = 1}^M \partial_j \tilde{F} \langle v_j, Y \rangle\), for \(F \in \operatorname{Cyl}^b_{B_X \times B_Y}\) having the following form \(F (X, Y) = \tilde{F} (\langle u_1, X \rangle, \ldots, \langle u_N, X \rangle, \langle v_1, Y \rangle, \ldots, \langle v_M, Y \rangle)\), with \(\tilde{F} \in C^2_b (\mathbb{R}^{N + M})\) and \(u_i \in \mathcal{S} (\mathbb{R}^2)\), \(i = 1, \ldots, N\), and \(v_i \in \mathcal{S} (\mathbb{R}^2)\), \(i = 1, \ldots, M\), and, for \(\beta \in (0, 1)\),

\(\displaystyle \mathfrak{D}_{\beta} = (\nabla_{X^1_0 - X^2_0} X^{\varepsilon}_t ((1 - \beta) X^1_0 + \beta X^2_0), 0, \nabla_{X^1_0 - X^2_0} Y^{\varepsilon}_t ((1 - \beta) Y^1_0 + \beta Y^2_0), \nabla_{Y^1_0 - Y^2_0} Y^{\varepsilon}_t ((1 - \beta) Y^1_0 + \beta Y^2_0)) .\)

We want to get some bounds for \(\left| \int_0^1 \langle \mathrm{d} F_{\beta}, \mathfrak{D}_{\beta} \rangle \mathrm{d} \beta \right|\).

For the term \(| \langle \mathrm{d} F_{\beta}, \nabla_{X^1_0 - X^2_0} X^{\varepsilon}_t ((1 - \beta) X^1_0 + \beta X^2_0) \rangle |\) we have

\begin{eqnarray*} | \langle \mathrm{d} F_{\beta}, \nabla_{X^1_0 - X^2_0} X^{\varepsilon}_t ((1 - \beta) X^1_0 + \beta X^2_0) \rangle | & = & | \langle \mathrm{d} F_{\beta}, P_t ((1 - \beta) X^1_0 + \beta X^2_0) \rangle |\\ & \leqslant & e^{- m^2 t} \| \mathrm{d} F_{\beta} \| \| (1 - \beta) X^1_0 + \beta X^2_0 \|_{C^{- \delta}_{\ell}} . \end{eqnarray*}

For the term \(| \langle \mathrm{d} F_{\beta}, \nabla_{Y^1_0 - Y^2_0} Y^{\varepsilon}_t ((1 - \beta) Y^1_0 + \beta Y^2_0) \rangle |\), considering equation (3.8), and multiplying it by \(\tilde{g}_{\varepsilon} \ast \nabla_{Y_0} Y_t\) and exploiting the negativity of the non-linearity, we get the following a priori estimate for some constant \(k \in (0, m^2)\)

\(\displaystyle \partial_t \| \tilde{g}_{\varepsilon} \ast \nabla_{Y^1_0 - Y^2_0} Y^{\varepsilon}_t ((1 - \beta) Y^1_0 + \beta Y^2_0) \|_{L^2}^2 + k \| \tilde{g}_{\varepsilon} \ast \nabla_{Y^1_0 - Y^2_0} Y^{\varepsilon}_t ((1 - \beta) Y^1_0 + \beta Y^2_0) \|_{L^2}^2 \leqslant 0,\)

and therefore, by Gronwall lemma,

\(\displaystyle \| \tilde{g}_{\varepsilon} \ast \nabla_{Y^1_0 - Y^2_0} Y^{\varepsilon}_t ((1 - \beta) Y^1_0 + \beta Y^2_0) \|_{L^2}^2 \lesssim e^{- k t},\)

and the estimate is independent of \(\varepsilon\).

Consider now the operator \(f \mapsto \tilde{g}_{\varepsilon} \ast f\), and recall that in Fourier representation convolution corresponds to multiplication, i.e. the previous operator can be viewed as \(\hat{f} \mapsto \widehat{\tilde{g}}_{\varepsilon} \cdot \hat{f}\). When \(\hat{f}\) has compact support, such an operator is invertible if \(0 < \varepsilon \leqslant \varepsilon_0\) for some positive constant \(\varepsilon_0\) depending on the size of the support of \(\hat{f}\), with inverse given by

\(\displaystyle \hat{\mathcal{Q}}_{\varepsilon} : \hat{f} \mapsto \frac{1}{\widehat{\tilde{g}}_{\varepsilon}} \cdot \hat{f},\)

which is a well-defined operation because \(\widehat{\tilde{g}}_{\varepsilon} (k) = \widehat{\tilde{g}}_1 (\varepsilon k)\), \(\widehat{\tilde{g}}_1 (0) = 1\), and \(\widehat{\tilde{g}}_1\) is smooth since \(\tilde{g}_1\) is a Schwartz function. Using the previous notation, we have

\begin{eqnarray*} | \langle \mathrm{d} F_{\beta}, \nabla_{Y^1_0 - Y^2_0} Y^{\varepsilon}_t ((1 - \beta) Y^1_0 + \beta Y^2_0) \rangle | & = & | \langle \mathrm{d} F_{\beta} \circ \mathcal{Q}_{\varepsilon}, \tilde{g}_{\varepsilon} \ast \nabla_{Y^1_0 - Y^2_0} Y^{\varepsilon}_t ((1 - \beta) Y^1_0 + \beta Y^2_0) \rangle |\\ & \lesssim & e^{- k t} \| \mathrm{d} F_{\beta} \circ \mathcal{Q}_{\varepsilon} \|\\ & \lesssim & e^{- k t} \sup_{0 < \varepsilon \leqslant \varepsilon_0} \| \mathrm{d} F_{\beta} \circ \mathcal{Q}_{\varepsilon} \|, \end{eqnarray*}

where we used the fact that \(F\) has compact support in Fourier variables and so the norm \(\| \mathrm{d} F_{\beta} \circ \mathcal{Q}_{\varepsilon} \|\) is bounded for any \(0 < \varepsilon \leqslant \varepsilon_0\), and also that

\(\displaystyle \sup_{\text{\scriptsize{$\begin{array}{c} 0 < \varepsilon < \varepsilon_0\\ k \in \operatorname{supp} (F) \end{array}$}}} | \widehat{\tilde{g}}_{\varepsilon} (k) |^{- 1} < + \infty .\)

Now, focus on the term \(| \langle \mathrm{d} F_{\beta}, \nabla_{X^1_0 - X^2_0} Y^{\varepsilon}_t ((1 - \beta) Y^1_0 + \beta Y^2_0) \rangle |\). By equation (3.9), we have

\begin{eqnarray*} & & \partial_t \| \nabla_{X^1_0 - X^2_0} Y^{\varepsilon}_t ((1 - \beta) Y^1_0 + \beta Y^2_0) \|_{L^2}^2 + \| \nabla_{X^1_0 - X^2_0} Y^{\varepsilon}_t ((1 - \beta) Y^1_0 + \beta Y^2_0) \|_{H^1}^2\\ & & + m^2 \| \nabla_{X^1_0 - X^2_0} Y^{\varepsilon}_t ((1 - \beta) Y^1_0 + \beta Y^2_0) \|_{L^2}^2\\ & \lesssim & \int_{\mathbb{R}^2} \mathrm{D}_X \mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon} (X^{\varepsilon}_t ((1 - \beta) X^1_0 + \beta X^2_0), Y^{\varepsilon}_t ((1 - \beta) Y^1_0 + \beta Y^2_0)) \nabla_{X^1_0 - X^2_0} X^{\varepsilon}_t ((1 - \beta) X^1_0 + \beta X^2_0)\\ & & \times \nabla_{X^1_0 - X^2_0} Y^{\varepsilon}_t ((1 - \beta) Y^1_0 + \beta Y^2_0) \mathrm{d} z, \end{eqnarray*}

and by Hölder and Young inequalities, and re-absorbing the terms properly, we have that, for any \(\varsigma > 0\), the following inequality holds

\begin{eqnarray*} & & \partial_t \| \nabla_{X^1_0 - X^2_0} Y^{\varepsilon}_t ((1 - \beta) Y^1_0 + \beta Y^2_0) \|_{L^2}^2 + k \| \nabla_{X^1_0 - X^2_0} Y^{\varepsilon}_t ((1 - \beta) Y^1_0 + \beta Y^2_0) \|_{L^2}^2\\ & \lesssim & \frac{1}{\varsigma} \| \mathrm{D}_X \mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon} (X^{\varepsilon}_t ((1 - \beta) X^1_0 + \beta X^2_0), Y^{\varepsilon}_t ((1 - \beta) Y^1_0 + \beta Y^2_0)) \|_{B^{- s}_{p, p}}^2 \| \nabla_{X^1_0 - X^2_0} X^{\varepsilon}_t ((1 - \beta) X^1_0 + \beta X^2_0) \|^2_{B^s_{q, q}} . \end{eqnarray*}

Now, we can bound the norm \(\| \nabla_{X^1_0 - X^2_0} X^{\varepsilon}_t ((1 - \beta) X^1_0 + \beta X^2_0) \|^2_{B^s_{q, q}}\) by a constant since \(X^1_0\) and \(X^2_0\) belong to compact sets. Moreover, by Lemma A.7, we have

\begin{eqnarray*} & & \partial_t \| \nabla_{X^1_0 - X^2_0} Y^{\varepsilon}_t ((1 - \beta) Y^1_0 + \beta Y^2_0) \|_{L^2}^2 + k \| \nabla_{X^1_0 - X^2_0} Y^{\varepsilon}_t ((1 - \beta) Y^1_0 + \beta Y^2_0) \|_{L^2}^2\\ & \lesssim & \frac{e^{- m^2 t}}{t^{\delta + s}} \| \mathrm{D}_X \mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon} (X^{\varepsilon}_t ((1 - \beta) X^1_0 + \beta X^2_0), 0) \|_{B^{- s}_{p, p}}^2 . \end{eqnarray*}

Applying Gronwall lemma yields the following inequality

\(\displaystyle \| \nabla_{X^1_0 - X^2_0} Y^{\varepsilon}_t ((1 - \beta) Y^1_0 + \beta Y^2_0) \|_{L^2}^2 \lesssim e^{- k t} \int_0^t \frac{e^{(k - m^2) \tau}}{\tau^{\delta + s}} \| \mathrm{D}_X \mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon} (X^{\varepsilon}_{\tau} ((1 - \beta) X^1_0 + \beta X^2_0), 0) \|_{B^{- s}_{p, p}}^2 \mathrm{d} \tau .\)

Thus, we get, for some constant \(C_{\varsigma} > 0\) depending on \(\varsigma > 0\),

\begin{eqnarray*} & & \mathbb{E} [\| \nabla_{X^1_0 - X^2_0} Y^{\varepsilon}_t ((1 - \beta) Y^1_0 + \beta Y^2_0) \|_{L^2} \mathbb{I}_{(X^1_0, Y^1_0) \in K_1, (X^2_0, Y^2_0) \in K_2}]\\ & \leqslant & \mathbb{E} [\| \nabla_{X^1_0 - X^2_0} Y^{\varepsilon}_t ((1 - \beta) Y^1_0 + \beta Y^2_0) \|_{L^2}^2 \mathbb{I}_{(X^1_0, Y^1_0) \in K_1, (X^2_0, Y^2_0) \in K_2}]^{1 / 2}\\ & \lesssim & C_{\varsigma} \left( e^{- k t} \int_0^t \frac{e^{(k - m^2) \tau}}{\tau^{\delta + s}} \mathbb{E} [ \| \mathrm{D}_X \mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon} (X^{\varepsilon}_{\tau} ((1 - \beta) X^1_0 + \beta X^2_0), 0) \|_{B^{- s}_{p, p}}^2] \mathrm{d} \tau \right)^{1 / 2}\\ & \lesssim & C_{\varsigma} \left( e^{- k t} \int_0^t \frac{e^{(k - m^2) \tau}}{\tau^{\delta + s}} \mathrm{d} \tau \right)^{1 / 2}, \end{eqnarray*}

since the law of \(X^{\varepsilon}_{\tau} ((1 - \beta) X^1_0 + \beta X^2_0)\) is that of a Gaussian free field independent of \(\beta \in (0, 1)\) and of \(\varepsilon > 0\).

Putting everything together, we get

\begin{eqnarray*} & & \mathbb{E} [(F (X^{\varepsilon, 1}_t, Y^{\varepsilon, 1}_t) - F (X^{\varepsilon, 2}_t, Y^{\varepsilon, 2}_t)) \mathbb{I}_{(X^1_0, Y^1_0) \in K_1, (X^2_0, Y^2_0) \in K_2}]\\ & \lesssim & e^{- m^2 t} \| \mathrm{d} F_{\beta} \| \mathbb{E} [\| (1 - \beta) X^1_0 + \beta X^2_0 \|_{C^{- \delta}_{\ell}}] + e^{- k t} \| \mathrm{d} F_{\beta} \| + C_{\varsigma} \left( e^{- k t} \int_0^t \frac{e^{(k - m^2) \tau}}{\tau^{\delta + s}} \mathrm{d} \tau \right)^{1 / 2}\\ & \lesssim & e^{- m^2 t} \| \mathrm{d} F_{\beta} \| + e^{- k t} \| \mathrm{d} F_{\beta} \| + C_{\varsigma} \left( e^{- k t} \int_0^t \frac{e^{(k - m^2) \tau}}{\tau^{\delta + s}} \mathrm{d} \tau \right)^{1 / 2}, \end{eqnarray*}

where we used that the law of \((1 - \beta) X^1_0 + \beta X^2_0\) does not depend on \(\beta \in (0, 1)\).

Therefore, we get

\begin{eqnarray*} & & \left| \lambda \int G^{\lambda}_{\varepsilon} (X_0, Y_0) \hspace{1em} \mu_1 (\mathrm{d} X_0, \mathrm{d} Y_0) - \lambda \int G^{\lambda}_{\varepsilon} (X_0, Y_0) \hspace{1em} \mu_2 (\mathrm{d} X_0, \mathrm{d} Y_0) \right|\\ & \lesssim & \left| \lambda \int_0^{+ \infty} \left[ e^{- \lambda t} e^{- m^2 t} \| \mathrm{d} F_{\beta} \| + e^{- k t} \sup_{0 < \varepsilon \leqslant \varepsilon_0} \| \mathrm{d} F_{\beta} \circ \mathcal{Q}_{\varepsilon} \| (1 + C_{\varsigma}) \left( 1 + \int_0^t \frac{e^{(k - m^2) \tau}}{\tau^{\delta + s}} \mathrm{d} \tau \right)^{1 / 2} \right] \mathrm{d} t \right|\\ & & + 2 \varsigma \| F \|_{L^{\infty}} \lambda \int_0^{+ \infty} e^{- \lambda \tau} \mathrm{d} \tau . \end{eqnarray*}

For some constant \(K > 0\), we have

\begin{eqnarray*} & & \left| \lambda \int_0^{+ \infty} \left[ e^{- \lambda t} e^{- m^2 t} \| \mathrm{d} F_{\beta} \| + e^{- k t} \sup_{0 < \varepsilon \leqslant \varepsilon_0} \| \mathrm{d} F_{\beta} \circ \mathcal{Q}_{\varepsilon} \| (1 + C_{\varsigma}) \left( 1 + \int_0^t \frac{e^{(k - m^2) \tau}}{\tau^{\delta + s}} \mathrm{d} \tau \right)^{1 / 2} \right] \mathrm{d} t \right|\\ & \lesssim & (1 + C_{\varsigma}) \lambda \int_0^{+ \infty} e^{- \lambda t - K t} \mathrm{d} t\\ & \lesssim & (1 + C_{\varsigma}) \frac{\lambda}{\lambda + K}, \end{eqnarray*}

and the last term converges to zero as \(\lambda \rightarrow 0\). Thus, we have

\(\displaystyle \left| \lambda \int G^{\lambda}_{\varepsilon} (X_0, Y_0) \hspace{1em} \mu_1 (\mathrm{d} X_0, \mathrm{d} Y_0) - \lambda \int G^{\lambda}_{\varepsilon} (X_0, Y_0) \hspace{1em} \mu_2 (\mathrm{d} X_0, \mathrm{d} Y_0) \right| \lesssim \varsigma + (1 + C_{\varsigma}) \frac{\lambda}{\lambda + K} .\)

Proof of point iii. Let \(\mathcal{K} \in B^{q, \ell / 2}_{\exp}\) and note that \(\nabla_{Y_0} Y^{\varepsilon}_t [\mathcal{K}]\) solves equation (3.8). We write

\(\displaystyle \nabla \mathcal{Y}_t^{\varepsilon} = \nabla_{Y_0} Y^{\varepsilon}_t [\mathcal{K}] - P_t \mathcal{K}. \) (3.15)

Recall that in the proof of point i. in Proposition 3.5 we saw that

\(\displaystyle \nabla_{Y_0} G^{\lambda}_{\varepsilon} (X_0, Y_0) =\mathbb{E}_{\xi} \left[ \int_0^{+ \infty} e^{- \lambda t} \mathrm{d} F (X_t, Y^{\varepsilon}_t) \nabla_{Y_0} Y_t^{\varepsilon} \mathrm{d} t \right] .\)

Now, recalling the representation (3.15) of \(\nabla \mathcal{Y}^{\varepsilon}_t\), we have

\begin{eqnarray*} & & \int \mathbb{E}_{\xi} \left[ \int_0^{+ \infty} e^{- \lambda t} \mathrm{d} F (X_t, Y^{\varepsilon}_t) \nabla_{Y_0} Y^{\varepsilon}_t [\mathcal{K}] \mathrm{d} t \right] \mathrm{d} \mu\\ & \lesssim & \| \mathrm{d} F \| \int \mathbb{E}_{\xi} \left[ \int_0^{+ \infty} e^{- \lambda t} \| \nabla_{Y_0} Y^{\varepsilon}_t [\mathcal{K}] \|_{B_{\exp}^{q, \ell / 2}} \mathrm{d} t \right] \mathrm{d} \mu\\ & \lesssim & \int \mathbb{E}_{\xi} \left[ \int_0^{+ \infty} e^{- \lambda t} (\| P_t \mathcal{K} \|_{B_{\exp}^{q, \ell / 2}} + \| \nabla \mathcal{Y}^{\varepsilon}_t \|_{L^2_{\ell} (\mathbb{R}^2)}) \mathrm{d} t \right] \mathrm{d} \mu . \end{eqnarray*}

By Lemma 3.9 below, we have

\begin{eqnarray*} & & \int \mathbb{E}_{\xi} \left[ \int_0^{+ \infty} e^{- \lambda t} \mathrm{d} F (X_t, Y^{\varepsilon}_t) \nabla_{Y_0} Y^{\varepsilon}_t [\mathcal{K}] \mathrm{d} t \right] \mathrm{d} \mu\\ & \lesssim & \int \mathbb{E}_{\xi} \left[ \int_0^{+ \infty} e^{- \lambda t} (1 + (1 + t)^{\sigma} \| f_{\varepsilon} : e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X_s)} : \|_{L^r_{\ell'''} (\mathbb{R}_+, B_{\exp}^{r, \ell / 2})}) \| \mathcal{K} \|_{B_{\exp}^{q, \ell / 2}} \mathrm{d} t \right] \mathrm{d} \mu . \end{eqnarray*}

Hölder inequality with respect to all measures, together with the fact that \(\lambda > 0\), yields

\begin{eqnarray*} & & \int \mathbb{E}_{\xi} \left[ \int_0^{+ \infty} e^{- \lambda t} \mathrm{d} F (X_t, Y^{\varepsilon}_t) \nabla_{Y_0} Y^{\varepsilon}_t [\mathcal{K}] \mathrm{d} t \right] \mathrm{d} \mu\\ & \lesssim_{\lambda} & \left( \int \| \mathcal{K} \|^q_{B_{\exp}^{q, \ell / 2}} \mathrm{d} \mu \right)^{1 / q} \left( \int \mathbb{E}_{\xi} [\| f_{\varepsilon} : e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X_t)} : \|^r_{L^r_{\ell'''} (\mathbb{R}_+, B_{\exp}^{r, \ell / 2})}] \mathrm{d} \mu \right)^{1 / r} \int_0^{+ \infty} e^{- \lambda t} (1 + (1 + t)^{\sigma}) \mathrm{d} t. \end{eqnarray*}

Here, since \(\lambda > 0\), the integral \(\int_0^{+ \infty} e^{- \lambda t} (1 + (1 + t)^{\sigma}) \mathrm{d} t\) is finite. Moreover, by Proposition B.3, we have that the term

\(\displaystyle \left( \int \mathbb{E}_{\xi} [\| f_{\varepsilon} : e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X_s)} : \|^r_{L^r_{\ell'''} (\mathbb{R}_+, B_{\exp}^{r, \ell / 2})}] \mathrm{d} \mu \right)^{1 / r}\)

is bounded with respect to \(\varepsilon > 0\). This concludes the proof of Proposition 3.5.\(\Box\)

We close the section with an auxiliary lemma used in the previous proof.

Lemma 3.9. Let \(\alpha^2 < 4 \pi \tilde{\gamma}_{\max}\). Then, for every \(\varepsilon > 0\), \(Y_0 \in B_Y^{\leqslant}\), and \(X_0\) in a set of full measure with respect to the free field measure \(\mu^{\operatorname{free}}\) with mass \(m > 0\), we have that

\(\displaystyle \| \nabla \mathcal{Y}^{\varepsilon}_t \|_{L^2_{\ell} (\mathbb{R}^2)} \lesssim (1 + t)^{\sigma} \| f_{\varepsilon} : e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X_s)} : \|_{L^r_{\ell'''} (\mathbb{R}_+, B_{\exp}^{r, \ell / 2})} \| \mathcal{K} \|_{B_{\exp}^{q, \ell / 2}} .\)

Proof. Let \(\gamma = \alpha^2 / (4 \pi) < \tilde{\gamma}_{\max}\). The proof is similar to the one of Theorem C.1. Indeed, by multiplying the equation for \(\nabla \mathcal{Y}^{\varepsilon}\) by \(g_{\varepsilon} \ast \nabla \mathcal{Y}^{\varepsilon}\), and integrating, we get

\begin{eqnarray*} & & \rho_{\ell'} (t) \| \tilde{g}_{\varepsilon} \ast \nabla \mathcal{Y}_t^{\varepsilon} \|_{L^2_{\ell}}^2 + \int_0^t \rho_{\ell'} (s) \| \tilde{g}_{\varepsilon} \ast \nabla \mathcal{Y}^{\varepsilon}_s \|_{H_{\ell}^1}^2 \hspace{1em} \mathrm{d} s\\ & & + \int_0^t \rho_{2 \ell} (z) \rho_{\ell'} (s) \alpha^2 f_{\varepsilon} : e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X_s) (z)} : e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast Y_s) (z)} (g_{\varepsilon} \ast \nabla \mathcal{Y}^{\varepsilon}_s (z))^2 \hspace{1em} \mathrm{d} s \hspace{1em} \mathrm{d} z\\ & \lesssim & \int_0^t \rho_{\ell'} (s) (\rho_{2 \ell} (z) f_{\varepsilon} : e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X_s) (z)} : e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast Y_s) (z)} (g_{\varepsilon} \ast e^{- (- \Delta + m^2) s} \mathcal{K})) g_{\varepsilon} \ast \nabla \mathcal{Y}^{\varepsilon}_s (z) \hspace{1em} \mathrm{d} s \hspace{1em} \mathrm{d} z. \end{eqnarray*}

In the last line, we have

\(\displaystyle \int_0^t \rho_{\ell'} (s) \rho_{2 \ell} (z) f_{\varepsilon} \underbrace{: e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X_s)} :}_{L^p (\mathbb{R}, B_{\exp}^{r, \ell / 2} (\mathbb{R}^2))} \underbrace{e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast Y_s)}}_{L^{\infty} (\mathbb{R}_+ \times \mathbb{R}^2)} \underbrace{(g_{\varepsilon} \ast e^{- (- \Delta + m^2) s} \mathcal{K})}_{L^{\infty} (t^{- \beta} \mathbb{R}_+, B^{- \gamma (q - 1) - \delta + 2 \beta - 2 / q}_{q, q, \ell / 2} (\mathbb{R}^2))} \underbrace{g_{\varepsilon} \ast \nabla \mathcal{Y}^{\varepsilon}_s}_{\text{\scriptsize{$\begin{array}{l} L_{\ell' / 2}^{\infty} (\mathbb{R}_+, L_{\ell}^2 (\mathbb{R}^2)) \cap\\ \cap L_{\ell' / 2}^2 (\mathbb{R}_+, H^1_{\ell} (\mathbb{R}^2)) \end{array}$}}} \hspace{1em} \mathrm{d} s.\)

Now, whenever \(\gamma < \tilde{\gamma}_{\max}\), there exist \(p, q \geqslant 1\), \(0 \leqslant \theta \leqslant 1\), \(0 \leqslant \beta \leqslant 1\), \(\delta > 0\), such that the following system has a solution

\(\displaystyle \left\{ \begin{array}{l} - \gamma (q - 1) + 2 \beta - \frac{2}{q} > 0,\\ - \gamma (p - 1) - \delta + \theta > 0,\\ \frac{1}{p} + \frac{1}{2} < 1,\\ \frac{1}{p} + \beta - \delta + \frac{\theta}{2} \leqslant 1. \end{array} \right.\)

The proof is complete.\(\Box\)

3.3Proof of uniqueness of solutions to Problem B

In the case of Problem B a better result can be obtained. It is useful to introduce a new approximating operator \(\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon, \bar{\varepsilon}}\) for \(\mathcal{L}\) of the form

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon, \bar{\varepsilon}} (\Phi) := \frac{1}{2} \operatorname{tr} (\nabla_X^2 \Phi) - \langle (- \Delta + m^2) X, \nabla_X \Phi \rangle - \langle (- \Delta + m^2) Y +\mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon, \bar{\varepsilon}} (X, Y), \nabla_Y \Phi \rangle,\)

where

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon, \bar{\varepsilon}} (X, Y) := \alpha f_{\varepsilon} (: e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X)} : e^{\alpha (g_{\bar{\varepsilon}} \ast Y)}) .\)

Here \(f_{\varepsilon}\) and \(g_{\varepsilon}\) are defined as in Section 2.2. Recall that by Remark 2.9, since \(\lim_{\bar{\varepsilon}, \varepsilon \rightarrow 0} \mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon, \bar{\varepsilon}} =\mathcal{L}\), Problem B with the operator \(\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon, \bar{\varepsilon}}\) is equivalent to the one with operator \(\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}\) defined in equation (2.14). We can then consider the resolvent equation associated to \(\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon, \bar{\varepsilon}}\), namely

\(\displaystyle (\lambda -\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon, \bar{\varepsilon}}) G^{\lambda}_{\varepsilon, \bar{\varepsilon}} = F,\)

where \(F \in \operatorname{Cyl}_{B_X \times B_Y}\) with compact support in Fourier variables. A solution to such equation is given by

\(\displaystyle G^{\lambda}_{\varepsilon, \bar{\varepsilon}} (X_0, Y_0) =\mathbb{E}_{\xi} \left[ \int_0^{+ \infty} e^{- \lambda t} F (X_t, Y^{\varepsilon, \bar{\varepsilon}}_t) \mathrm{d} t \right], \) (3.16)

where \(X_t, Y^{\varepsilon, \bar{\varepsilon}}_t\) solves the following system of equations

\(\displaystyle (\partial_t - \Delta + m^2) X_t\) \(\displaystyle =\) \(\displaystyle \xi_t, \hspace{8.4em} X (0) = X_0,\) (3.17)
\(\displaystyle (\partial_t - \Delta + m^2) Y^{\varepsilon, \bar{\varepsilon}}_t\) \(\displaystyle =\) \(\displaystyle -\mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon, \bar{\varepsilon}} (X_t, Y^{\varepsilon, \bar{\varepsilon}}_t), \qquad Y^{\varepsilon, \bar{\varepsilon}} (0) = Y_0 .\) (3.18)

It is easy to show that all the results in Proposition 3.8 hold also for equations (3.17)–(3.18), adapting the form of the equations for the derivatives. This implies that points i., ii. stated in Proposition 3.5 hold true also for \(G^{\lambda}_{\varepsilon, \bar{\varepsilon}}\). As far as point iii in Proposition 3.5 is concerned, we can get a slightly better result in the present scenario. Recall that \(\gamma_{\max}\) is defined as in Remark 1.1.

Notice that the operator (3.16) and equations (3.17)–(3.18) can be defined also for the case \(\bar{\varepsilon} = 0\). Moreover, (a suitable adaptation of) Proposition 3.8 holds also for the case \(\bar{\varepsilon} = 0\), and point iii. of Proposition 3.5 holds also for \(G^{\lambda}_{\varepsilon, 0}\).

Remark 3.10. It is worth to note that the operator \(\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon, \bar{\varepsilon}}\) cannot be use directly to solve Problem A”, since the solution to the resolvent equation depends in general on \((X, Y)\) and not only on \(X + Y\).

Proposition 3.11. Let \(F \in \operatorname{Cyl}_{B_X \times B_Y}\) and consider \(G_{\varepsilon, \bar{\varepsilon}}^{\lambda}\) given by (3.16). If \(\alpha^2 < 4 \pi \gamma_{\max}\), then there exists \(q > 1\) such that, for every \(\mu \in \mathcal{M}_{B_X \times B_Y}\), we have

\(\displaystyle \lim_{\bar{\varepsilon} \rightarrow 0} \int | \langle \nabla_{Y_0} G^{\lambda}_{\varepsilon, \bar{\varepsilon}}, \mathcal{G}-\mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon, \bar{\varepsilon}} \rangle | \hspace{1em} \mathrm{d} \mu \lesssim_{\lambda} \left( \int \| \mathcal{G}-\mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon, 0} \|_{B_{\exp}^{q, \ell / 2}}^q \hspace{1em} \mathrm{d} \mu \right)^{1 / q},\)

uniformly in \(\varepsilon > 0\).

In order to prove the previous result, we need some technical lemmas. First, we deal with the convergence as \(\bar{\varepsilon} \rightarrow 0\). In particular, whenever an object has \(\bar{\varepsilon}\) as one of its parameters (e.g. \(\nabla_{Y_0} G^{\lambda}_{\varepsilon, \bar{\varepsilon}}\)), the same notation with \(\bar{\varepsilon} = 0\) indicates that it is the limiting object as \(\bar{\varepsilon} \rightarrow 0\) (e.g. \(\nabla_{Y_0} G^{\lambda}_{\varepsilon, 0} = \lim_{\bar{\varepsilon} \rightarrow 0} \nabla_{Y_0} G^{\lambda}_{\varepsilon, \bar{\varepsilon}}\)), whenever it exists.

Lemma 3.12. For every \(\varepsilon > 0\), \(Y_0 \in B_Y^{\leqslant}\), and \(X_0\) in a set of full measure with respect to the free field measure \(\mu^{\operatorname{free}}\) with mass \(m > 0\), we have that \(\nabla_{Y_0} Y^{\varepsilon, \bar{\varepsilon}}_t (\mathcal{G}-\mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon, \bar{\varepsilon}})\) converges to \(\nabla_{Y_0} Y^{\varepsilon, 0}_t (\mathcal{G}-\mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon, 0})\) in \(B_{\exp}\), as \(\bar{\varepsilon} \rightarrow 0\).

Proof. Recall that \(\nabla_{Y_0} Y^{\varepsilon, \bar{\varepsilon}}_t (\mathcal{G}-\mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon, \bar{\varepsilon}})\) solves equation (3.8). This means that

\(\displaystyle \nabla \mathcal{Y}_t^{\varepsilon, \bar{\varepsilon}} = \nabla_{Y_0} Y^{\varepsilon, \bar{\varepsilon}}_t (\mathcal{G}-\mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon, \bar{\varepsilon}}) - P_t (\mathcal{G}-\mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon, \bar{\varepsilon}})\)

solves the equation

\(\displaystyle (\partial_t - \Delta + m^2) \nabla \mathcal{Y}_t^{\varepsilon, \bar{\varepsilon}}\) \(\displaystyle =\) \(\displaystyle - \mathrm{D}_Y \mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon, \bar{\varepsilon}} (X_t, Y_t^{\varepsilon, \bar{\varepsilon}}) [\nabla \mathcal{Y}_t^{\varepsilon, \bar{\varepsilon}}] - \mathrm{D}_Y \mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon, \bar{\varepsilon}} (X_t, Y_t^{\varepsilon, \bar{\varepsilon}}) [P_t (\mathcal{G}-\mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon, \bar{\varepsilon}})],\) (3.19)
\(\displaystyle \nabla \mathcal{Y}_0^{\varepsilon, \bar{\varepsilon}}\) \(\displaystyle =\) \(\displaystyle 0.\)

By Corollary C.3, we get the estimate

\(\displaystyle \| \nabla \mathcal{Y}_t^{\varepsilon, \bar{\varepsilon}} \| \lesssim \rho_{\ell''} (t) P_2 (\| f_{\varepsilon} : e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X_t)} : \|_{L^{\infty}}, \| \mathcal{G}-\mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon, \bar{\varepsilon}} \|_{B_{\exp}}) .\)

Such a bound is uniform with respect to \(\bar{\varepsilon} > 0\) and therefore we get a converging subsequence whose limit \(\nabla \mathcal{Y}_t^{\varepsilon, 0}\) solves equation (3.19) with \(\bar{\varepsilon} = 0\). Since the term \(P_t (\mathcal{G}-\mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon, \bar{\varepsilon}})\) converges point-wise to \(P_t (\mathcal{G}-\mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon, 0})\), the result is proved.\(\Box\)

Let us prove that it makes sense to consider some parameters satisfying certain conditions that will be useful in upcoming results.

Lemma 3.13. Suppose that \(\gamma = \alpha^2 / (4 \pi) < \gamma_{\max}\). Then there exist \(q > 1\) such that \(\gamma q < 2\), \(\kappa > 0\), and \(r\) and \(\delta\) as in Definition 2.6, such that the following inequalities are satisfied

\(\displaystyle - \gamma (r - 1) - \gamma (q - 1) - 2 \delta + \kappa > 0, \qquad \frac{1}{q} + \frac{1}{r} < 1, \qquad \frac{\kappa}{2} q < 1. \) (3.20)

Proof. In order to prove the result, it is enough to show that there exists some solution with the previous properties to the system of equations

\(\displaystyle - \gamma (r - 1) - \gamma \frac{1}{r - 1} + \frac{2 (r - 1)}{r} = 0, \qquad \frac{1}{q} + \frac{1}{r} = 1, \qquad \frac{\kappa}{2} q = 1.\)

From such relations we get the equality

\(\displaystyle \gamma = \frac{2 (r - 1)^2}{r ((r - 1)^2 + 1)} . \) (3.21)

We get the maximum value \(\gamma_{\max}\) of \(\gamma\) for some \(r = \bar{r} \approx 2.52\). Since, with this choices of parameters, we have \(q \approx 1.21\), the result is proved.\(\Box\)

In the proof of Lemma 3.12, we introduced the object

\(\displaystyle \nabla \mathcal{Y}_t^{\varepsilon, \bar{\varepsilon}} = \nabla_{Y_0} Y^{\varepsilon, \bar{\varepsilon}}_t (\mathcal{G}-\mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon, \bar{\varepsilon}}) - P_t (\mathcal{G}-\mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon, \bar{\varepsilon}}), \) (3.22)

satisfying equation (3.19) and admitting a limit \(\nabla \mathcal{Y}_t^{\varepsilon, 0}\) as \(\bar{\varepsilon} \rightarrow 0\).

Lemma 3.14. Suppose that \(\alpha^2 < 4 \pi \gamma_{\max}\), consider the parameters \(q\) and \(r\) defined as in Lemma 3.13, and \(\ell, \ell'''\) such that \(\ell q / 2 > 2\), \(\ell r / 2 > 2\) and \(\ell''' r > 1\). Then, for every \(\varepsilon > 0\), \(Y_0 \in B_Y^{\leqslant}\), and \(X_0\) in a set of full measure with respect to the free field measure \(\mu^{\operatorname{free}}\) with mass \(m > 0\), we have that

\(\displaystyle \| \nabla \mathcal{Y}^{\varepsilon, 0}_t \|_{L^1_{\ell} (\mathbb{R}^2)} \lesssim (1 + t)^{\sigma} \| f_{\varepsilon} : e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X_s)} : \|_{L^r_{\ell'''} (\mathbb{R}_+, B_{\exp}^{r, \ell / 2} (\mathbb{R}^2))} \| \mathcal{G}-\mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon, 0} \|_{B_{\exp}^{q, \ell / 2} (\mathbb{R}^2)} .\)

Proof. Let \(\gamma = \alpha^2 / (4 \pi)\). With the same notation as in Lemma 3.12, we consider the limit \(\nabla \mathcal{Y}^{\varepsilon, 0}_t\) and show that it converges to zero. Indeed, it satisfies

\(\displaystyle (\partial_t - \Delta_z + m^2 + \alpha^2 f_{\varepsilon} : e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X_t) (z)} : e^{\alpha Y_t (z)}) \nabla \mathcal{Y}^{\varepsilon, 0}_t = - \alpha^2 f_{\varepsilon} : e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X_t) (z)} : e^{\alpha Y_t (z)} P_t (\mathcal{G}-\mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon, 0}),\)

with \(\nabla \mathcal{Y}^{\varepsilon, 0}_0 = 0\).

We now want to exploit a similar argument as the one used in Theorem C.2 to get some estimates concerning the solution to the previous equation. Let \(w : \mathbb{R} \rightarrow \mathbb{R}\) be an increasing smooth function with bounded derivatives and such that \(w (0) = 0\), \(w (x) \rightarrow \pm 1\) as \(x \rightarrow \pm \infty\), and define \(W (x) = \int_0^x w (y) \mathrm{d} y\). Then, multiplying the equation by \(\rho_{\ell} (\theta \cdot) w (\tilde{\mathcal{K}}_t^{\varepsilon, 0})\), where \(\theta, \ell > 0\), and integrating, we have

\(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle \partial_t \| \rho_{\ell} (\theta \cdot) W (\nabla \mathcal{Y}^{\varepsilon, 0}_t) \|_{L^1} + m_{\theta}^2 \| \rho_{\ell} (\theta \cdot) w (\nabla \mathcal{Y}^{\varepsilon, 0}_t) \tilde{\mathcal{K}}_t^{\varepsilon, 0} \|_{L^1} + \| \rho_{\ell} (\theta \cdot) w' (\nabla \mathcal{Y}^{\varepsilon, 0}_t) (\nabla (\nabla \mathcal{Y}^{\varepsilon, 0}_t))^2 \|_{L^1}\)
\(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle + \int \alpha^2 \rho_{\ell} (\theta z) f_{\varepsilon} : e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X_t) (z)} : e^{\alpha Y_t (z)} w (\nabla \mathcal{Y}^{\varepsilon, 0}_t) \nabla \mathcal{Y}^{\varepsilon, 0}_t \mathrm{d} z\)
\(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle \lesssim\) \(\displaystyle - \int \alpha^2 \rho_{\ell / 2} (\theta z) f_{\varepsilon} : e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X_t) (z)} : e^{\alpha Y_t (z)} w (\nabla \mathcal{Y}^{\varepsilon, 0}_t) \rho_{\ell / 2} (\theta z) (P_t (\mathcal{G}-\mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon, 0})) \mathrm{d} z,\) (3.23)

where \(0 < m_{\theta} \leqslant \sqrt{m^2 - \left| \frac{\nabla \rho_{\ell} (\theta z)}{\rho_{\ell} (\theta z)} \right|}\) for every \(z \in \mathbb{R}^2\), which holds for \(\theta > 0\) small enough.

If we choose the parameters as in Lemma 3.13, we have

\begin{eqnarray*} \rho_{\ell / 2} (\theta \cdot) f_{\varepsilon} : e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X_t)} : e^{\alpha Y_t} & \in & L^r_{\ell'''} (\mathbb{R}_+, B_{\exp}^{r, 0}),\\ w (\nabla \mathcal{Y}^{\varepsilon, 0}_t) & \in & L^{\infty} (\mathbb{R}^2),\\ \rho_{\ell / 2} (\theta \cdot) (P_t (\mathcal{G}-\mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon, 0})) & \in & L^{\infty} (| \cdot |^{- \kappa / 2} \mathbb{R}_+, B^{- \gamma (q - 1) - \delta + \kappa}_{q, q} (\mathbb{R}^2)) . \end{eqnarray*}

Therefore, by integrating with respect to time equation (3.23) and recalling that \(\nabla \mathcal{Y}^{\varepsilon, 0}_0 = 0\), we get

\begin{eqnarray*} & & \| \rho_{\ell} (\theta \cdot) W (\nabla \mathcal{Y}^{\varepsilon, 0}_t) \|_{L^1} \lesssim\\ & & \int_0^t \| \rho_{\ell / 2} (\theta \cdot) f_{\varepsilon} : e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X_s)} : e^{\alpha Y_s} w (\nabla \mathcal{Y}^{\varepsilon, 0}_s) \|_{B_{\exp}^{r, 0}} \| \rho_{\ell / 2} (\theta \cdot) (e^{- (- \Delta + m^2) s} (\mathcal{G}-\mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon, 0})) \|_{B^{- \gamma (q - 1) - \delta + \kappa}_{q, q}} \mathrm{d} s \end{eqnarray*}

where we used the fact that \(\frac{1}{r} + \frac{1}{q} < 1\) and \(- \gamma (r - 1) - \gamma (q - 1) - 2 \delta + \kappa > 1\). By Proposition A.4, we have that

\(\displaystyle \| f_{\varepsilon} : e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X_s)} : e^{\alpha Y_s} w (\nabla \mathcal{Y}^{\varepsilon, 0}_s) \|_{B_{\exp}^{r, \ell / 2}} \lesssim \| f_{\varepsilon} : e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X_s)} : \|_{B_{\exp}^{r, \ell / 2}} \| e^{\alpha Y_s} \|_{L^{\infty}} \| w (\nabla \mathcal{Y}^{\varepsilon, 0}_s) \|_{L^{\infty}} .\)

Therefore, by Proposition A.6, we have

\(\displaystyle \| \rho_{\ell} (\theta \cdot) W (\nabla \mathcal{Y}^{\varepsilon, 0}_t) \|_{L^1} \lesssim \int_0^t \| f_{\varepsilon} : e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X_s)} : \|_{B_{\exp}^{r, \ell / 2}} s^{\kappa / 2} \| \mathcal{G}-\mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon, 0} \|_{B_{\exp}^{q, \ell / 2}} \mathrm{d} s.\)

Multiply and divide then by \(\rho_{\ell'''} (s)\) and apply Hölder inequality with respect to \(s\) to get

\begin{eqnarray*} & & \| \rho_{\ell} (\theta \cdot) W (\nabla \mathcal{Y}^{\varepsilon, 0}_t) \|_{L^1}\\ & \lesssim & \| \rho_{\ell / 2} (\theta \cdot) f_{\varepsilon} : e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X_s)} : \|_{L^r_{\ell'''} (\mathbb{R}_+, B_{\exp}^{r, \ell / 2})} \| \mathcal{G}-\mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon, 0} \|_{B_{\exp}^{q, \ell / 2}} \left( \int_0^t s^{q \kappa / 2} \rho_{- \ell''' q} (s) \mathrm{d} s \right)^{1 / q} . \end{eqnarray*}

Since the expression \(\left( \int_0^t s^{q \kappa / 2} \rho_{- \ell''' q} (s) \mathrm{d} s \right)^{1 / q}\) is bounded for \(t \rightarrow 0\) and grows polynomially in time as \(t \rightarrow + \infty\), we deduce that the result holds.\(\Box\)

Proof of Proposition 3.11. As in the proof of point i. of Proposition 3.5 we can get

\(\displaystyle \nabla_{Y_0} G^{\lambda}_{\varepsilon, \bar{\varepsilon}} (X_0, Y_0) =\mathbb{E}_{\xi} \left[ \int_0^{+ \infty} e^{- \lambda t} \mathrm{d} F (X_t, Y^{\varepsilon, \bar{\varepsilon}}_t) \nabla_{Y_0} Y_t^{\varepsilon, \bar{\varepsilon}} \mathrm{d} t \right] .\)

Therefore, by Lemma 3.12 and Lebesgue's dominated convergence theorem, we have

\begin{eqnarray*} \lim_{\bar{\varepsilon} \rightarrow 0} \int \nabla_{Y_0} G^{\lambda}_{\varepsilon, \bar{\varepsilon}} (X_0, Y_0) (\mathcal{G}-\mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon, \bar{\varepsilon}}) \mathrm{d} \mu & = & \lim_{\bar{\varepsilon} \rightarrow 0} \int \mathbb{E}_{\xi} \left[ \int_0^{+ \infty} e^{- \lambda t} \mathrm{d} F (X_t, Y^{\varepsilon, \bar{\varepsilon}}_t) \nabla_{Y_0} Y^{\varepsilon, \bar{\varepsilon}}_t (\mathcal{G}-\mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon, \bar{\varepsilon}}) \mathrm{d} t \right] \mathrm{d} \mu\\ & = & \int \mathbb{E}_{\xi} \left[ \int_0^{+ \infty} e^{- \lambda t} \mathrm{d} F (X_t, Y^{\varepsilon, 0}_t) \nabla_{Y_0} Y^{\varepsilon, 0}_t (\mathcal{G}-\mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon, 0}) \mathrm{d} t \right] \mathrm{d} \mu\\ & = & \int \nabla_{Y_0} G^{\lambda}_{\varepsilon, 0} (X_0, Y_0) (\mathcal{G}-\mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon, 0}) \mathrm{d} \mu . \end{eqnarray*}

Now, recalling the representation (3.22) of \(\nabla \mathcal{Y}^{\varepsilon, 0}_t\), we have

\begin{eqnarray*} & & \int \mathbb{E}_{\xi} \left[ \int_0^{+ \infty} e^{- \lambda t} \mathrm{d} F (X_t, Y^{\varepsilon, 0}_t) \nabla_{Y_0} Y^{\varepsilon, 0}_t (\mathcal{G}-\mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon, 0}) \mathrm{d} t \right] \mathrm{d} \mu\\ & \lesssim & \| \mathrm{d} F \|_{L^{\infty}} \int \mathbb{E}_{\xi} \left[ \int_0^{+ \infty} e^{- \lambda t} \| \nabla_{Y_0} Y^{\varepsilon, 0}_t (\mathcal{G}-\mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon, 0}) \|_{B_{\exp}^{q, \ell / 2}} \mathrm{d} t \right] \mathrm{d} \mu\\ & \lesssim & \int \mathbb{E}_{\xi} \left[ \int_0^{+ \infty} e^{- \lambda t} (\| P_t (\mathcal{G}-\mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon, 0}) \|_{B_{\exp}^{q, \ell / 2}} + \| \nabla \mathcal{Y}^{\varepsilon, 0}_t \|_{L^1_{\ell} (\mathbb{R}^2)}) \mathrm{d} t \right] \mathrm{d} \mu . \end{eqnarray*}

At this point, the proof follows the same steps as the one of point iv. in Proposition 3.5.\(\Box\)

4Existence of solutions via Lyapunov functions

In this section, we prove existence of solutions to Problem A” and B. Since existence for the Problem B implies existence for Problem A” (see point iii. in Theorem 2.5), we only prove the first. We actually prove a stronger statement than the one given in Problem B, namely that equation (2.5) holds for every \(\Phi \in \mathcal{F}\) and not only for \(\Phi \in \operatorname{Cyl}_{B_X \times B_Y}\). We exploit a strategy based on Lyapunov functions.

Let us introduce a finite-dimensional approximation to the operator \(\mathcal{L}\). Let \(M, N \in \mathbb{N}\), \(\varepsilon > 0\), and consider \(\mathbb{T}^2_M\) the two-dimensional torus of size \(M\) which we identify hereafter with the subset \((- M \pi, M \pi]^2 \subset \mathbb{R}^2\). Consider the Fejér operator \(Q_{N, M} : \mathcal{S}' (\mathbb{T}^2_M) \rightarrow C^{\infty} (\mathbb{T}^2_M)\) defined as, for \(F \in \mathcal{S}' (\mathbb{T}^2_M)\),

\(\displaystyle Q_{N, M} (F) =\operatorname{Fej}_{N, M} \ast F, \qquad \operatorname{Fej}_{N, M} (x) = \sum_{| j_1 |, | j_2 | \leqslant N - 1} \left( 1 - \frac{| j_1 |}{N} \right) \left( 1 - \frac{| j_2 |}{N} \right) e^{i j x / M}, \qquad x \in \mathbb{T}^2_M .\)

Let us stress that the operator \(Q_{N, M}\) is both a positive operator, i.e. \(\langle Q_{N, M} (F), F \rangle \geqslant 0\), and a positive preserving operator, namely, if \(F\) is a positive distribution, then \(Q_{N, M} (F)\) is a positive function. The latter property following from the positivity of the kernel \(\operatorname{Fej}_{N, M}\).

The new approximating operator \(\mathcal{L}_{N, M, \varepsilon}\) is then given by, for \(\Phi \in \mathcal{F}\), by

\begin{eqnarray*} \mathcal{L}_{N, M, \varepsilon} (\Phi) & = & \frac{1}{2} \operatorname{tr}_{L^2 (\mathbb{T}^2_M)} (P_{\mathbb{T}^2_M} (\operatorname{Per}_{\mathbb{T}^2_M} \nabla_X^2 \Phi) P_{\mathbb{T}^2_M}) - \langle (- \Delta + m^2) X, \nabla_X \Phi \rangle\\ & & - \langle (- \Delta + m^2) Y +\mathcal{G}_{N, M, \varepsilon} (X, Y), \nabla_Y \Phi \rangle, \end{eqnarray*}

where \(\mathcal{G}_{N, M, \varepsilon}\) is the following approximation for the non-linearity in equation (3.4),

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{G}_{N, M, \varepsilon} (X, Y) = \alpha Q_{N, M} (g_{\varepsilon} \ast (: e^{\alpha Q_{N, M} (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X)} : e^{\alpha Q_{N, M} (g_{\varepsilon} \ast Y)})) . \) (4.1)

Here, \(g_{\varepsilon}\) is defined as in Section 2.2, \(P_{\mathbb{T}^2_M}\) is the natural projection of \(L^2 (\mathbb{R}^2)\) on the space \(L^2 (\mathbb{T}^2_M)\), and \(\operatorname{Per}_{\mathbb{T}^2_M}\) is the periodicization on the torus \(\mathbb{T}^2_M\). Moreover, the term \(: \exp (\alpha Q_{N, M} (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X)) :\) is defined as follows

\(\displaystyle : e^{\alpha Q_{N, M} (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X)} : = e^{\alpha Q_{N, M} (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X)} - \frac{\alpha^2}{2} c_{N, M, \varepsilon}, \) (4.2)

where \(c_{N, M, \varepsilon} = \int_{\mathbb{T}^2_M} Q_{N, M} (g_{\varepsilon}) (z) (- \Delta + m^2)^{- 1} (Q_{N, M} g_{\varepsilon}) (z) \mathrm{d} z\), where the inverse \((- \Delta + m^2)^{- 1}\) is taken with periodic boundary conditions on \(\mathbb{T}^2_M\).

The system of equations for the flow is then given by

\(\displaystyle (\partial_t - \Delta + m^2) X^M_t\) \(\displaystyle =\) \(\displaystyle \xi^M_t,\) (4.3)
\(\displaystyle (\partial_t - \Delta + m^2) Y^{N, M, \varepsilon}_t\) \(\displaystyle =\) \(\displaystyle -\mathcal{G}_{N, M, \varepsilon} (X_t^M, Y_t^{N, M, \varepsilon}),\) (4.4)

where \(Y^{N, M, \varepsilon}\) is negative, periodic, and it belongs to a subspace of \(\mathfrak{H}_{N, M} := \operatorname{span} \{ e^{i n \cdot / M} ; | n | \leqslant N \}\). As usual, we drop the dependence on the parameters \(N, M, \varepsilon\) when no ambiguity occurs.

4.1Lyapunov functions

We introduce some Lyapunov functions \(V_1\), \(V_2\), and \(V_3\) that will be crucial in the proof of the estimates for \(X\) and \(Y\). In particular, we take them such that \(V_1\) and \(V_2\) depend on both \(X\) and \(Y\), while \(V_3\) depends on \(X\) only.

Lemma 4.1. Consider two Banach spaces \(B_1\) and \(B_2\). Let \(\tilde{\mathcal{L}}\) be an operator taking values in some space of functions \(\mathcal{F}\) and consider a measure \(\tilde{\mu}\) such that \(\int \tilde{\mathcal{L}} \Phi \hspace{1em} \mathrm{d} \tilde{\mu} = 0\) for every \(\Phi \in \mathcal{F}\). Suppose that there exist some functions \(V_1, V_2 : B_1 \times B_2 \rightarrow \mathbb{R}\), and \(V_3 : B_1 \rightarrow \mathbb{R}\), such that we have \(V_1, V_2, V_3 \in \mathcal{F}\) and

  1. \(V_2\) and \(V_3\) are positive,

  2. The inequality

    \(\displaystyle \tilde{\mathcal{L}} V_1 (X, Y) \leqslant - V_2 (X, Y) + V_3 (X)\)

    holds true.

Then, we have

\(\displaystyle \int V_2 (X, Y) \tilde{\mu} (\mathrm{d} X, \mathrm{d} Y) \leqslant \int V_3 (X) \tilde{\mu} (\mathrm{d} X, \mathrm{d} Y) . \) (4.5)

Proof. The statement follows from the fact that \(\int \tilde{\mathcal{L}} V_1 (X, Y) \tilde{\mu} (\mathrm{d} X, \mathrm{d} Y) = 0\).\(\Box\)

We now work on the estimate for \(X\). We will have to choose the aforementioned Lyapunov functions, we start with \(V_1 : B_X \rightarrow \mathbb{R}\), that we take to be, for \(s' > 0\),

\(\displaystyle V_1 (X) = \| X \|^p_{B^{- s'}_{p, p, \ell}} .\)

Note that, since \(X\) corresponds to the free field, the previous norm is finite. Notice also that the previous function is not cylindric. Moreover, if we consider the representation of Besov spaces given in Proposition A.9, then we have, for \(- p s' / 2 + 1 < 0\),

\(\displaystyle \| X \|^p_{B^{- s'}_{p, p, \ell}} \simeq \| \varphi_0 (\mathrm{D}) X \|^p_{L_{\ell}^p} + \int_0^{+ \infty} \int_{\mathbb{R}^2} \frac{| \rho^k_{\ell} |^p}{t^{- p s' / 2 + 1}} | P_t X (z) |^p \mathrm{d} z \mathrm{d} t =: V_{1, 1} (X) + V_{1, 2} (X),\)

where \(k\) is to be determined, similarly as in the proof of Proposition 2.10.

Proposition 4.2. Let \(s' > 0\), \(p > 1\) and \(\ell > 0\) such that \(p \ell > 2\) and \(- p s' / 2 + 1 < 0\). Consider \(X\) to be the solution to equation (4.3) on the torus \(\mathbb{T}^2_M\). For some \(\delta \in (0, 1)\), we have the inequality

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{L}_{N, M, \varepsilon} V_1 (X) =\mathcal{L}_{N, M, \varepsilon} (\| X \|^p_{B^{- s'}_{p, p, \ell}}) \lesssim - (1 - \delta) \| X \|^p_{B^{- s' + 2 / p}_{p, p, \ell}} + \frac{1}{\delta} C. \) (4.6)

Proof. We want to evaluate \(\mathcal{L}_{N, M, \varepsilon} V_1 (X)\). The gradient of \(V_1\) is given by

\begin{eqnarray*} \nabla V_1 (X) (h) & = & \nabla \| \varphi_0 (\mathrm{D}) X \|^p_{L_{\ell}^p} (h) + \int_0^{+ \infty} \int_{\mathbb{R}^2} \frac{| \rho^k_{\ell} |^p}{t^{- p s' / 2 + 1}} \nabla (| P_t X (z) |^p) (h) \mathrm{d} z \mathrm{d} t\\ & = & p \int_{\mathbb{R}^2} | \rho^k_{\ell} |^p | \varphi_0 (\mathrm{D}) X (z) |^{p - 1} \operatorname{sign} (\varphi_0 (\mathrm{D}) X) \varphi_0 (\mathrm{D}) h \mathrm{d} z\\ & & + p \int_0^{+ \infty} \int_{\mathbb{R}^2} \frac{| \rho^k_{\ell} |^p}{t^{- p s' / 2 + 1}} | P_t X (z) |^{p - 1} \operatorname{sign} (P_t X (z)) P_t h \mathrm{d} z \mathrm{d} t, \end{eqnarray*}

while its second derivative is

\begin{eqnarray*} & & \nabla^2 V_1 (X) (h, h')\\ & = & p \int_{\mathbb{R}^2} | \rho^k_{\ell} |^p \nabla (| \varphi_0 (\mathrm{D}) X (z) |^{p - 1} \operatorname{sign} (\varphi_0 (\mathrm{D}) X) \varphi_0 (\mathrm{D}) h) (h') \mathrm{d} z\\ & & + p \int_0^{+ \infty} \int_{\mathbb{R}^2} \frac{| \rho^k_{\ell} |^p}{t^{- p s' / 2 + 1}} \nabla (| P_t X (z) |^{p - 1} \operatorname{sign}(P_t X (z)) P_t h) (h') \mathrm{d} z \mathrm{d} t\\ & = & p (p - 1) \int_{\mathbb{R}^2} | \rho^k_{\ell} |^p | \varphi_0 (\mathrm{D}) X (z) |^{p - 2} (\varphi_0 (\mathrm{D}) h) (\varphi_0 (\mathrm{D}) h') \mathrm{d} z\\ & & + p (p - 1) \int_0^{+ \infty} \int_{\mathbb{R}^2} \frac{| \rho^k_{\ell} |^p}{t^{- p s' / 2 + 1}} | P_t X (z) |^{p - 2} (P_t h) (P_t h') \mathrm{d} z \mathrm{d} t. \end{eqnarray*}

The first term to deal with is \(\langle (- \Delta + m^2) X, \nabla_X V_1 (X) \rangle\). We have

\begin{eqnarray*} - \langle (- \Delta + m^2) X, \nabla_X V_{1, 1} (X) \rangle & = & - p \int_{\mathbb{R}^2} | \rho^k_{\ell} |^p | \varphi_0 (\mathrm{D}) X (z) |^{p - 1} \operatorname{sign} (\varphi_0 (\mathrm{D}) X) (\varphi_0 (\mathrm{D}) (- \Delta + m^2) X) \mathrm{d} z\\ & = & - p \int_{\mathbb{R}^2} \nabla (| \rho^k_{\ell} |^p | \varphi_0 (\mathrm{D}) X (z) |^{p - 1} \operatorname{sign} (\varphi_0 (\mathrm{D}) X)) \nabla (\varphi_0 (\mathrm{D}) X) \mathrm{d} z\\ & & - p m^2 \int_{\mathbb{R}^2} | \rho^k_{\ell} |^p | \varphi_0 (\mathrm{D}) X (z) |^p \mathrm{d} z\\ & = & - p (p - 1) \int_{\mathbb{R}^2} | \rho^k_{\ell} |^p | \varphi_0 (\mathrm{D}) X (z) |^{p - 2} | \nabla (\varphi_0 (\mathrm{D}) X) |^2 \mathrm{d} z\\ & & - p^2 \int_{\mathbb{R}^2} | \rho^k_{\ell} |^{p - 1} | \varphi_0 (\mathrm{D}) X (z) |^{p - 1} (\nabla \rho^k_{\ell}) \operatorname{sign} (\varphi_0 (\mathrm{D}) X) \nabla (\varphi_0 (\mathrm{D}) X) \mathrm{d} z\\ & & - p m^2 \int_{\mathbb{R}^2} | \rho^k_{\ell} |^p | \varphi_0 (\mathrm{D}) X (z) |^p \mathrm{d} z. \end{eqnarray*}

Rearranging the second term on the right-hand side and exploiting Young's inequality, we have, for all \(\sigma > 0\),

\begin{eqnarray*} - \langle (- \Delta + m^2) X, \nabla_X V_{1, 1} (X) \rangle & \lesssim & - p (p - 1) \int_{\mathbb{R}^2} | \rho^k_{\ell} |^p | \varphi_0 (\mathrm{D}) X (z) |^{p - 2} | \nabla (\varphi_0 (\mathrm{D}) X) |^2 \mathrm{d} z\\ & & + p^2 \int_{\mathbb{R}^2} | \rho^k_{\ell} |^p | \varphi_0 (\mathrm{D}) X (z) |^{p / 2} \frac{| \nabla \rho^k_{\ell} |}{\rho^k_{\ell}} | \varphi_0 (\mathrm{D}) X |^{(p - 2) / 2} | \nabla (\varphi_0 (\mathrm{D}) X) | \mathrm{d} z\\ & & - p m^2 \int_{\mathbb{R}^2} | \rho^k_{\ell} |^p | \varphi_0 (\mathrm{D}) X (z) |^p \mathrm{d} z\\ & \lesssim & - p (p - 1) \int_{\mathbb{R}^2} | \rho^k_{\ell} |^p | \varphi_0 (\mathrm{D}) X (z) |^{p - 2} | \nabla (\varphi_0 (\mathrm{D}) X) |^2 \mathrm{d} z\\ & & + \frac{p^2}{4 \sigma} \int_{\mathbb{R}^2} | \rho^k_{\ell} |^p | \varphi_0 (\mathrm{D}) X (z) |^p \frac{| \nabla \rho^k_{\ell} |^2}{\rho^k_{2 \ell}} \mathrm{d} z\\ & & + p^2 \sigma \int_{\mathbb{R}^2} | \rho^k_{\ell} |^p | \varphi_0 (\mathrm{D}) X |^{p - 2} | \nabla (\varphi_0 (\mathrm{D}) X) |^2 \mathrm{d} z\\ & & - p m^2 \int_{\mathbb{R}^2} | \rho^k_{\ell} |^p | \varphi_0 (\mathrm{D}) X (z) |^p \mathrm{d} z, \end{eqnarray*}

from which, choosing \(\sigma > 0\) small enough and \(k > 0\) large enough, and appropriately rearranging the terms, we get

\(\displaystyle - \langle (- \Delta + m^2) X, \nabla_X V_{1, 1} (X) \rangle \lesssim - \| \varphi_0 (\mathrm{D}) X \|_{L^p_{\ell}}^p .\)

We also have

\begin{eqnarray*} & & - \langle (- \Delta + m^2) X, \nabla_X V_{1, 2} (X) \rangle\\ & = & - p \int_0^{+ \infty} \int_{\mathbb{R}^2} \frac{| \rho_{\ell} |^p}{t^{- p s' / 2 + 1}} | P_t X (z) |^{p - 1} \operatorname{sign} (P_t X (z)) (P_t (- \Delta + m^2) X (z)) \mathrm{d} z \mathrm{d} t\\ & = & \int_0^{+ \infty} \int_{\mathbb{R}^2} \frac{| \rho_{\ell} |^p}{t^{- p s' / 2 + 1}} \partial_t | P_t X (z) |^p \mathrm{d} z \mathrm{d} t\\ & = & - \int_0^{+ \infty} \int_{\mathbb{R}^2} \partial_t \left( \frac{| \rho_{\ell} |^p}{t^{- p s' / 2 + 1}} \right) | P_t X (z) |^p \mathrm{d} z \mathrm{d} t\\ & = & - \int_0^{+ \infty} \int_{\mathbb{R}^2} \left( \frac{p s'}{2} - 1 \right) \frac{| \rho_{\ell} |^p}{t^{- p s' / 2 + 2}} | P_t X (z) |^p \mathrm{d} z \mathrm{d} t\\ & = & - \int_0^{+ \infty} \int_{\mathbb{R}^2} \left( \frac{p s'}{2} - 1 \right) \frac{| \rho_{\ell} |^p}{t^{p (- s' + 2 / p) / 2 + 1}} | P_t X (z) |^p \mathrm{d} z \mathrm{d} t\\ & \simeq & - (\| X \|^p_{B^{- s' + 2 / p}_{p, p, \ell}} - \| \varphi_0 (\mathrm{D}) X \|^p_{L_{\ell}^p}) . \end{eqnarray*}

The second term to deal with is the trace of the second derivative, i.e. \(\operatorname{tr}_{L^2 (\mathbb{T}^2_M)} (\nabla^2 V_1 (X))\). By the results presented in Section XI.V of [RS79], it suffices to consider the second derivative with \(h' = h\), i.e. \(\nabla^2 V_1 (X) (h, h)\), and integrate with respect to \(h\). Then, exploiting Young inequality for products and using the fact that \(p \ell > 2\), we have for any \(\sigma > 0\)

\begin{eqnarray*} \operatorname{tr} (\nabla^2 V_{1, 1} (X)) & = & p (p - 1) \int_{\mathbb{T}^2_M} \int_{\mathbb{R}^2} | \rho^k_{\ell} |^p | \varphi_0 (\mathrm{D}) X (z) |^{p - 2} | \hat{\varphi}_0 (z - y) |^2 \mathrm{d} z \mathrm{d} y\\ & = & p (p - 1) \int_{\mathbb{R}^2} | \rho^k_{\ell} |^p | \varphi_0 (\mathrm{D}) X (z) |^{p - 2} \int_{\mathbb{T}^2_M} | \hat{\varphi}_0 (z - y) |^2 \mathrm{d} y \mathrm{d} z\\ & \lesssim & p (p - 1) \int_{\mathbb{R}^2} | \rho^k_{\ell} |^p | \varphi_0 (\mathrm{D}) X (z) |^{p - 2} \mathrm{d} z\\ & \lesssim & \frac{1}{\sigma} p (p - 1) \int_{\mathbb{R}^2} | \rho^k_{\ell} |^p \mathrm{d} z + \sigma p (p - 1) \int_{\mathbb{R}^2} | \rho^k_{\ell} |^p | \varphi_0 (\mathrm{D}) X (z) |^p \mathrm{d} z\\ & \lesssim & \frac{1}{\sigma} C + \sigma \| \varphi_0 (\mathrm{D}) X \|_{L^p_{\ell}}^p . \end{eqnarray*}

We get, if \(\mathcal{P}^M_t\) is the heat kernel on the torus \(\mathbb{T}^2_M\), that, for any \(\sigma > 0\),

\begin{eqnarray*} \operatorname{tr} (\nabla^2 V_{1, 2} (X)) & = & p (p - 1) \int_{\mathbb{T}^2_M} \int_0^{+ \infty} \int_{\mathbb{R}^2} \frac{| \rho_{\ell} |^p}{t^{- p s' / 2 + 1}} | P_t X (z) |^{p - 2} | \mathcal{P}^M_t (z - y) |^2 \mathrm{d} z \mathrm{d} t \mathrm{d} y\\ & = & p (p - 1) \int_0^{+ \infty} \int_{\mathbb{R}^2} \frac{| \rho_{\ell} |^p}{t^{- p s' / 2 + 1}} | P_t X (z) |^{p - 2} \left( \int_{\mathbb{T}^2_M} | \mathcal{P}^M_t (z - y) |^2 \mathrm{d} y \right) \mathrm{d} z \mathrm{d} t\\ & = & p (p - 1) \int_0^{+ \infty} \int_{\mathbb{R}^2} \frac{| \rho_{\ell} |^p}{t^{- p s' / 2 + 1}} | P_t X (z) |^{p - 2} \frac{C e^{- m^2 t}}{t} \mathrm{d} z \mathrm{d} t\\ & = & p (p - 1) \int_0^{+ \infty} \int_{\mathbb{R}^2} \frac{C e^{- m^2 t} | \rho_{\ell} |^p}{t^{- p s' / 2 + 2}} | P_t X (z) |^{p - 2} \mathrm{d} z \mathrm{d} t\\ & \lesssim & \frac{1}{\sigma} C' \int_0^{+ \infty} \int_{\mathbb{R}^2} \frac{| \rho_{\ell} |^p e^{- q (p) m^2 t}}{t^{- p s' / 2 + 2}} \mathrm{d} z \mathrm{d} t + \sigma \int_0^{+ \infty} \int_{\mathbb{R}^2} \frac{| \rho_{\ell} |^p}{t^{- p s' / 2 + 2}} | P_t X (z) |^p \mathrm{d} z \mathrm{d} t\\ & \lesssim & \frac{1}{\sigma} C + \sigma (\| X \|^p_{B^{- s' + 2 / p}_{p, p, \ell}} - \| \varphi_0 (\mathrm{D}) X \|^p_{L_{\ell}^p}), \end{eqnarray*}

where we used Young inequality as above.

Choosing \(\sigma > 0\) small enough, we get

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{L}V_1 (X) \lesssim - (1 - \sigma) \| X \|^p_{B^{- s' + 2 / p}_{p, p, \ell}} + \frac{1}{\sigma} C,\)

which gives the result.\(\Box\)

Remark 4.3. The result in Lemma 4.1 with \(B_2 = B_X\), no dependence on \(B_1\) whatsoever, and with Lyapunov functions chosen as

\begin{eqnarray*} V_1 (X) & = & \| X \|^p_{B^{- s'}_{p, p, \ell}},\\ V_2 (X) & = & (1 - \sigma) \| X \|^p_{B^{- s' + 2 / p}_{p, p, \ell}},\\ V_3 & \equiv & \frac{1}{\sigma} C, \end{eqnarray*}

for some constant \(C > 0\), gives the estimate

\(\displaystyle (1 - \sigma) \int \| X \|^p_{B^{- s' + 2 / p}_{p, p, \ell}} \mu_{N, M, \varepsilon} (\mathrm{d} X, \mathrm{d} Y) \lesssim \frac{1}{\sigma} C.\)

Notice further that the bound (4.6) can be chosen to be uniform with respect to the size \(M\) of the torus \(\mathbb{T}^2_M\) since the integrals \(\int_{\mathbb{T}^2_M} | \hat{\varphi}_0 (z - y) |^2 \mathrm{d} y\) and \(\int_{\mathbb{T}^2_M} | | \mathcal{P}^M_t (z - y) | (z - y) |^2 \mathrm{d} y\) are uniformly bounded in \(M\). It is worth to note also that by the Besov embedding (Proposition A.2) the space \(B^{- s' + 2 / p}_{p, p, \ell} (\mathbb{R}^2)\) is embedded in \(B_X = C^{- \delta}_{\ell} (\mathbb{R}^2)\).

Let us choose the Lyapunov functions in order to get an estimate for \(Y\). We may need \(X \mapsto V_1 (X, \cdot)\) to be \(C^1\) and \(Y \mapsto V_1 (\cdot, Y)\) to be \(C^2\).

Let \(s > 0\). Referring to the representation of Besov spaces given in Proposition A.9 and Remark A.10, take \((k - s / 2) p > 1\), and consider

\(\displaystyle V_1 (Y) = \| Y \|_{B^s_{p, p, \ell}}^p \simeq \| Y \|_{L^p_{\ell}}^p + \int_0^{+ \infty} \int_{\mathbb{R}^2} \frac{t^{k p} | \rho_{\ell} |^p}{t^{p s / 2 + 1}} | \partial_{t^k} P_t Y (z) |^p \mathrm{d} z \mathrm{d} t =: V_{1, 1} (Y) + V_{1, 2} (Y) . \) (4.7)

Theorem 4.4. Suppose that \(Y\) is a solution to equation (4.4). Let \(\gamma = \alpha^2 / (4 \pi) < 2\), and \(1 < p, q < + \infty\). Take \(s\) and \(r\) such that

\(\displaystyle 0 < s < \gamma + 2 - \sqrt{8 \gamma}, \quad \tfrac{2 + \gamma - s - \sqrt{(s - \gamma - 2)^2 - 8 \gamma}}{2 \gamma} < r < \tfrac{2 + \gamma - s + \sqrt{(s - \gamma - 2)^2 - 8 \gamma}}{2 \gamma}, \quad \gamma r < 2. \) (4.8)

If \(: e^{\alpha X} : \in B_{\exp}^{r, \ell} = B^{- \gamma (r - 1) - \delta}_{r, r, \ell} (\mathbb{R}^2)\), for \(\ell\) large enough, then we have, for any \(\sigma > 0\),

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{L}_{N, M, \varepsilon} V_1 (Y) =\mathcal{L}_{N, M, \varepsilon} (\| Y \|^p_{B^s_{p, p, \ell}}) \lesssim - (1 - \sigma) \| Y \|_{B^{s + 2 / p}_{p, p, \ell}}^p + \frac{1}{\sigma} \| \mathcal{G}_{N, M, \varepsilon} (X, Y) \|^{(p r - r + 1) / (p r^2)}_{B_{\exp}^{r, \ell}} . \) (4.9)

Proof. In the following proof, we neglect the term \(V_{1, 1} (Y)\) appearing in (4.7), which can be dealt with in a similar way as in the proof of Proposition 4.2. The gradient of \(V_1\) is given by

\begin{eqnarray*} \nabla V_{1, 2} (Y) (h) & = & \int_0^{+ \infty} \int_{\mathbb{R}^2} \frac{t^{p k} | \rho_{\ell} |^p}{t^{p s / 2 + 1}} \nabla (| \partial_{t^k} P_t Y (z) |^p) (h) \mathrm{d} z \mathrm{d} t\\ & = & \int_0^{+ \infty} \int_{\mathbb{R}^2} \frac{t^{p k} | \rho_{\ell} |^p}{t^{p s / 2 + 1}} p | \partial_{t^k} P_t Y (z) |^{p - 1} \partial_{t^k} P_t h \mathrm{d} z \mathrm{d} t. \end{eqnarray*}

Therefore, taking \(h = - (- \Delta + m^2) Y -\mathcal{G}_{N, M, \varepsilon} (X, Y)\), we have

\begin{eqnarray*} & & - \langle (- \Delta + m^2) Y +\mathcal{G}_{N, M, \varepsilon} (X, Y), \nabla V_{1, 2} (Y) \rangle\\ & = & \int_0^{+ \infty} \int_{\mathbb{R}^2} \frac{t^{p k} | \rho_{\ell} |^p}{t^{p s / 2 + 1}} p | \partial_{t^k} P_t Y (z) |^{p - 1} \partial_{t^k} P_t (- (- \Delta + m^2) Y -\mathcal{G}_{N, M, \varepsilon} (X, Y)) \mathrm{d} z \mathrm{d} t\\ & = & \int_0^{+ \infty} \int_{\mathbb{R}^2} \frac{t^{p k} | \rho_{\ell} |^p}{t^{p s / 2 + 1}} \partial_t (| \partial_{t^k} P_t Y |^p) \mathrm{d} z \mathrm{d} t\\ & & + \int_0^{+ \infty} \int_{\mathbb{R}^2} \frac{t^{p k} | \rho_{\ell} |^p}{t^{p s / 2 + 1}} p | \partial_{t^k} P_t Y (z) |^{p - 1} \partial_{t^k} P_t (-\mathcal{G}_{N, M, \varepsilon} (X, Y)) \mathrm{d} z \mathrm{d} t\\ & =: & I_1 + I_2 . \end{eqnarray*}

Let us focus on \(I_1\). Integrating by parts, we get

\begin{eqnarray*} I_1 & = & - \int_0^{+ \infty} \int_{\mathbb{R}^2} \partial_t \left( \frac{t^{p k} | \rho_{\ell} |^p}{t^{p s / 2 + 1}} \right) | \partial_{t^k} P_t Y |^p \mathrm{d} z \mathrm{d} t\\ & = & - \int_0^{+ \infty} \int_{\mathbb{R}^2} \left( p k - \frac{p s}{2} - 1 \right) \frac{t^{p k} | \rho_{\ell} |^p}{t^{p s / 2 + 2}} | \partial_{t^k} P_t Y |^p \mathrm{d} z \mathrm{d} t\\ & = & - \int_0^{+ \infty} \int_{\mathbb{R}^2} \left( p k - \frac{p s}{2} - 1 \right) \frac{t^{p k} | \rho_{\ell} |^p}{t^{p (s + 2 / p) / 2 + 1}} | \partial_{t^k} P_t Y |^p \mathrm{d} z \mathrm{d} t\\ & \simeq & - (\| Y \|_{B^{s + 2 / p}_{p, p, \ell}}^p - \| Y \|^p_{L^p_{\ell}}) . \end{eqnarray*}

Consider now \(I_2\). Exploiting Hölder inequality with \(q_1, p_1\) and introducing \(s' > 0\) yields

\begin{eqnarray*} I_2 & = & \int_0^{+ \infty} \int_{\mathbb{R}^2} \frac{t^{p k} | \rho_{\ell} |^p}{t^{p s / 2 + 1}} p | \partial_{t^k} P_t Y (z) |^{p - 1} \partial_{t^k} P_t (-\mathcal{G}_{N, M, \varepsilon} (X, Y)) \mathrm{d} z \mathrm{d} t\\ & = & \int_0^{+ \infty} \frac{1}{t} \int_{\mathbb{R}^2} \left[ \frac{t^{(p - 1) k} | \rho_{\ell} |^{p - 1}}{t^{p s / 2 + s' / 2}} p | \partial_{t^k} P_t Y (z) |^{p - 1} \right] \\ & & \times [t^{k + s' / 2} | \rho_{\ell} | \partial_{t^k} P_t (-\mathcal{G}_{N, M, \varepsilon} (X, Y))] \mathrm{d} z \mathrm{d} t\\ & \leqslant & \left( \int_0^{+ \infty} \int_{\mathbb{R}^2} \left| \frac{t^{(p - 1) k} | \rho_{\ell} |^{p - 1}}{t^{p s / 2 + s' / 2}} p | \partial_{t^k} P_t Y (z) |^{p - 1} \right|^{p_1} \mathrm{d} z \frac{\mathrm{d} t}{t} \right)^{1 / p_1}\\ & & \times \left( \int_0^{+ \infty} \int_{\mathbb{R}^2} | t^{s' / 2} (t^k | \rho_{\ell} |) \partial_{t^k} P_t (-\mathcal{G}_{N, M, \varepsilon} (X, Y)) |^{q_1} \mathrm{d} z \frac{\mathrm{d} t}{t} \right)^{1 / q_1} . \end{eqnarray*}

If we apply Young inequality, we then have, for any \(\sigma > 0\),

\begin{eqnarray*} I_2 & \leqslant & \sigma \left( \int_0^{+ \infty} \int_{\mathbb{R}^2} \left| \frac{t^{(p - 1) k} | \rho_{\ell} |^{p - 1}}{t^{p s / 2 + s' / 2}} p | \partial_{t^k} P_t Y (z) |^{p - 1} \right|^{p_1} \mathrm{d} z \frac{\mathrm{d} t}{t} \right)^{p_2 / p_1}\\ & & + \frac{1}{\sigma} \left( \int_0^{+ \infty} \int_{\mathbb{R}^2} | t^{s' / 2} (t^k | \rho_{\ell} |) \partial_{t^k} P_t (-\mathcal{G}_{N, M, \varepsilon} (X, Y)) |^{q_1} \mathrm{d} z \frac{\mathrm{d} t}{t} \right)^{q'' / q_1}\\ & = & \sigma \left( \int_0^{+ \infty} \int_{\mathbb{R}^2} \left( \frac{t^{(p - 1) k} | \rho_{\ell} |^{p - 1}}{t^{p s / 2 + s' / 2}} \right)^{p_1} p^{p_1} | \partial_{t^k} P_t Y (z) |^{p_1 p - p_1} \mathrm{d} z \frac{\mathrm{d} t}{t} \right)^{p_2 / p_1}\\ & & + \frac{1}{\sigma} \left( \int_0^{+ \infty} \int_{\mathbb{R}^2} | t^{s' / 2} (t^k | \rho_{\ell} |) \partial_{t^k} P_t (-\mathcal{G}_{N, M, \varepsilon} (X, Y)) |^{q_1} \mathrm{d} z \frac{\mathrm{d} t}{t} \right)^{q'' / q_1}\\ & = & \sigma \left( \int_0^{+ \infty} \int_{\mathbb{R}^2} \frac{t^{p_1 (p - 1) k}}{t^{p s p_1 / 2 + s' p_1 / 2}} \left( \frac{t^k}{t^{(p s + s') / (2 (p - 1))}} \right)^{p_1 (p - 1)} p^{p_1} \right.\\ & & \times \left. | \rho_{\ell} |^{p_1 (p - 1)} | \partial_{t^k} P_t Y (z) |^{p_1 (p - 1)} \mathrm{d} z \frac{\mathrm{d} t}{t} \right)^{p_2 / p_1}\\ & & + \frac{1}{\sigma} \left( \int_0^{+ \infty} \int_{\mathbb{R}^2} | t^{k + s' / 2} | \rho_{\ell} | \partial_{t^k} P_t (-\mathcal{G}_{N, M, \varepsilon} (X, Y)) |^{q_1} \mathrm{d} z \frac{\mathrm{d} t}{t} \right)^{q_2 / q_1}\\ & = & \sigma \| Y \|_{B^{(p s + s') / (p - 1)}_{p_1 (p - 1), p_1 (p - 1), \ell}}^{p_2 / p_1} + \frac{1}{\sigma} \left( \int_0^{+ \infty} \int_{\mathbb{R}^2} | t^{s' / 2} (t^k | \rho_{\ell} |) \partial_{t^k} P_t (-\mathcal{G}_{N, M, \varepsilon} (X, Y)) |^{q_1} \mathrm{d} z \frac{\mathrm{d} t}{t} \right)^{q_2 / q_1}\\ & =: & I_{2, 1} + I_{2, 2} . \end{eqnarray*}

We want to apply Besov embedding (see Proposition A.2) in order to reabsorb the term \(I_{2, 1}\) in \(I_1\): considering the parameters of the involved norms, we get the condition

\(\displaystyle s > \frac{p s + s'}{p - 1} - \frac{2}{p_1 (p - 1)}, \) (4.10)

so that the reabsorbing procedure follows by a suitable choice of \(p_2\).

On the other hand, we have by Proposition A.4

\begin{eqnarray*} I_{2, 2} & \lesssim & \frac{1}{\sigma} \| \mathcal{G}_{N, M, \varepsilon} (X, Y) \|_{B_{q_1, q_1, \ell}^{- s'}}^{q_2 / q_1} . \end{eqnarray*}

All in all, we have the inequality

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{L}_{N, M, \varepsilon} V_1 (Y) =\mathcal{L}_{N, M, \varepsilon} \| Y \|_{B^s_{p, p, \ell}}^p \lesssim - (1 - \sigma) \| Y \|_{B^{s + 2 / p}_{p, p, \ell}}^p + \frac{1}{\sigma} \| \mathcal{G}_{N, M, \varepsilon} (X, Y) \|_{B_{q_1, q_1, \ell}^{- s'}}^{q_2 / q_1},\)

provided condition (4.10) holds true and \(p_2\) is chosen in an appropriate way.

Now, in order to obtain (4.9), we consider \(q_1 = r\) and \(s' > \gamma (r - 1)\), and have to check the condition

\(\displaystyle \gamma r^2 + r (s - \gamma - 2) + 2 < 0,\)

which is satisfied with the choice of parameters given in (4.8).\(\Box\)

Remark 4.5. If \(Y \leqslant 0\), we have the bound

\(\displaystyle \| \mathcal{G}_{N, M, \varepsilon} (X, Y) \|^{(p r - r + 1) / (p r^2)}_{B_{\exp}^{r, \ell}} \leqslant \| \mathcal{G}_{N, M, \varepsilon} (X, 0) \|^{(p r - r + 1) / (p r^2)}_{B_{\exp}^{r, \ell}},\)

on the right-hand side of inequality (4.9). In that case, choosing the Lyapunov functions as follows

\begin{eqnarray*} V_1 (Y) & = & \| Y \|_{B^s_{p, p, \ell}}^p,\\ V_2 (Y) & = & C_{\sigma} \| Y \|_{B^{s + 2 / p}_{p, p, \ell}}^p,\\ V_3 (X) & = & \frac{1}{\sigma} \| \mathcal{G}_{N, M, \varepsilon} (X, 0) \|^{(p r - r + 1) / (p r^2)}_{B_{\exp}^{r, \ell}}, \end{eqnarray*}

we get that the conditions in Lemma 4.1 are satisfied thanks to Theorem 4.4. Therefore, we have

\(\displaystyle \int \| Y \|_{B^{s + 2 / p}_{p, p, \ell}}^p \mu_{N, M, \varepsilon} (\mathrm{d} X, \mathrm{d} Y) \leqslant \frac{1}{\sigma} \int \| \mathcal{G}_{N, M, \varepsilon} (X, 0) \|^{(p r - r + 1) / (p r^2)}_{B_{\exp}^{r, \ell}} \mu_{N, M, \varepsilon} (\mathrm{d} X, \mathrm{d} Y) .\)

4.2Measure of the approximating problem

Equations (4.3) and (4.4) induce a Markov process on \(B_X \times B_Y^{\leqslant}\). This is due to the fact that, if we start from a negative initial condition for \(Y\), then \(Y\) remains negative throughout all the evolution.

Proposition 4.6. The operator \((\mathcal{L}_{N, M, \varepsilon}, \mathcal{F})\) is the restriction of the generator of the Markov process associated to equations (4.3) and (4.4) to the space of functions \(\mathcal{F}\).

Proof. The proof is based on the fact that we can apply Itô formula to the functions in \(\mathcal{F}\). Exploiting Itô formula, the proof follows the argument of the proof of point ii. in Proposition 3.5.\(\Box\)

By linearity, the invariant measure for equation (4.3) is given by the free field measure with mass \(m\) on the torus \(\mathbb{T}^2_M\), that we can identify as usual with the periodic free field of mass \(m\) on the whole space \(\mathbb{R}^2\).

We are interested in studying (infinitesimally) invariant measures for the aforementioned pair of equations, i.e. equations (4.3) and (4.4). Indeed, by Proposition 4.6, an invariant measure for those two equations is a solution to a FPK equation associated with \(\mathcal{L}_{N, M, \varepsilon}\) in the sense of Problem B. Let us start off by giving an argument for the existence of an infinitesimally invariant measure (for its definition and the relation with invariant measures see Chapter 5 in [BKRS15]) by means of the Lyapunov functions introduced in Section 4.1.

Proposition 4.7. Equations (4.3) and (4.4) admit an infinitesimally invariant measure.

Proof. The proof follows the same argument as Lemma 3.3 in [ADRU22]. We start equations (4.3) and (4.4) from deterministic initial conditions \((x_0, y_0) \in B_X \times B_Y^{\leqslant}\), and let \(\mu_{(x_0, y_0), t}\) denote the measure of the solution of the equations starting at \((x_0, y_0)\). Let

\(\displaystyle \tilde{\mu}_T = T^{- 1} \int_0^T \mu_{(x_0, y_0), t} \mathrm{d} t.\)

If such a measure is tight with respect to \(T\), and \(\tilde{\mu}\) is its weak limit as \(T \rightarrow + \infty\), then \(\tilde{\mu}\) is an infinitesimal invariant measure of the equation. We consider the following Lyapunov functions, consisting of the sum of the one considered in Section 4.1 where we dealt with \(X\) and \(Y\) separately (see Remarks 4.3 and 4.5), namely

\(\displaystyle V_1 (X, Y)\) \(\displaystyle =\) \(\displaystyle \| X \|^p_{B^{- s'}_{p, p, \ell}} + \| Y \|_{B^s_{p, p, \ell}}^p,\) (4.11)
\(\displaystyle V_2 (X, Y)\) \(\displaystyle =\) \(\displaystyle (1 - \sigma) \| X \|^p_{B^{- s' + 2 / p}_{p, p, \ell}} + C_{\sigma} \| Y \|_{B^{s + 2 / p}_{p, p, \ell}}^p,\) (4.12)
\(\displaystyle V_3 (X)\) \(\displaystyle =\) \(\displaystyle \frac{1}{\sigma} (C + \| \mathcal{G}_{N, M, \varepsilon} (X, 0) \|^{(p r - r + 1) / (p r^2)}_{B_{\exp}^{r, \ell}}) .\) (4.13)

Then, by Proposition 4.2 and Theorem 4.4, we have

\begin{eqnarray*} \mathbb{E} [V_1 (X_t, Y_t) - V_1 (x_0, y_0)] & = & \mathbb{E} \int_0^t \mathcal{L}_{N, M, \varepsilon} V_1 (X_{\tau}, Y_{\tau}) \mathrm{d} \tau\\ & \leqslant & \int_0^t \mathbb{E} [- V_2 (X_{\tau}, Y_{\tau}) + V_3 (X_{\tau})] \mathrm{d} \tau . \end{eqnarray*}

Recall that \(X_{\tau} = e^{- (- \Delta + m^2) \tau} x_0 + \int_0^{\tau} e^{- (- \Delta + m^2) (\tau - s)} \xi_s \mathrm{d} s\), then \(\mathbb{E} \left[ \|\mathcal{G}_{N, M, \varepsilon} \left( {X_{\tau}} , 0 \right) \|^{(p r - r + 1) / (p r^2)}_{B_{\exp}^{r, \ell}} \right]\) converges exponentially to a constant, and hence

\(\displaystyle K = \sup_{t \in [0, + \infty)} \frac{1}{t} \int_0^t \mathbb{E} [\| \mathcal{G}_{N, M, \varepsilon} (X_{\tau}, 0) \|^{(p r - r + 1) / (p r^2)}_{B_{\exp}^{r, \ell}}] \mathrm{d} \tau < + \infty .\)

Therefore, we have, for any \(t > 0\),

\(\displaystyle \frac{1}{t} \mathbb{E} [V_1 (X_t, Y_t) - V_1 (x_0, y_0)] + \frac{1}{t} \int_0^t \mathbb{E} [V_2 (X_{\tau}, Y_{\tau})] \mathrm{d} \tau \lesssim K,\)

which yields

\(\displaystyle \frac{1}{t} \int_0^t \mathbb{E} [V_2 (X_{\tau}, Y_{\tau})] \mathrm{d} \tau \lesssim K + \frac{1}{t} \mathbb{E} [V_1 (x_0, y_0)] .\)

Taking the supremum over \(t \in [0, + \infty)\), we have

\(\displaystyle \sup_{t \in [0, + \infty)} \frac{1}{t} \int_0^t \mathbb{E} [V_2 (X_{\tau}, Y_{\tau})] \mathrm{d} \tau < + \infty .\)

On the other hand, for any \(t > 0\),

\(\displaystyle \int V_2 (X, Y) \tilde{\mu}_t (\mathrm{d} X, \mathrm{d} Y) = \frac{1}{t} \int_0^t \mathbb{E} [V_2 (X_{\tau}, Y_{\tau})] \mathrm{d} s.\)

Since \(V_2 (X, Y)\) has compact sub-levels on \(B_X \times B_Y^{\leqslant}\), tightness of \(\tilde{\mu}_t\) is implied.\(\Box\)

We can actually prove a stronger result on the form of one invariant measure for equations (4.3) and (4.4). Indeed, if we start from \(x_0 = 0\) and \(y_0 = 0\) in the proof of Proposition 4.7, then we have that the invariant measure that has been built there must be the measure of the process solving the equation

\(\displaystyle X_t = \int_{- \infty}^t P_{t - s} \xi_s \mathrm{d} s, \qquad Y_t = \int_{- \infty}^t P_{t - s} \mathcal{G}_{N, M, \varepsilon} (X_s, Y_s) \mathrm{d} s. \) (4.14)

It is possible, in fact, to prove that the previous integral equations admit a unique solution.

Proposition 4.8. Equation (4.14) admits a unique solution in \(B_X \times \mathfrak{H}_{N, M} \subset B_X \times B_Y\). Moreover, for every \(t \in \mathbb{R}\), the law of \((X_t, Y_t)\) is a solution to the FPK equation for \(\mathcal{L}_{N, M, \varepsilon}\) in the sense of Problem B.

Proof. Existence and uniqueness for equation (4.14) can be proved in the same way as in the proof of Theorem E.1.

In order to prove the second part of the result, we exploit the fact that

\(\displaystyle \operatorname{law} (X_t, Y_t) = \lim_{t \rightarrow + \infty} \mu_{((0, 0), t)} = \lim_{t \rightarrow + \infty} \frac{1}{t} \int_0^t \mu_{((0, 0), s)} \mathrm{d} s.\)

Indeed, if \(X_t^{0, - T}\) and \(Y_t^{0, - T}\) are the processes solution to equations (4.3) and (4.4) starting at time \(- T\) with initial condition zero, then we have that

\(\displaystyle \operatorname{law} (X_t^{0, - T}, Y_t^{0, - T}) = \mu_{((0, 0), t)},\)

and that \((X_t^{0, - T}, Y_t^{0, - T}) \rightarrow (X_t, Y_t)\) as \(T \rightarrow + \infty\), almost surely. Therefore, by the proof of Proposition 4.7, the law of \((X_t, Y_t)\) is an invariant measure for equations (4.3) and (4.4), and thus a solution to the FPK equation for \(\mathcal{L}_{N, M, \varepsilon}\).\(\Box\)

Hereafter, we denote by \(\mu_{N, M, \varepsilon}\) the law at fixed time \(t\) of the process \((X_t, Y_t)\) solution to equation (4.14), which is a solution to the FPK equation for \(\mathcal{L}_{N, M, \varepsilon}\). Thanks to the representation given by Proposition 4.8, we are able to establish some more precise properties of the measure \(\mu_{N, M, \varepsilon}\).

Proposition 4.9. For every \(M, N \in \mathbb{N}\), and every \(\varepsilon > 0\), the measure \(\mu_{N, M, \varepsilon}\) satisfies the following properties

  1. \(\operatorname{supp} (\mu_{N, M, \varepsilon}) \subset B_{2, 2, \ell}^2 (\mathbb{T}^2_M) \times \mathfrak{H}_{N, M}\),

  2. For every \(F \in \mathcal{F}\), we have

    \(\displaystyle \int \mathcal{L}_{N, M, \varepsilon} F \mathrm{d} \mu_{N, M, \varepsilon} = 0,\)

  3. For every \(F, G \in \mathfrak{F}\), we have

    \(\displaystyle \int \mathcal{L}_{N, M, \varepsilon} F (X + Y) G (X + Y) \mu_{N, M, \varepsilon} (\mathrm{d} X, \mathrm{d} Y) = \int F (X + Y) \mathcal{L}_{N, M, \varepsilon} G (X + Y) \mu_{N, M, \varepsilon} (\mathrm{d} X, \mathrm{d} Y) .\)

Proof. Property i. follows from the fact that the solution \(Y_t\) to the second equation in (4.14) is supported on the image of the projection of the operator \(Q_{N, M}\), which is exactly \(\mathfrak{H}_{N, M}\).

Property ii. is due to the fact that \(\mu_{N, M, \varepsilon}\) solves the FPK equation for \(\mathcal{L}_{N, M, \varepsilon}\). Indeed, the system (4.14) can be split in an infinite-dimensional linear equation and an independent finite-dimensional non-linear equation. The statement then follows from the results in Section 2.3 in [Da 04] for the infinite-dimensional part and Theorem 5.2.2 in [BKRS15] for the non-linear finite-dimensional part.

As far as point iii.\(\) is concerned, letting \(Z_t = X_t + Y_t\) and calling \(Z_t^1\) the projection of \(Z_t\) on \(\mathfrak{H}_{N, M}\) and \(Z_t^2\) the projection on \(\mathfrak{H}_{N, M}^{\bot}\), we have that \(Z_t^1\) and \(Z_t^2\) solve two independent equations: the equation for \(Z_t^1\) is a non-linear finite-dimensional equation, since \(\mathfrak{H}_{N, M}\) is a finite-dimensional Hilbert space a linear equation, while the equation for \(Z_t^2\) is a linear equation in infinite dimensions. More precisely, we have

\begin{eqnarray*} \partial_t Z_t^1 & = & (\Delta - m^2) Z^1_t + \frac{\delta V_{N, M, \varepsilon}}{\delta Z} (Z_t^1) + \xi^1_t,\\ \partial_t Z_t^2 & = & (\Delta - m^2) Z^2_t + \xi^2_t, \end{eqnarray*}

where \(\delta\) is the functional derivative of

\(\displaystyle V_{N, M, \varepsilon} (Z) = \int_{\mathbb{T}^2_M} \exp \left( \alpha Q_{N, M} Z (z) - \frac{\alpha^2}{2} c_{N, M, \varepsilon} \right) \mathrm{d} z,\)

with \(c_{N, M, \varepsilon}\) is the constant appearing in the definition (4.2) of the Wick product appearing in the functional \(\mathcal{G}_{N, M, \varepsilon}\), and \(\)\(\xi^1_t\) and \(\xi^2_t\) are the projection of \(\xi\) on the spaces \(\mathfrak{H}_{N, M}\) and \(\mathfrak{H}_{N, M}^{\bot}\), respectively.

Since the equation for \(Z_t^1\) is a finite-dimensional equation with drift given by the gradient of a function and the equation for \(Z_t^2\) is linear with self-adjoint drift, then the unique invariant measure of the process \((Z^1, Z^2)\) satisfies property iii., and hence also their sum \(Z^1 + Z^2 = X + Y\) does. For further details on the relation between symmetric processes and the integration by parts formula see e.g. [AR91, Bog10, Föl88].\(\Box\)

Remark 4.10. From the proof of the previous result it is evident that the system consists of two independent equations: an infinite-dimensional linear one and a finite-dimensional non-linear one. This means that \(\mu_{M, N, \varepsilon}\) is the unique invariant measure of the system.

4.3Tightness of the measure

We prove tightness of the measures \(\mu_{N, M, \varepsilon}\).

Theorem 4.11. The family of measures \((\mu_{N, M, \varepsilon})_{N, M, \varepsilon}\) is tight in \(B_X \times B_Y^{\leqslant}\).
