Proof. Let \(V_1\), \(V_2\), and \(V_3\) be the Lyapunov functions defined in equations (4.11), (4.12), and (4.13), respectively. Since the measures \(\mu_{N, M, \varepsilon}\) are solutions to the FPK equation associated with \(\mathcal{L}_{N, M, \varepsilon}\) in the sense of Problem B and \(V_1 \in \mathcal{F}\), then

\(\displaystyle \int \mathcal{L}_{N, M, \varepsilon} V_1 (X, Y) \mu_{N, M, \varepsilon} (\mathrm{d} X, \mathrm{d} Y) = 0.\)

Moreover, we have by Theorem 4.2 together with Remark 4.3 and by Theorem 4.4 together with Remark 4.5, that \(V_1\) is a Lyapunov function for \((X, Y)\), where \(X\) and \(Y\) solves equation (4.3) and (4.4), respectively. Thus, by Lemma 4.1, we have

\(\displaystyle \int V_2 (X, Y) \mu_{N, M, \varepsilon} (\mathrm{d} X, \mathrm{d} Y) \lesssim \int V_3 (X) \mu_{N, M, \varepsilon} (\mathrm{d} X, \mathrm{d} Y) = \int V_3 (X) \mu^{\operatorname{free}}_M (\mathrm{d} X) .\)

Since \(\sup_{M \in \mathbb{N}} \int V_3 (X) \mu^{\operatorname{free}}_M (\mathrm{d} X) < + \infty\), and since \(V_2\) has compact sub-levels, the thesis follows.\(\Box\)

We get the following consequence.

Corollary 4.12. There exist three sequences \((N_k)_{k \in \mathbb{N}} \subset \mathbb{N}\), \((M_l)_{l \in \mathbb{N}} \subset \mathbb{N}\), and \((\varepsilon_j)_j \subset \mathbb{R}_+\) such that \(N_k \rightarrow + \infty\), \(M_l \rightarrow + \infty\), and \(\varepsilon_j \rightarrow 0\) as \(k, l, j \rightarrow + \infty\), respectively, and some probability measures \(\mu_{M_l, \varepsilon_j}\), \(\mu_{\varepsilon_j}\), and \(\mu\) such that, for any \(M_l\) and any \(\varepsilon_j\), we have

\(\displaystyle \lim_{k \rightarrow + \infty} \mu_{N_k, M_l, \varepsilon_j} = \mu_{M_l, \varepsilon_j},\)

for any \(\varepsilon_j\) we have

\(\displaystyle \lim_{l \rightarrow + \infty} \mu_{M_l, \varepsilon_j} = \mu_{\varepsilon_j},\)

and finally

\(\displaystyle \lim_{j \rightarrow + \infty} \mu_{\varepsilon_j} = \mu .\)

Proof. The result follows from Theorem 4.11 and a diagonalization argument.\(\Box\)

4.4Limit of the operator

We want now to prove that any limit measure \(\mu\) built in Corollary 4.12 solves the FPK equation in the sense of Problem B. We first prove that any measure appearing in Corollary 4.12 solves the FPK equation associated with the corresponding operator. For simplicity, let us drop the dependence on the parameters \(k\), \(l\), and \(j\) introduced in Corollary 4.12.

The aim of the section is to prove the following result.

Theorem 4.13. Let \(\Phi \in \mathcal{F}\). We have the limit as \(N, M \rightarrow + \infty\) and \(\varepsilon \rightarrow 0\),

\(\displaystyle \int \mathcal{L}_{N, M, \varepsilon} \Phi \mathrm{d} \mu_{N, M, \varepsilon} \rightarrow \int \mathcal{L} \Phi \mathrm{d} \mu . \) (4.15)

In order to prove the limit (4.15), we proceed by first taking \(N \rightarrow + \infty\), then \(M \rightarrow + \infty\), and eventually \(\varepsilon \rightarrow 0\), showing the convergence step by step.

4.4.1Limit as \(N \rightarrow + \infty\)

As mentioned above, we start off by taking \(N \rightarrow + \infty\), namely we want to show that

\(\displaystyle \int \mathcal{L}_{N, M, \varepsilon} (\Phi) \mathrm{d} \mu_{N, M, \varepsilon} \rightarrow \int \mathcal{L}_{M, \varepsilon} (\Phi) \mathrm{d} \mu_{M, \varepsilon}, \qquad \text{as } N \rightarrow + \infty .\)

The term in the operator \(\mathcal{L}_{M, N, \varepsilon}\) involving \(X\) only is independent of \(N\), in particular we do not need to consider the trace. Instead, we need to work on the terms involving only the derivatives with respect to \(Y\). Since, by Corollary 4.12, \(\mu_{N, M, \varepsilon}\) is tight and converges weakly up to subsequences to some limit \(\mu_{M, \varepsilon}\), we have to show that

\(\displaystyle \int [\mathcal{L}_{N, M, \varepsilon} (\Phi (X, Y)) -\mathcal{L}_{M, \varepsilon} (\Phi (X, Y))] \mu_{N, M, \varepsilon} (\mathrm{d} X, \mathrm{d} Y) \rightarrow 0, \qquad \text{as } N \rightarrow + \infty .\)

Let us compute the integrand and rearrange its terms. We have

\begin{eqnarray*} & & \mathcal{L}_{N, M, \varepsilon} (\Phi (X, Y)) -\mathcal{L}_{M, \varepsilon} (\Phi (X, Y))\\ & = & \alpha \langle Q_{N, M} (g_{\varepsilon} \ast (: e^{\alpha Q_{N, M} (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X)} : e^{\alpha Q_{N, M} (g_{\varepsilon} \ast Y)})), \nabla_Y \Phi \rangle - \alpha \langle g_{\varepsilon} \ast (: e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X)} : e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast Y)}), \nabla_Y \Phi \rangle\\ & = & \mathbb{I}+\mathbb{I}\mathbb{I}+\mathbb{I}\mathbb{I}\mathbb{I} \end{eqnarray*}

with

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{lll} \mathbb{I} & := & \alpha \langle Q_{N, M} (g_{\varepsilon} \ast (: e^{\alpha Q_{N, M} (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X)} : e^{\alpha Q_{N, M} (g_{\varepsilon} \ast Y)})), \nabla_Y \Phi \rangle - \alpha \langle g_{\varepsilon} \ast (: e^{\alpha Q_{N, M} (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X)} : e^{\alpha Q_{N, M} (g_{\varepsilon} \ast Y)}), \nabla_Y \Phi \rangle\\ & = & \langle g_{\varepsilon} \ast (: e^{\alpha Q_{N, M} (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X)} : e^{\alpha Q_{N, M} (g_{\varepsilon} \ast Y)}), (Q_{N, M} - I) \operatorname{Per}_{\mathbb{T}^2_M} \nabla_Y \Phi \rangle \end{array} \) (4.16)
\(\displaystyle \mathbb{I}\mathbb{I} := \alpha \langle g_{\varepsilon} \ast (: e^{\alpha Q_{N, M} (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X)} : e^{\alpha Q_{N, M} (g_{\varepsilon} \ast Y)}), \nabla_Y \Phi \rangle - \alpha \langle g_{\varepsilon} \ast (: e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X)} : e^{\alpha Q_{N, M} (g_{\varepsilon} \ast Y)}), \nabla_Y \Phi \rangle \) (4.17)

and

\(\displaystyle \mathbb{I}\mathbb{I}\mathbb{I} := \alpha \langle g_{\varepsilon} \ast (: e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X)} : e^{\alpha Q_{N, M} (g_{\varepsilon} \ast Y)}), \nabla_Y \Phi \rangle - \alpha \langle g_{\varepsilon} \ast (: e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X)} : e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast Y)}), \nabla_Y \Phi \rangle \) (4.18)

Let us deal with the term \(\mathbb{I}\) (4.16). We need, for \(p > 1\), the bound

\(\displaystyle \int \| : e^{\alpha Q_{N, M} (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X)} : e^{\alpha Q_{N, M} (g_{\varepsilon} \ast Y)} \|_{L^{\infty}}^p \mathrm{d} \mu_{N, M, \varepsilon} < C_{M, \varepsilon} . \) (4.19)

Indeed, if estimate (4.19) holds, then, by the regularization property of \(g_{\varepsilon}\) – namely the continuity for any \(s > 0\) of the operator \(g_{\varepsilon} \ast (\cdot) : L^{\infty} (\mathbb{T}^2_M) \rightarrow B^s_{\infty, \infty} (\mathbb{T}^2_M)\) – and exploiting the fact that the norm \(\| Q_{N, M} - I \|_{L (B^s_{\infty, \infty}, B^{s - \delta}_{\infty, \infty})}\) converges to zero as \(N \rightarrow + \infty\) for any \(s > 0\) and \(\delta > 0\), we get the convergence of the term \(\mathbb{I}\) to \(0\) as \(N \rightarrow + \infty\). Let us prove the bound (4.19). The exponential involving \(Y\) disappears, since \(Y \leqslant 0\). Moreover, since \(\varepsilon > 0\), we have that the Wick exponential can be written as the exponential divided by some positive constant \(C_{N, M, \varepsilon}\), i.e.

\(\displaystyle : e^{\alpha Q_{N, M} (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X)} : = C_{N, M, \varepsilon}^{- 1} e^{\alpha Q_{N, M} (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X)},\)

which converges to some finite number as \(N \rightarrow + \infty\). Then, we have the inequality

\(\displaystyle \| : e^{\alpha Q_{N, M} (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X)} : \|_{L^{\infty}} \leqslant C_{N, M, \varepsilon}^{- 1} e^{\| \alpha Q_{N, M} (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X) \|_{L^{\infty}}},\)

and hence, by positivity of \(Q_{N, M}\), we obtain

\(\displaystyle \| : e^{\alpha Q_{N, M} (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X)} : \|_{L^{\infty}} \leqslant C_{N, M, \varepsilon}^{- 1} e^{\| \alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X) \|_{L^{\infty}}},\)

which is in \(L^p (\mu_M^{\operatorname{free}})\) by Fernique's theorem (see Theorem 2.8.5 in [Bog98]).

We deal with the term \(\mathbb{I}\mathbb{I}\) given by (4.17). We have

\begin{eqnarray*} \mathbb{I}\mathbb{I} & \leqslant & \alpha \| g_{\varepsilon} \|_{L^1} \| : e^{\alpha Q_{N, M} (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X)} : - : e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X)} : \|_{L^p} \| e^{\alpha Q_{N, M} (g_{\varepsilon} \ast Y)} \|_{L^{\infty}} \| \operatorname{Per}_{\mathbb{T}^2_M} \nabla_Y \Phi \|_{L^q}, \end{eqnarray*}

and, by stochastic estimates (see Proposition B.2), we have convergence of the mean of the term \(\mathbb{I}\mathbb{I}\) to zero.

For the term \(\mathbb{I}\mathbb{I}\mathbb{I}\) given by (4.18) we exploit the non-positivity of \(Y\) and replace the exponential by a bounded smooth function with bounded derivatives. Then we have that

\(\displaystyle e^{\alpha Q_{N, M} (g_{\varepsilon} \ast Y)} \rightarrow e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast Y)}, \qquad \text{in } L^{\infty} .\)

provided

\(\displaystyle Q_{N, M} (g_{\varepsilon} \ast Y) \rightarrow g_{\varepsilon} \ast Y, \qquad \text{in } L^{\infty} .\)

On the other hand, we have the bound

\(\displaystyle \| Q_{N, M} (g_{\varepsilon} \ast Y) - g_{\varepsilon} \ast Y \|_{L^{\infty}} \leqslant \| Q_{N, M} - I \|_{L (C^{\delta}, L^{\infty})} \| g_{\varepsilon} \ast Y \|_{C^{\delta}} .\)

But \(\| Q_{N, M} - I \|_{L (C^{\delta}, L^{\infty})} \rightarrow 0\) depending only on \(M\), while \(\| g_{\varepsilon} \ast Y \|_{C^{\delta}} \in L^p\) uniformly since by the proof of Theorem 4.11 we have that the integral \(\int V_2 (X, Y) \mu_{N, M, \varepsilon} (\mathrm{d} X, \mathrm{d} Y)\) is bounded uniformly with respect to \(M\).

4.4.2Limit as \(M \rightarrow + \infty\)

We now have to take \(M \rightarrow + \infty\), namely we want to show that

\(\displaystyle \int \mathcal{L}_{M, \varepsilon} (\Phi) \mathrm{d} \mu_{M, \varepsilon} \rightarrow \int \mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon} (\Phi) \mathrm{d} \mu_{\varepsilon}, \qquad \text{as } M \rightarrow + \infty . \) (4.20)

Notice that the only \(M\)-dependent term is now the trace-term \(\operatorname{tr}_{L^2 (\mathbb{T}^2_M)} (P_{\mathbb{T}^2_M} \nabla_X^2 \Phi P_{\mathbb{T}^2_M})\). By the same considerations as above, we have to show

\(\displaystyle \int [\mathcal{L}_{M, \varepsilon} (\Phi (X, Y)) -\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon} (\Phi (X, Y))] \mu_{M, \varepsilon} (\mathrm{d} X, \mathrm{d} Y) \rightarrow 0, \qquad \text{as } M \rightarrow + \infty .\)

Since \(\Phi \in \mathcal{F}\) (see Definition 3.1 and Remark 3.2), we have

\(\displaystyle \operatorname{tr}_{L^2} (| \nabla^2_X \Phi |) < f_{\Phi} (X), \qquad \text{and} \qquad \operatorname{tr}_{L^2} (| \rho_{- \ell} \nabla^2_X \Phi |) < f_{\Phi} (X), \quad \ell > 1,\)

where \(\int f_{\Phi} \mathrm{d} \mu^{\operatorname{free}} < + \infty\). First, note that

\begin{eqnarray*} \operatorname{tr}_{L^2} (P_M \nabla^2_X \Phi P_M) -\operatorname{tr}_{L^2} (\nabla^2_X \Phi) & = & \operatorname{tr}_{L^2} ((P_M - I) \nabla^2_X \Phi P_M) +\operatorname{tr}_{L^2} (\nabla^2_X \Phi (P_M - I))\\ & = & \operatorname{tr}_{L^2} ((P_M - I) \nabla^2_X \Phi P_M) +\operatorname{tr}_{L^2} (\nabla^2_X \Phi \rho_{\ell} \rho_{- \ell} (P_M - I))\\ & = & \operatorname{tr}_{L^2} (\nabla^2_X \Phi \rho_{\ell} \rho_{- \ell} (P_M - I)) . \end{eqnarray*}

Therefore, we have the bound

\(\displaystyle | \operatorname{tr}_{L^2} (P_M \nabla^2_X \Phi P_M) -\operatorname{tr}_{L^2} (\nabla^2_X \Phi) | \leqslant \operatorname{tr}_{L^2} (| \nabla^2_X \Phi \rho_{- \ell} |) \| \rho_{\ell} (P_M - I) \|_{L (L^2 (\mathbb{R}^2), L^2 (\mathbb{R}^2))} .\)

Now, let \(h \in L^2 (\mathbb{R}^2)\), then we have

\(\displaystyle (\rho_{\ell} (P_M - I)) (h) = \rho_{\ell} \mathbb{I}_{\mathbb{R}^2 \setminus \mathbb{T}^2_M} (\operatorname{Per}_{\mathbb{T}_M^2} h - h) .\)

Take \(\ell > \ell' > 1\), then, considering the operator \(L^2\) norm, we have

\(\displaystyle \| \rho_{\ell} (P_M - I) (h) \|_{L^2}^2 \leqslant 2 \sum_{y \in 2 \pi \mathbb{Z}^2 \setminus \{ 0 \}} \int_{\mathbb{T}^2_M} \rho_{\ell} (z - y) ^2 h (z)^2 \mathrm{d} z + 2 \int_{\mathbb{R}^2 \setminus \mathbb{T}^2_M} \rho_{\ell} (z) ^2 h (z)^2 \mathrm{d} z.\)

Since \(\rho_{\ell} (z - y) = \rho_{\ell'} (z - y) \rho_{\ell - \ell'} (z - y) \leqslant \rho_{\ell'} (z - y) (1 + M)^{- (\ell - \ell')}\), we get

\begin{eqnarray*} & & \| \rho_{\ell} (P_M - I) (h) \|_{L^2}^2\\ & \lesssim & 2 (1 + M)^{- 2 (\ell - \ell')} \sum_{y \in 2 \pi \mathbb{Z}^2 \setminus \{ 0 \}} \int_{\mathbb{T}^2_M} \rho_{\ell'} (z - y) ^2 h (z)^2 \mathrm{d} z + 2 (1 + M)^{- 2 \ell} \int_{\mathbb{R}^2 \setminus \mathbb{T}^2_M} h (z)^2 \mathrm{d} z. \end{eqnarray*}

Now note

\(\displaystyle \sum_{y \in 2 \pi \mathbb{Z}^2 \setminus \{ 0 \}} \int_{\mathbb{T}^2_M} \rho_{\ell'} (z - y) ^2 h (z)^2 \mathrm{d} z \lesssim \int_{\mathbb{T}^2_M} (\operatorname{Per}_{\mathbb{T}^2_M} (\rho_{\ell'}) (z))^2 h (z)^2 \mathrm{d} z,\)

and \(\operatorname{Per}_{\mathbb{T}^2_M} (\rho_{\ell'}) (z) \leqslant C_{\ell'} := \sum_{y \in \mathbb{Z}^2} \rho_{\ell'} (y)\), to get

\begin{eqnarray*} \| \rho_{\ell} (P_M - I) (h) \|_{L^2}^2 & \lesssim & 2 (1 + M)^{- 2 (\ell - \ell')} C_{\ell'} \int_{\mathbb{T}^2_M} h (z)^2 \mathrm{d} z + 2 (1 + M)^{- 2 \ell} \int_{\mathbb{R}^2 \setminus \mathbb{T}^2_M} h (z)^2 \mathrm{d} z\\ & \lesssim & 2 (1 + M)^{- 2 (\ell - \ell')} (C_{\ell'} + 1) \int_{\mathbb{T}^2_M} h (z)^2 \mathrm{d} z. \end{eqnarray*}

By taking the supremum over all \(h\) with \(\| h \|_{L^2 (\mathbb{R}^2)} \leqslant 1\), we then obtain

\(\displaystyle \| \rho_{\ell} (P_M - I) \|_{L (L^2 (\mathbb{R}^2), L^2 (\mathbb{R}^2))} \lesssim (1 + M)^{- 2 (\ell - \ell')} .\)

Letting \(M \rightarrow + \infty\), we get the limit (4.20).

4.4.3Limit as \(\varepsilon \rightarrow 0\)

Now, we are left to show the convergence

\(\displaystyle \int \mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon} (\Phi) \mathrm{d} \mu_{\varepsilon} \rightarrow \int \mathcal{L} (\Phi) \mathrm{d} \mu, \qquad \text{as } \varepsilon \rightarrow 0.\)

As above, we have to show that

\(\displaystyle \int [\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon} (\Phi (X, Y)) -\mathcal{L} (\Phi (X, Y))] \mu_{\varepsilon} (\mathrm{d} X, \mathrm{d} Y) \rightarrow 0, \qquad \text{as } \varepsilon \rightarrow 0.\)

Rewriting the integrand, we get

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{lll} \mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon} (\Phi (X, Y)) -\mathcal{L} (\Phi (X, Y)) & = & \alpha \langle (g_{\varepsilon} \ast (: e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X)} : e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast Y)})), \nabla_Y \Phi \rangle - \alpha \langle : e^{\alpha X} : e^{\alpha Y}, \nabla_Y \Phi \rangle\\ & = & \mathbb{I}' +\mathbb{I}\mathbb{I}' +\mathbb{I}\mathbb{I}\mathbb{I}' \end{array} \) (4.21)

with

\(\displaystyle \mathbb{I}' := \alpha \langle (g_{\varepsilon} \ast (: e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X)} : e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast Y)})), \nabla_Y \Phi \rangle - \alpha \langle (: e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X)} : e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast Y)}), \nabla_Y \Phi \rangle \) (4.22)
\(\displaystyle \mathbb{I}\mathbb{I}' : = \alpha \langle (: e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X)} : e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast Y)}), \nabla_Y \Phi \rangle - \alpha \langle (: e^{\alpha X} : e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast Y)}), \nabla_Y \Phi \rangle \) (4.23)
\(\displaystyle \mathbb{I}\mathbb{I}\mathbb{I}' := \alpha \langle : e^{\alpha X} : e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast Y)}), \nabla_Y \Phi \rangle - \alpha \langle (: e^{\alpha X} : e^{\alpha Y}), \nabla_Y \Phi \rangle \) (4.24)

The stochastic estimates for \(: \exp (\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X)) :\) are done in Proposition B.1. We now deal with the term \(\mathbb{I}'\) (4.22). We have the inequality

\begin{eqnarray*} \mathbb{I}' & = & \alpha \langle : e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X)} : e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast Y)}, (g_{\varepsilon} - I) \ast \nabla_Y \Phi \rangle\\ & \leqslant & \alpha \| : e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X)} : e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast Y)} \|_{B^s_{p, p, \ell}} \| (g_{\varepsilon} - I) \ast \nabla_Y \Phi \|_{B^{- s}_{q, q, - \ell}}\\ & \lesssim & \alpha \| : e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X)} : \|_{B^s_{p, p, \ell}} \| e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast Y)} \|_{L^{\infty}} \| g_{\varepsilon} - I \|_{L (B^{- s + \delta}_{q, q, - \ell}, B^{- s}_{q, q, - \ell})} \| \nabla_Y \Phi \|_{B^{- s + \delta}_{q, q, - \ell}} . \end{eqnarray*}

Taking the expectation and exploiting the negativity of \(Y\), we have

\begin{eqnarray*} & & \mathbb{E} [\alpha \langle (g_{\varepsilon} \ast (: e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X)} : e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast Y)})) - : e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X)} : e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast Y)}, \nabla_Y \Phi \rangle]\\ & \lesssim & \mathbb{E} [\| : e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X)} : \|_{B^s_{p, p, \ell}}] \| g_{\varepsilon} - I \|_{L (B^{- s + \delta}_{q, q, - \ell}, B^{- s}_{q, q, - \ell})} \| \nabla_Y \Phi \|_{L^{\infty} (B_X \times B_Y, B^{- s + \delta}_{q, q, - \ell})}\\ & \lesssim & \| (g_{\varepsilon} - 1) \|_{L (B^{- s + \delta}_{q, q, - \ell}, B^{- s}_{q, q, - \ell})}, \end{eqnarray*}

and this last term converges to zero as \(\varepsilon \rightarrow 0\). The convergence to zero of the term \(\mathbb{I}\mathbb{I}'\)\(\) (4.23) follows from Proposition B.1. Finally for the term \(\mathbb{I}\mathbb{I}\mathbb{I}'\)\(\) (4.24), we proceed as follows. We have the bound

\(\displaystyle \mathbb{I}\mathbb{I}\mathbb{I}' \lesssim \| : e^{\alpha X} : \|_{B^s_{p, p, \ell}} ((\| Y \|_{B^{- s + \delta}_{q, q, \ell}} \| g_{\varepsilon} - I \|_{L (B^{- s + \delta}_{q, q, - \ell}, B^{- s}_{q, q, - \ell})}) \wedge 2) \| \nabla_Y \Phi \|_{B^{- s + \delta}_{q, q, - \ell}} . \) (4.25)

Since \(\mu_{\varepsilon}\) is tight and \(\| : \exp (\alpha X) : \|_{B^s_{p, p, \ell}}\) is uniformly integrable with respect to the measure \(\mu_{\varepsilon}\), then, for any \(\tau > 0\), there exists a Borel subset \(\Omega_{\tau} \subset B_X \times B_Y\) such that

\(\displaystyle \int_{\Omega_{\tau}^c} \| : e^{\alpha X} : \|_{B^s_{p, p, \ell}} \mathrm{d} \mu_{\varepsilon} < \tau,\)

and \(R_{\Omega_{\tau}} = \sup_{\Omega_{\tau}} \| Y \|_{B^{- s + \delta}_{q, q, \ell}} < + \infty\). Therefore, we have

\begin{eqnarray*} \int | \mathbb{I}\mathbb{I}\mathbb{I}' | \mathrm{d} \mu_{\varepsilon} & \leqslant & \int_{\Omega_{\tau}} | \alpha \langle : e^{\alpha X} : (e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast Y)} - e^{\alpha Y}), \nabla_Y \Phi \rangle | \mathrm{d} \mu_{\varepsilon} + \int_{\Omega_{\tau}^c} | \alpha \langle : e^{\alpha X} : (e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast Y)} - e^{\alpha Y}), \nabla_Y \Phi \rangle | \mathrm{d} \mu_{\varepsilon}\\ & \leqslant & R_{\Omega_{\tau}} \| \nabla_Y \Phi \|_{B^{- s + \delta}_{q, q, - \ell}} \| g_{\varepsilon} - I \|_{L (B^{- s + \delta}_{q, q, - \ell}, B^{- s}_{q, q, - \ell})} \int_{\Omega} \| : e^{\alpha X} : \|_{B^s_{p, p, \ell}} \mathrm{d} \mu + 2 \tau \| \nabla_Y \Phi \|_{B^{- s + \delta}_{q, q, - \ell}} . \end{eqnarray*}

Thus,

\(\displaystyle \lim_{\varepsilon \rightarrow 0} \int | \mathbb{I}\mathbb{I}\mathbb{I}' | \mathrm{d} \mu_{\varepsilon} \leqslant 2 \| \nabla_Y \Phi \|_{B^{- s + \delta}_{q, q, - \ell}} \tau,\)

which gives convergence to zero by arbitrary choice of \(\delta\). In order to show inequality (4.25), note that

\begin{eqnarray*} | \mathbb{I}\mathbb{I}\mathbb{I}' | & \lesssim & \alpha \| : \exp (\alpha X) : (\exp (\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast Y)) - \exp (\alpha Y)) \|_{B^{s - \delta}_{l', l', 2 \ell}} \| \nabla_Y \Phi \|_{B^{- s + \delta}_{l, l, - \ell}}\\ & \lesssim & \alpha \| : \exp (\alpha X) : (\exp (\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast Y)) - \exp (\alpha Y)) \|_{B^s_{1, 1, 2 \ell}} \| \nabla_Y \Phi \|_{B^{- s + \delta}_{l, l, - \ell}}\\ & \lesssim & \alpha [(\| : \exp (\alpha X) : \|_{B^s_{p, p, \ell}} \| (g_{\varepsilon} \ast Y) - Y \|_{B^{- s}_{q, q, \ell}}) \wedge (2 \| : \exp (\alpha X) : \|_{B^s_{1, 1, \ell}})] \| \nabla_Y \Phi \|_{B^{- s + \delta}_{l, l, - \ell}}\\ & \lesssim & (\| : \exp (\alpha X) : \|_{B^s_{p, p, \ell}} + \| : \exp (\alpha X) : \|_{B^s_{1, 1, \ell}}) (\| (g_{\varepsilon} \ast Y) - Y \|_{B^{- s}_{q, q, \ell}} \wedge 2) \| \nabla_Y \Phi \|_{B^{- s + \delta}_{l, l, - \ell}}, \end{eqnarray*}

where \(l \in (1, + \infty)\) and \(1 / l' + 1 / l = 1\) such that \(\delta > 2 - 2 / l'\).Now, if \(| s |\) is small enough such that \(B^{- s + \delta}_{q, q, \ell} \subset B_Y\), then we have the

\(\displaystyle (\| (g_{\varepsilon} \ast Y) - Y \|_{B^{- s}_{q, q, \ell}} \wedge 2) \leqslant (\| Y \|_{B^{- s + \delta}_{q, q, \ell}} \| g_{\varepsilon} - I \|_{L (B^{- s + \delta}_{q, q, - \ell}, B^{- s}_{q, q, - \ell})}) \wedge 2,\)

which is converging to zero for every \(Y \in\)\(B^{- s + \delta}_{q, q, \ell} \subset B_Y\). We conclude the argument by Lebesgue dominated convergence theorem and with the reasonings similar as above.

Appendix ABesov spaces and heat semigroup

In this section, we collect some results about weighted Besov spaces. While we only focus on spaces defined on the whole space \(\mathbb{R}^n\), the results hold also for Besov spaces on the \(n\)-dimensional torus \(\mathbb{T}^n\).

Let us start by introducing Littlewood-Paley blocks. Let \(\chi\) and \(\varphi\) be smooth non-negative functions from \(\mathbb{R}^n\) into \(\mathbb{R}\) satisfying the following properties:

  • \(\operatorname{supp} (\chi) \subset B_{4 / 3} (0)\) and \(\operatorname{supp} (\varphi) \subset B_{8 / 3} (0) \setminus B_{3 / 4} (0)\),

  • \(\chi, \varphi \leqslant 1\) and \(\chi (y) + \sum_{j \geqslant 0} \varphi (2^{- j} y) = 1\), for any \(y \in \mathbb{R}^n\),

  • \(\operatorname{supp} (\chi) \cap \operatorname{supp} (\varphi (2^{- j} \cdot)) = \varnothing\), for \(j \geqslant 1\),

  • \(\operatorname{supp} (\varphi (2^{- j} \cdot)) \cap \operatorname{supp} (\varphi (2^{- i} \cdot)) = \varnothing\), if \(| i - j | > 1\),

where \(B_r (x)\) denotes the ball centered at \(x \in \mathbb{R}^n\) with radius \(r > 0\).

We introduce the following notations: \(\varphi_{- 1} = \chi\), \(\varphi_j (\cdot) = \varphi (2^{- j} \cdot)\), \(D_j = \hat{\varphi}_j\), and for any \(f \in \mathcal{S}' (\mathbb{R}^n)\) we put \(\Delta_j (f) = D_j \ast f\). Moreover, we write, for any \(\ell > 0\), \(\rho_{\ell} (y) = (1 + | y |^2)^{- \ell / 2}\), and let \(L^p_{\ell} (\mathbb{R}^n)\) be the \(L^p\)-space with respect to the norm

\(\displaystyle \| f \|_{L^p_{\ell}} = \left( \int_{\mathbb{R}^n} (f (y) \rho_{\ell} (y))^p \mathrm{d} y \right)^{1 / p},\)

where \(p \in [1, + \infty]\).

Definition A.1. (Besov space \(B^s_{p, q, \ell}\)) Let \(s \in \mathbb{R}\), \(p, q, \in [1, + \infty]\), and \(\ell \in \mathbb{R}\). For \(f \in \mathcal{S}' (\mathbb{R}^n)\), we define the norm

\(\displaystyle \| f \|_{B^s_{p, q, \ell}} = \left( \sum_{j \geqslant - 1} 2^{s q j} \| \Delta_j (f) \|^q_{L^p_{\ell}} \right)^{1 / q} .\)

The space \(B^s_{p, q, \ell} (\mathbb{R}^n)\) is the subset of \(\mathcal{S}' (\mathbb{R}^n)\) such that the norm \(\| \cdot \|_{B^s_{p, q, \ell}}\) is finite.

In the case where \(p = q = + \infty\), the weighted Besov space \(B^s_{\infty, \infty, \ell} (\mathbb{R}^n)\) is denoted by \(C^s_{\ell} (\mathbb{R}^d)\) and it is called weighted Besov-Hölder space with regularity \(s\). Moreover, if \(s \in \mathbb{R}_+ \setminus \mathbb{Z}\), the space \(C^s_{\ell} (\mathbb{R}^d)\) coincides with the Banach space of \(s\)-Hölder-continuous functions.

The relation between weighted Besov spaces is stated in the following result.

Proposition A.2. (Besov embedding) Let \(p_1, p_2, q_1, q_2 \in [1, + \infty]\), \(\ell_1, \ell_2 \in \mathbb{R}\), and \(s_1, s_2 \in \mathbb{R}\) be such that \(s_1 - \frac{n}{p_1} > s_2 - \frac{n}{p_2}\) and \(\ell_1 > \ell_2\). Then, we have the compact immersion

\(\displaystyle B^{s_2}_{p_2, q_2, \ell_2} \subset B^{s_1}_{p_1, q_1, \ell_1} .\)

Proof. The proof can be found in Theorem 6.7 in [Tri06].\(\Box\)

If \(p = q = 2\), the Besov space \(B_{2, 2, \ell}^s\) coincides with the Sobolev space \(H_{\ell}^s\), i.e. the space of measurable tempered distributions \(f\) with bounded norm

\(\displaystyle \| f \|_{H_{\ell}^s}^2 = \int_{\mathbb{R}^n} \rho_{\ell}^2 (y) ((- \Delta + 1)^{s / 2} f)^2 (y) \hspace{1em} \mathrm{d} y.\)

The following theorem allows to extend products between a smooth function and a distribution to elements of Besov spaces.

Theorem A.3. (Paraproduct) Let \(p_1, p_2, p, q_1, q_2, q \in [1, + \infty]\), \(\ell_1, \ell_2, \ell, s_1, s_2, s \in \mathbb{R}\), be such that

\(\displaystyle \frac{1}{p} = \frac{1}{p_1} + \frac{1}{p_2}, \qquad \frac{1}{q} = \frac{1}{q_1} + \frac{1}{q_2}, \qquad \ell = \ell_1 + \ell_2, \qquad s_1 + s_2 > 0, \quad s = s_1 \wedge s_2 . \) (A.1)

Consider the bilinear map \(\Pi : \mathcal{S}' (\mathbb{R}^n) \times \mathcal{S} (\mathbb{R}^n) \rightarrow \mathcal{S}' (\mathbb{R}^n)\), mapping \((f, g) \mapsto \Pi (f, g) = f \cdot g\). Then, there exists a unique continuous extension of \(\Pi\) as the map

\(\displaystyle \Pi : B^{s_1}_{p_1, q_1, \ell_1} (\mathbb{R}^n) \times B^{s_2}_{p_2, q_2, \ell_2} (\mathbb{R}^n) \rightarrow B^s_{p, q, \ell} (\mathbb{R}^n),\)

and we have, for any \(f \in B^{s_1}_{p_1, q_1, \ell_1} (\mathbb{R}^n)\), \(g \in B^{s_2}_{p_2, q_2, \ell_2} (\mathbb{R}^n)\),

\(\displaystyle \| \Pi (f, g) \|_{B^s_{p, q, \ell}} \lesssim \| f \|_{B^{s_1}_{p_1, q_1, \ell_1}} \| g \|_{B^{s_2}_{p_2, q_2, \ell_2}} .\)

Proof. See Section 3.3 in [MW17] for Besov spaces with exponential weights. The proof for polynomial weights follows in a similar way.\(\Box\)

In the case of products between a positive measure and an element of a Besov space, the previous result can be improved as follows.

Proposition A.4. Consider the same parameters as in Theorem A.3 satisfying (A.1) and \(s_1 > 0\), \(s_2 \leqslant 0\). Suppose that \(f \in B^{s_1}_{p_1, q_1, \ell_1} (\mathbb{R}^2) \cap L^{\infty} (\mathbb{R}^2)\) and that \(\mu \in B^{s_2}_{p_2, q_2, \ell_2} (\mathbb{R}^2)\) is a positive measure, then we have

\(\displaystyle \| f \cdot \mu \|_{B^{s_2}_{p_2, q_2, \ell_2}} \lesssim \| f \|_{L^{\infty}} \cdot \| \mu \|_{B^{s_2}_{p_2, q_2, \ell_2}} .\)

Proof. See, e.g., Lemma 28 in [ADG21].\(\Box\)

The next result is an interpolation estimate for Besov spaces.

Proposition A.5. Consider \(p_1, p_2, q_1, q_2 \in [1, + \infty]\), \(\ell_1, \ell_2 \in \mathbb{R}\) and \(s_1, s_2 \in \mathbb{R}\), and write, for any \(\theta \in [0, 1]\),

\(\displaystyle \frac{1}{p_{\theta}} = \frac{\theta}{p_1} + \frac{1 - \theta}{p_2}, \qquad \frac{1}{q_{\theta}} = \frac{\theta}{q_1} + \frac{1 - \theta}{q_2}, \qquad \ell_{\theta} = \theta \ell_1 + (1 - \theta) \ell_2, \qquad s_{\theta} = \theta s_1 + (1 - \theta) s_2 . \qquad\)

If \(f \in B^{s_1}_{p_1, q_1, \ell_1} (\mathbb{R}^n) \cap B^{s_2}_{p_2, q_2, \ell_2} (\mathbb{R}^n)\), then \(f \in B^{s_{\theta}}_{p_{\theta}, q_{\theta}, \ell_{\theta}} (\mathbb{R}^n)\), and furthermore

\(\displaystyle \| f \|_{B^{s_{\theta}}_{p_{\theta}, q_{\theta}, \ell_{\theta}}} \leqslant \| f \|_{B^{s_1}_{p_1, q_1, \ell_1}}^{\theta} \| f \|_{B^{s_2}_{p_2, q_2, \ell_2}}^{1 - \theta} .\)

Proof. The proof is based on the fact that the complex interpolation of the two spaces \(B^{s_1}_{p_1, q_1, \ell_1} (\mathbb{R}^n)\) and \(B^{s_2}_{p_2, q_2, \ell_2} (\mathbb{R}^n)\) is given by \(B^{s_{\theta}}_{p_{\theta}, q_{\theta}, \ell_{\theta}} (\mathbb{R}^n)\). Such an interpolation is shown in Theorem 6.4.5 in [BL76] for unweighted Besov spaces. The proof for weighted spaces follows from the fact that \(f \in B^s_{p, q, \ell}\) if and only if \(f \cdot \rho_{\ell} \in B^s_{p, q}\) (see Theorem 6.5 in [Tri06]).\(\Box\)

We introduce now the heat kernel and present some of its properties. Let \(P_t = e^{- (- \Delta + m^2) t}\), we consider \(f \in L^r_{\ell_1} (\mathbb{R}, B^s_{p, q, \ell_2} (\mathbb{R}^2))\) and define the heat kernel on \(f\) as

\(\displaystyle e^{- (- \Delta + m^2)} f (t) = \int_{- \infty}^t P_{t - \tau} f (\tau) \mathrm{d} \tau .\)

Notice that \(e^{- (- \Delta + m^2)} f (t)\) is a distribution.

More precisely, if \(s > 0\) and if \(f (t, x)\), \((t, x) \in \mathbb{R} \times \mathbb{R}^2\), is a measurable function then

\(\displaystyle e^{- (- \Delta + m^2)} f (t, x) = \int_{- \infty}^t \int_{\mathbb{R}^2} \frac{1}{4 \pi (t - \tau)} e^{- \frac{(x - y)^2}{4 (t - \tau)} + m^2 (t - \tau)} f (\tau, y) \mathrm{d} y \mathrm{d} \tau .\)

We have the following regularization property for \(e^{- (- \Delta + m^2)}\). Let us remark that all the following results hold also in the case where \(f \in L_{\ell_1}^r ([t_1, t_2], B^s_{p, q, \ell_2} (\mathbb{R}^2))\), where \(- \infty \leqslant t_1 < t_2 \leqslant + \infty \), and the operator \(e^{- (- \Delta + m^2)} f (t)\) is defined as the integral from \(t_1\) to \(t \in [t_1, t_2]\).

Theorem A.6. Consider \(r \in [1, + \infty]\), \(p, q \in [1, + \infty]\), \(s \in \mathbb{R}\), and let \(f \in L_{\ell_1}^r (\mathbb{R}, B^s_{p, q, \ell_2} (\mathbb{R}^2))\). Then, for any \(\beta_1, \beta_2 > 0\) such that \(\beta_1 + \beta_2 < 1\), we have

\(\displaystyle e^{- (- \Delta + m^2)} f \in B^{\beta_2}_{r, r, \ell_1} (\mathbb{R}, B^{s + 2 \beta_1}_{p, q, \ell_2} (\mathbb{R}^2)) . \) (A.2)

Notice that (A.2) states that we are gaining regularity \(\beta_2\) in time and \(2 \beta_1\) in space.

In order to prove Theorem A.6, we need the following result saying that when we apply the heat kernel at time \(t\) we gain \(2 \beta_1\) in space-regularity, but we have to pay with a multiplicative factor of \(t^{- \beta_1}\).

Lemma A.7. Let \(m > 0\) and consider \(g \in B^s_{p, q, \ell} (\mathbb{R}^2)\). We have, for every \(t > 0\),

\(\displaystyle \| P_t g \|_{B^{s + 2 \beta_1}_{p, q, \ell} (\mathbb{R}^2)} \lesssim t^{- \beta_1} e^{- m^2 t} \| g \|_{B^s_{p, q, \ell}} .\)

Proof. See Proposition 5 in [MW17].\(\Box\)

We will also need the following lemma saying that giving up some space-regularity we can gain a factor \(t^{\beta}\) on the right-hand side.

Lemma A.8. Consider \(0 < \beta < 1\) and \(g \in B^s_{p, q, \ell} (\mathbb{R}^2)\). Then, for any \(t > 0\),

\(\displaystyle \| (1 - P_t) g \|_{B^{s - 2 \beta}_{p, q, \ell}} \lesssim t^{\beta} \| g \|_{B^s_{p, q, \ell}} .\)

Proof. See Proposition 6 in [MW17].\(\Box\)

Proof of Theorem A.6. We give the proof for unweighted Besov spaces, the general case follows the same lines. We use the difference characterization of space-time Besov spaces (see e.g. Theorem 2.36 in [BCD11] or Chapter 2.6.1 in [Tri92]), which yields

\begin{eqnarray*} \| e^{- (- \Delta - m^2)} f \|^r_{B^{\beta_2}_{r, r} (\mathbb{R}, B_{p, q}^{s + 2 \beta_1} (\mathbb{R}^2))} & \sim & \| e^{- (- \Delta - m^2)} f \|^r_{L^r (\mathbb{R}, B_{p, q}^{s + 2 \beta_1} (\mathbb{R}^2))}\\ & & + \int_{\mathbb{R}} \int_{| \Delta t | \leqslant 1} \frac{\| e^{- (- \Delta - m^2)} f (t + \Delta t) - e^{- (- \Delta - m^2)} f (t) \|^r_{B^s_{p, q}}}{| \Delta t |^{1 + r \beta_2}} \mathrm{d} (\Delta t) \mathrm{d} t. \end{eqnarray*}

First, we prove that the first term on the right-hand side is finite. Write \(\tilde{f} = e^{- (- \Delta + m^2)} f\), we have

\begin{eqnarray*} \| \tilde{f} \|_{L^r (\mathbb{R}, B_{p, q}^{s + 2 \beta_1} (\mathbb{R}^2))}^r & = & \int_{\mathbb{R}} \| \tilde{f} (t) \|^r_{B^{s + 2 \beta_1}_{p, q}} \mathrm{d} t\\ & = & \int_{\mathbb{R}} \left\| \int_{- \infty}^t P_{t - k} f (k) \mathrm{d} k \right\|^r_{B^{s + 2 \beta_1}_{p, q}} \mathrm{d} t\\ & \lesssim & \int_{\mathbb{R}} \left( \int_{- \infty}^t e^{- m^2 (t - k)} \| e^{\Delta (t - k)} f (k) \|_{B^{s + 2 \beta_1}_{p, q}} \mathrm{d} k \right)^r \mathrm{d} t \end{eqnarray*}

Lemma A.7 and Young inequality yield

\begin{eqnarray*} \| \tilde{f} \|_{L^r (\mathbb{R}, B_{p, q}^{s + 2 \beta_1} (\mathbb{R}^2))}^r & \lesssim & \int_{\mathbb{R}} \left( \int_{\mathbb{R}} \frac{\mathbb{I}_{[0, + \infty]} (t - k)}{(t - k)^{\beta_1}} e^{- m^2 (t - k)} \| f (k) \|_{B^s_{p, q}} \mathrm{d} k \right)^r \mathrm{d} t\\ & \lesssim & \left( \int_{\mathbb{R}} \frac{\mathbb{I}_{[0, + \infty]} (t - k)}{(t - k)^{\beta_1}} e^{- m^2 (t - k)} \mathrm{d} k \right)^r + \| f \|^r_{L^r (\mathbb{R}, B^s_{p, q} (\mathbb{R}^n))}, \end{eqnarray*}

where the first integral on the last step is finite if and only if \(\beta_1 < 1\).

Consider now the difference term. We have

\begin{eqnarray*} \tilde{f} (t + \Delta t) - \tilde{f} (t) & = & \int_t^{t + \Delta t} P_{t + \Delta t - k} f (k) \mathrm{d} k + (1 - e^{- (- \Delta + m^2) \Delta t}) \int_{- \infty}^t P_{t - k} f (k) \mathrm{d} k\\ & =: & I_1 + I_2 . \end{eqnarray*}

Now, by Lemma A.7 and Young inequality,

\begin{eqnarray*} \left\| \left( \| I_1 \|_{B^{s + 2 \beta_1}_{p, q}} \right) \right\|_{L^r}^r & \lesssim & \int_{\mathbb{R}} \left( \int_{\mathbb{R}} \frac{\mathbb{I}_{[- \Delta t, 0]} (t - k)}{(t + \Delta t - k)^{\beta_1}} \| f (k) \|_{B^s_{p, q}} \right)^r\\ & \lesssim & (\Delta t)^{1 - \beta_1} \| f \|_{L^r (\mathbb{R}, B^s_{p, q} (\mathbb{R}^n))} \end{eqnarray*}

Consider \(\beta_2 < \tilde{\beta} < 1 - \beta_1\), then, by Lemma A.8,

\begin{eqnarray*} \left\| \left( \| I_2 \|_{B^{s + 2 \beta_1}_{p, q}} \right) \right\|_{L^r}^r & \lesssim & (\Delta t)^{\tilde{\beta}} \| e^{- (- \Delta + m^2)} f \|_{L^r (\mathbb{R}, B^{s + 2 \beta_1 + 2 \tilde{\beta}})}\\ & \lesssim & (\Delta t)^{\tilde{\beta}} \| f \|_{L^r (\mathbb{R}, B^s_{p, q})}, \end{eqnarray*}

where we used the first part of the proof in the last step and the fact that \(\beta_1 + \tilde{\beta} < 1\). Putting everything together, we get

\begin{eqnarray*} \int_{\mathbb{R}} \int_{| \Delta t | \leqslant 1} \frac{\| \tilde{f} (t + \Delta t) - \tilde{f} (t) \|^r_{B^s_{p, q}}}{| \Delta t |^{1 + r \beta_2}} \mathrm{d} (\Delta t) \mathrm{d} t & \lesssim & \| f \|_{L^1 (\mathbb{R}, B^s_{p, q})} \int_{| \Delta t | \leqslant 1} \frac{(\Delta t)^{(1 - \beta_1) r} + (\Delta t)^{\tilde{\beta} r}}{| \Delta t |^{1 + r \beta_2}} \mathrm{d} (\Delta t)\\ & \lesssim & \| f \|_{L^1 (\mathbb{R}, B^s_{p, q})} \int_{| \Delta t | \leqslant 1} \frac{1}{| \Delta t |^{1 - (\tilde{\beta} - \beta_2) r}} \mathrm{d} (\Delta t)\\ & \lesssim & \| f \|_{L^r (\mathbb{R}, B^s_{p, q})}, \end{eqnarray*}

which gives the result.\(\Box\)

Thanks to the heat kernel, we have another representation for weighted Besov spaces.

Proposition A.9. Let \(s \in \mathbb{R}\), \(p, q \in (0, + \infty]\), and \(k \in \mathbb{N}_0\) be such that

\(\displaystyle k > \frac{s}{2} .\)

Consider a smooth and compactly supported function \(\varphi_0\). Then, for any \(m > 0\), we have the following equivalence between norms

\(\displaystyle \| f \|_{B^s_{p, q, \ell}} \simeq \| \mathcal{F}^{- 1} (\varphi_0 \mathcal{F} (f)) \|_{L^p_{\ell}} + \left( \int_0^{+ \infty} t^{\left( k - \frac{s}{2} \right) q} \| \partial_{t^k} (P_t f) \|_{L^p_{\ell}}^q \frac{\mathrm{d} t}{t} \right)^{1 / q} . \) (A.3)

Proof. See Section 2.6.4 in [Tri92] for a version of the theorem with a mass-less heat kernel. In particular, such a result differs from the one presented above since the integral with respect to \(t\) in equation (A.3) goes from \(0\) to \(1\) instead of going from zero to \(+ \infty\). This extension is possible thanks to the regularization properties of the heat kernel and the exponential decay thereof, due to the presence of the positive mass \(m > 0\).\(\Box\)

Remark A.10. Under the same hypotheses as in Proposition A.9, if we assume further that \(s > 0\), then the norm \(\| \mathcal{F}^{- 1} (\varphi_0 \mathcal{F} (f)) \|_{L^p_{\ell}}\) appearing in equation (A.3) can be substituted by the weighted \(L^p\)-norm of \(f\), since we have

\(\displaystyle \| \mathcal{F}^{- 1} (\varphi_0 \mathcal{F} (f)) \|_{L^p_{\ell}} \lesssim \| f \|_{L^p_{\ell}} \lesssim \| f \|_{B^s_{p, q, \ell}} .\)

Namely, for \(s > 0\), equivalence (A.3) becomes

\(\displaystyle \| f \|_{B^s_{p, q, \ell}} \simeq \| f \|_{L^p_{\ell}} + \left( \int_0^1 t^{\left( k - \frac{s}{2} \right) q} \| \partial_{t^k} (P_t f) \|_{L^p_{\ell}}^q \frac{\mathrm{d} t}{t} \right)^{1 / q} .\)

Appendix BStochastic estimates for the Wick exponential

We prove here some stochastic estimates for the term \(: e^{\alpha g_{\varepsilon} \ast X} :\). We need two different kind of estimates. One has to be at the initial time with respect to the Gaussian free field, while the second one needs to estimate the term in some \(L^p\)-space with respect to the variable \(t\). Given \(g_{\varepsilon}\) as in Section 2.2 and \(m > 0\), and \(\mu^{\operatorname{free}}\) being the Gaussian free field with mass \(m\), we define the Wick exponential \(: \exp (\alpha g_{\varepsilon} \ast X) :\) as in equation (2.13).

The previous expression coincides with the standard Wick exponential in the case where we equip the space \(B_X\) with the free field measure \(\mu^{\operatorname{free}}\).

Proposition B.1. Let \(\alpha^2 < 8 \pi\), \(\varepsilon > 0\). Then, for every \(r > 1\) such that \(\alpha^2 r / (4 \pi) < 2\), and for every \(\ell > 0\) such that \(r \ell > 2\), and for every \(\delta > 0\), we have that the sequence

\(\displaystyle : \exp (\alpha g_{\varepsilon} \ast X) : \rightarrow : \exp (\alpha X) :, \qquad \text{in } L^r ((B_X, \mu^{\operatorname{free}}), B^{- \gamma (r - 1) - \delta}_{r, r, \ell} (\mathbb{R}^2)),\)

where \(: \exp (\alpha X) :\) is the unique (positive) limit random distribution and \(\gamma = \alpha^2 / (4 \pi)\).

Proof. We report here the proof for the case \(\alpha^2 < 4 \pi\), the general case can be obtained mixing the method presented here and the techniques by [HKK22]. We consider first the case \(r = 2\). We have, for \(K_j =\mathcal{F}^{- 1} (\varphi_j)\),

\(\displaystyle \mathbb{E} \left[ \int_{\mathbb{R}^2} | [(: e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X)} : - : e^{\alpha X} :) \ast K_j] (z) |^2 (\rho_{\ell} (z))^2 \mathrm{d} z \right] = \int_{\mathbb{R}^2} \mathbb{E} [| [(: e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X)} : - : e^{\alpha X} :) \ast K_j] (z) |^2] (\rho_{\ell} (z))^2 \mathrm{d} z.\)

By translation invariance and orthogonality of Wick polynomials, we can consider

\begin{eqnarray*} \mathbb{E} [| [(: e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X)} : - : e^{\alpha X} :) \ast K_j] (0) |^2] & = & \mathbb{E} [| \langle (: e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X)} : - : e^{\alpha X} :), K_j \rangle |^2]\\ & = & \sum_{n = 0}^{+ \infty} \frac{\alpha^{2 n}}{(n!)^2} \mathbb{E} [| \langle : (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X)^n : - : X^n :, K_j \rangle |^2] . \end{eqnarray*}

It then suffices to show that, for each \(n \in \mathbb{N}\),

\(\displaystyle \mathbb{E} [| \langle : (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X)^n : - : X^n :, K_j \rangle |^2] \rightarrow 0. \) (B.1)

Indeed, for \(| \alpha | < \sqrt{4 \pi}\), we have

\(\displaystyle \sum_{n = 0}^{+ \infty} \frac{\alpha^{2 n}}{(n!)^2} \mathbb{E} [| \langle : (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X)^n : - : X^n :, K_j \rangle |^2] \leqslant 2 \sum_{n = 0}^{+ \infty} \frac{\alpha^{2 n}}{(n!)^2} \mathbb{E} [| \langle : (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X)^n :, K_j \rangle |^2 + | \langle : X^n :, K_j \rangle |^2] .\)

We have then to show

\(\displaystyle \left| \frac{\alpha^{2 n}}{(n!)^2} \mathbb{E} [| \langle : (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X)^n :, K_j \rangle |^2 + | \langle : (X)^n :, K_j \rangle |^2] \right| \leqslant c_n,\)

for some \(\{ c_n \} \in \ell^1 (\mathbb{N})\).

We want an uniform bound on \(\| \Delta_j : (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X)^n : \|_{L^2}\), which would then give, as \(\varepsilon \rightarrow 0\), the convergence \(\Delta_j : (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X)^n : \rightarrow \Delta_j : X^n :\) almost surely.

Notice that

\(\displaystyle \| \Delta_j : (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X)^n : \|_{L^2}^2 = n! \int_{\mathbb{T}^2_M} K_j (x) K_j (x') (\mathcal{X}_{\varepsilon} (x - x'))^n \mathrm{d} x \mathrm{d} x',\)

where

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{X}_{\varepsilon} = g_{\varepsilon}^{\ast 2} \ast \mathcal{X}=\mathcal{F}^{- 1} \left( \frac{| \hat{g}_{\varepsilon} |^2}{| \cdot |^2 + m^2} \right) .\)

By Lemma 2.2 in [ORW21], we have, for some constant \(C > 0\),

\begin{eqnarray*} \mathcal{X}_{\varepsilon} (x) & \lesssim & - \frac{1}{2 \pi} \log (| x | \wedge \varepsilon) + C,\\ \mathcal{X} (x) & \lesssim & - \frac{1}{2 \pi} \log | x | + C. \end{eqnarray*}

Moreover, if \(x \neq 0\), we have the point-wise limits

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{X}_{\varepsilon} (x) \xrightarrow{\varepsilon \rightarrow 0} \mathcal{X} (x) .\)

Now,

\begin{eqnarray*} \| \Delta_j : (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X)^n : \|_{L^2}^2 & = & n! \int_{\mathbb{R}^2} K_j (x) K_j (x') (\mathcal{X}_{\varepsilon} (x - x'))^n \mathrm{d} x \mathrm{d} x'\\ & \lesssim & n! \left( \frac{1}{2 \pi} \log | 2^j | \right)^n,\\ \| \Delta_j : X^n : \|_{L^2}^2 & = & n! \int_{\mathbb{R}^2} K_j (x) K_j (x') (\mathcal{X} (x - x'))^n \mathrm{d} x \mathrm{d} x'\\ & \lesssim & n! \left( \frac{1}{2 \pi} \log | 2^j | \right)^n, \end{eqnarray*}

where the multiplicative constant absorbed in the symbol \(\lesssim\) does not depend on \(j\), \(\varepsilon\), and \(n\).

Summing up, we have

\(\displaystyle \left| \frac{\alpha^{2 n}}{(n!)^2} \mathbb{E} [| \langle : (X^{N, M})^n :, K_j \rangle |^2 + | \langle : (X^M)^n :, K_j \rangle |^2] \right| \lesssim \left| \frac{\alpha^{2 n}}{n!} \left( \frac{1}{2 \pi} \log | 2^j | \right)^n \right| =: c^j_n .\)

Then,

\(\displaystyle c^j = \sum_n c^j_n \lesssim \sum_n \left| \frac{\alpha^{2 n}}{n!} \left( \frac{1}{2 \pi} \log | 2^j | \right)^n \right| \lesssim \operatorname{xp} \left( \frac{\alpha^2}{2 \pi} \log | 2^j | \right) \lesssim 2^{j \alpha^2 / (2 \pi)},\)

which yields

\(\displaystyle \sum_{j \geqslant - 1} 2^{j s} c^j \lesssim \sum_{j \geqslant - 1} 2^{j (\alpha^2 / (2 \pi) + s)} .\)

Therefore, we need

\(\displaystyle s < - \frac{\alpha^2}{4 \pi} .\)

Now, we have

\begin{eqnarray*} \mathbb{E} [\| : \exp (\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X)) : - : \exp (\alpha X) : \|_{B^s_{2, 2, \ell}}^2] & = & \sum_{j \geqslant - 1} 2^{2 j s} \mathbb{E} \| \Delta_j : \exp (\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X)) : - \Delta_j : \exp (\alpha X) : \|^2_{L^2}\\ & \lesssim & \sum_{j \geqslant - 1} 2^{2 j s} \mathbb{E} [\langle K_j, : \exp (\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X)) : - : \exp (\alpha X) : \rangle^2]\\ & \lesssim & \sum_{j \geqslant - 1} \sum_n 2^{2 j s} \frac{\alpha^{2 n}}{(n!)^2} \mathbb{E} [\langle K_j, : (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X)^n : - : X^n : \rangle^2] . \end{eqnarray*}

Notice that each term in the sum converges to \(0\) in \(n\) and \(j\). Moreover, we have an uniform bound in \(n\) and \(j\), since

\(\displaystyle 2^{2 j s} \frac{\alpha^{2 n}}{(n!)^2} \mathbb{E} [\langle K_j, : (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X)^n : - : X^n : \rangle^2] \lesssim 2^{2 j s} c^j_n,\)

where the term on the right-hand side is summable, and therefore it is in \(\ell^1 (\mathbb{N}^2)\). By Lebesgue dominated convergence theorem, everything converges to zero.

We follow the proof of Theorem 3.8 in [BD21] to address the case \(r > 2\). Let \(r > 2\), take \(\gamma > 1\), and recall hypercontractivity of Gaussian Wick monomials (see e.g. Chapter III in [Üst95]):

\(\displaystyle \mathbb{E} [(: X :)^r] \leqslant (r - 1)^{n / 2} (\mathbb{E} [(: X :)^2])^{r / 2} .\)

We have

\begin{eqnarray*} & & \mathbb{E} [| [(: e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X)} : - : e^{\alpha X} :) \ast K_j] (0) |^r]^{1 / r}\\ & = & \mathbb{E} [| \langle (: e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X)} : - : e^{\alpha X} :), K_j \rangle |^r]^{1 / r}\\ & = & \sum_{n = 0}^{+ \infty} \frac{\alpha^n}{n!} \mathbb{E} [| \langle : (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X)^n : - : X^n :, K_j \rangle |^r]^{1 / r}\\ & \leqslant & \sum_{n = 0}^{+ \infty} \frac{(\alpha \sqrt{r - 1})^n}{n!} \mathbb{E} [| \langle : (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X)^n : - : X^n :, K_j \rangle |^2]^{1 / 2}\\ & \lesssim & \left( \frac{\kappa^2}{\kappa^2 - 1} \right)^{1 / 2} \left( \sum_{n = 0}^{+ \infty} \frac{\kappa^2 \alpha^{2 n} (r - 1)^n}{(n!)^2} \mathbb{E} [| \langle : (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X)^n : - : X^n :, K_j \rangle |^2] \right)^{1 / 2}\\ & \lesssim & \left( \frac{\kappa^2}{\kappa^2 - 1} \right)^{1 / 2} \mathbb{E} \left[ \left| \langle : e^{\alpha \kappa \sqrt{r - 1} (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X)} : - : e^{\alpha \kappa \sqrt{r - 1} X} :, K_j \rangle \right|^2 \right]^{1 / 2} . \end{eqnarray*}

Taking \(\kappa\) such that

\(\displaystyle s < - \frac{\alpha^2 \kappa^2 (r - 1)}{4 \pi},\)

we conclude the proof in the same way as in the case \(r = 2\).\(\Box\)

We need also a result for the periodic setting. In particular, we prove the convergence of the Wick exponential \(: e^{\alpha Q_{N, M} (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X)} :\) introduced in equation (4.2), as \(N \rightarrow + \infty\)..

Proposition B.2. Recall that \(\gamma = \alpha^2 / (4 \pi)\). If \(2 \leqslant p < 2 / \gamma\), \(\delta > 0\), \(\ell > 0\), and \(\ell' > \ell'_0 (p)\), for a positive constant depending on \(p\), then we have the convergence, as \(N \rightarrow + \infty\),

\(\displaystyle (: e^{\alpha Q_{N, M} (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X)} : - : e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X)} :) \rightarrow 0, \qquad \text{in } L^p ((C^{- \delta} (\mathbb{T}_M^2), \mu_F^M), L^p (\mathbb{T}_M^2)) .\)

Proof. Notice that, if \(Z\) is a Gaussian random variable, then

\(\displaystyle : e^{\beta Z} : = e^{\beta Z - \frac{\beta^2}{2} \mathbb{E} [Z^2]} .\)

Therefore, applying the previous reasoning for \(Z = Q_{N, M} (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X)\) and first \(\beta = \alpha\), and then \(\)\(\beta = p \alpha\), then we have

\(\displaystyle (: e^{\alpha Q_{N, M} (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X)} :)^p = e^{\alpha p Q_{N, M} (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X) - \frac{1}{2} p \alpha^2 \mathbb{E} [(Q_{N, M} (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X))^2]} = : e^{\alpha p Q_{N, M} (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X)} : e^{\frac{\alpha^2}{2} (p^2 - p) \mathbb{E} [(Q_{N, M} (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X))^2]} .\)

We observe that, if \(X \in C^{- \delta} (\mathbb{T}^2_M)\), then, for every \(x \in \mathbb{T}^2_M\),

\(\displaystyle Q_{N, M} (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X (x)) \rightarrow g_{\varepsilon} \ast X (x), \qquad \text{as } N \rightarrow + \infty .\)

Moreover, we have that

\(\displaystyle \mathbb{E} [(Q_{N, M} (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X))^2] \rightarrow \mathbb{E} [(g_{\varepsilon} \ast X)^2],\)

and also

\(\displaystyle : e^{\alpha Q_{N, M} g_{\varepsilon} \ast X} : \in L^p_{\mu^{\operatorname{free}}_M}, \qquad \text{uniformly.}\)

These last properties imply that

\(\displaystyle : e^{\alpha Q_{N, M} (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X)} : \rightarrow : e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X)} :, \qquad \text{in } L^p_{\mu^{\operatorname{free}}_M} . \) (B.2)

Taking the norms, we have by translation invariance

\(\displaystyle \mathbb{E} \left[ \int_{\mathbb{T}^2_M} | (: e^{\alpha Q_{N, M} (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X)} : - : e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X)} :) (z) |^p \mathrm{d} z \right] = (2 \pi M)^2 \mathbb{E} [| (: e^{\alpha Q_{N, M} (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X)} : - : e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X)} :) (0) |^p] .\)

and the last term converges to zero by (B.2).\(\Box\)

We now prove convergence in space-time of the Wick exponential.

Proposition B.3. Consider the same parameters \(\alpha, p, \ell, \gamma\) as in Proposition B.1. Let \((X_t)_{t \in \mathbb{R}_+}\) be the solution of equation (3.3) with \(X_0\) distributed as a Gaussian free field of mass \(m\). Then we have

\(\displaystyle : \exp (\alpha g_{\varepsilon} \ast X_t) : \rightarrow : \exp (\alpha X_t) :, \qquad \text{in } L^p (B_X \times \Omega, L^p_{\ell'} (\mathbb{R}_+, B^{- \gamma (p - 1) - \delta}_{p, p, \ell} (\mathbb{R}^2))),\)

where \(\ell' > 0\) is such that \(\ell' p > 1\).

Proof. The proof follows closely the one of Proposition B.1. See also Theorem 3.2 in [HKK22] and Lemma 2.5 in [HKK21].\(\Box\)

Appendix CEstimates on linearized PDEs

Consider the partial differential equation

\(\displaystyle (\partial_t - \Delta + m^2) \psi (t, z) = - B (t, z, \psi (t, z)) (A \psi (t, z) + C (t, z)), \qquad (t, z) \in \mathbb{R}_+ \times \mathbb{R}^2, \) (C.1)

where \(B : \mathbb{R}_+ \times \mathbb{R}^2 \times H^1 (\mathbb{R}^2) \rightarrow \mathbb{R}\) is a positive function with compact support with respect to \(z \in \mathbb{R}^2\) independently of \(t \in \mathbb{R}_+\), \(A : B^s_{2, 2, \ell} (\mathbb{R}^2) \rightarrow C^{\infty} (\mathbb{R}^2)\) is a linear and bounded operator which is self-adjoint with respect to the \(L^2 (\mathbb{R}^2)\) Hilbert space structure and which commutes with the Laplacian \(- \Delta\), while \(C : \mathbb{R}_+ \times \mathbb{R}^2 \rightarrow \mathbb{R}\) is a measurable function such that, for any \(t \geqslant 0\), we have

\(\displaystyle \int_0^t \| B (s, \cdot, \psi (s, \cdot)) C (s, \cdot) \|_{L^2}^2 \mathrm{d} s < + \infty,\)

where \(\psi \in L^2 (\mathbb{R}_+, H^1 (\mathbb{R}^2)) \cap L^{\infty} (\mathbb{R}_+, L^2 (\mathbb{R}^2))\).

In this section, we prove some a priori estimates for equation (C.1).

Theorem C.1. Consider equation (C.1) with \(\psi (0, z) \equiv 0\). We have the bound, for some constant \(K > 0\) and for every \(0 < \sigma < 1\),

\(\displaystyle \| A^{1 / 2} \psi (t, \cdot) \|_{L^2}^2 + \int_0^t (\| A^{1 / 2} \psi (s, \cdot) \|_{H^1}^2 + (m^2 - K \sigma) \| A^{1 / 2} \psi (s, \cdot) \|_{L^2}^2) \mathrm{d} s \lesssim \int_0^t \| B (s, \cdot, \psi (s, \cdot)) C (s, \cdot) \|_{L^2}^2 \mathrm{d} s,\)

where the constant implied in the symbol \(\lesssim\) does not depend neither on \(B\) nor on \(C\).

Proof. Multiplying equation (C.1) by \(A \psi (t, z)\) and integrating we have,

\begin{eqnarray*} & & \| A^{1 / 2} \psi (t, \cdot) \|_{L^2}^2 + \int_0^t (\| A^{1 / 2} \psi (s, \cdot) \|_{H^1}^2 + m^2 \| A^{1 / 2} \psi (s, \cdot) \|_{L^2}^2) \mathrm{d} s\\ & = & - \int_0^t \int B (s, z, \psi (s, z)) (A \psi (s, z) + C (s, z)) A \psi (s, z) \mathrm{d} z \mathrm{d} s\\ & = & - \int_0^t \int B (s, z, \psi (s, z)) (A \psi (s, z))^2 \mathrm{d} z \mathrm{d} s\\ & & - \int_0^t \int B (s, z, \psi (s, z)) C (s, z) A \psi (s, z) \mathrm{d} z \mathrm{d} s\\ & \leqslant & - \int_0^t \int B (s, z, \psi (s, z)) C (s, z) A \psi (s, z) \mathrm{d} z \mathrm{d} s. \end{eqnarray*}

Then, exploiting Young's inequality,

\begin{eqnarray*} & & \| A^{1 / 2} \psi (t, \cdot) \|_{L^2}^2 + \int_0^t (\| A^{1 / 2} \psi (s, \cdot) \|_{H^1}^2 + m^2 \| A^{1 / 2} \psi (s, \cdot) \|_{L^2}^2) \mathrm{d} s\\ & \leqslant & - \int_0^t \int B (s, z, \psi (s, z)) C (s, z) A \psi (s, z) \mathrm{d} z \mathrm{d} s\\ & \lesssim & C_{\sigma} \int_0^t \int (B (s, z, \psi (s, z)) C (s, z))^2 \mathrm{d} z \mathrm{d} s + C \sigma \int_0^t \| A \psi (s, \cdot) \|_{L^2}^2 \mathrm{d} s. \end{eqnarray*}

Since \(\| A \psi (s, \cdot) \|_{L^2} = \| A^{1 / 2} A^{1 / 2} \psi (s, \cdot) \|_{L^2} \lesssim \| A^{1 / 2} \psi (s, \cdot) \|_{L^2}\), we can reabsorb the last term on the right-hand side and apply Young's convolution inequality to the remaining term to get

\begin{eqnarray*} & & \| A^{1 / 2} \psi (t, \cdot) \|_{L^2}^2 + \int_0^t (\| A^{1 / 2} \psi (s, \cdot) \|_{H^1}^2 + (m^2 - C \sigma) \| A^{1 / 2} \psi (s, \cdot) \|_{L^2}^2) \mathrm{d} s\\ & \lesssim & C_{\sigma} \int_0^t \int (B (s, z, \psi (s, z)) C (s, z))^2 \mathrm{d} z \mathrm{d} s\\ & \lesssim & \int_0^t \| B (s, \cdot, \psi (s, \cdot)) C (s, \cdot) \|_{L^2}^2 \mathrm{d} s. \end{eqnarray*}

This concludes the proof.\(\Box\)

Let us modify the previous result in order to deal with weighted norms. In particular, we consider the case where \(A \psi = g_{\varepsilon} \ast \psi\), where \(g_{\varepsilon}\) is defined as in Section 3, with \(A^{1 / 2} \psi = \tilde{g}_{\varepsilon} \ast \psi\). Let us also recall the definition \(\rho^k_{\ell} (z) = (1 + k | z |^2)^{- \ell / 2}\), for \(z \in \mathbb{R}^2\) and \(k > 0\).

Theorem C.2. Consider equation (C.1) with \(\psi (0, z) \equiv 0\). Assume further that

\(\displaystyle \| B (\cdot, \cdot, \psi) \|_{L^{\infty} ([0, t], L^{\infty}_{- \ell} (\mathbb{R}^2))} < + \infty .\)

We have the bound, for some constant \(C_1, C_2 > 0\) and for every \(0 < \sigma, \tilde{\sigma} < 1\),

\begin{eqnarray*} & & \| A^{1 / 2} \psi (t, \cdot) \|_{L^2_{- \ell / 2}}^2 + \int_0^t ((1 - \sigma C_1) \| A^{1 / 2} \psi (s, \cdot) \|_{H^1_{- \ell / 2}}^2 + (m^2 - \tilde{\sigma} C_2) \| A^{1 / 2} \psi (s, \cdot) \|_{L^2_{- \ell / 2}}^2) \mathrm{d} s\\ & \lesssim & \int_0^t \| B (s, \cdot, \psi (s, \cdot)) C (s, \cdot) \|_{L^2_{- \ell / 2}}^2 \mathrm{d} s. \end{eqnarray*}

Proof. Instead of multiplying equation (C.1) by \(A \psi (t, z)\), we multiply it by \(A^{1 / 2} (\rho^k_{- \ell} A^{1 / 2} \psi (t, z))\). Proceeding as in the previous proof and noticing that

\(\displaystyle | A^{1 / 2} (\rho^k_{- \ell} A^{1 / 2} \psi (t, z)) | \lesssim \rho^k_{- \ell} (z) | A^{1 / 2} \psi (t, z) |,\)

we have, integrating by parts,

\begin{eqnarray*} & & \| \rho^k_{- \ell / 2} A^{1 / 2} \psi (t, \cdot) \|_{L^2}^2 + \int_0^t (\| \rho^k_{- \ell / 2} A^{1 / 2} \psi (s, \cdot) \|_{H^1}^2 + m^2 \| \rho^k_{- \ell / 2} A^{1 / 2} \psi (s, \cdot) \|_{L^2}^2) \mathrm{d} s\\ & & + \int_0^t \int (\nabla A^{1 / 2} \psi (s, z)) (A^{1 / 2} \psi (s, z)) \nabla \rho^k_{- \ell} (z) \mathrm{d} z \mathrm{d} s\\ & = & - \int_0^t \int B (s, z, \psi (s, z)) (A \psi (s, z) + C (s, z)) A^{1 / 2} (\rho^k_{- \ell} A^{1 / 2} \psi (s, z)) \mathrm{d} z \mathrm{d} s\\ & = & - \int_0^t \int B (s, z, \psi (s, z)) (A \psi (s, z)) (A^{1 / 2} (\rho^k_{- \ell} (z) A^{1 / 2} \psi (s, z))) \mathrm{d} z \mathrm{d} s\\ & & - \int_0^t \int B (s, z, \psi (s, z)) C (s, z) (A^{1 / 2} (\rho^k_{- \ell} (z) A^{1 / 2} \psi (s, z))) \mathrm{d} z \mathrm{d} s. \end{eqnarray*}

Let us focus on the term

\(\displaystyle \int_0^t \int (\nabla A^{1 / 2} \psi (s, z)) (A^{1 / 2} \psi (s, z)) \nabla \rho^k_{- \ell} (z) \mathrm{d} z \mathrm{d} s.\)

Multiplying and dividing by \(\rho^k_{- \ell} (z)\) inside the integrals gives

\(\displaystyle \int_0^t \int \rho^k_{- \ell} (z) (\nabla A^{1 / 2} \psi (s, z)) (A^{1 / 2} \psi (s, z)) \frac{\nabla \rho^k_{- \ell} (z)}{\rho^k_{- \ell} (z)} \mathrm{d} z \mathrm{d} s.\)

By Young's inequality we have

\begin{eqnarray*} & & \left| \int_0^t \int \rho^k_{- \ell} (z) (\nabla A^{1 / 2} \psi (s, z)) (A^{1 / 2} \psi (s, z)) \frac{\nabla \rho^k_{- \ell} (z)}{\rho^k_{- \ell} (z)} \mathrm{d} z \mathrm{d} s \right|\\ & \lesssim & C_{\sigma} \int_0^t \int \rho^k_{- \ell} (z) (A^{1 / 2} \psi (s, z))^2 \left( \frac{\nabla \rho^k_{- \ell} (z)}{\rho^k_{- \ell} (z)} \right)^2 \mathrm{d} z \mathrm{d} s\\ & & + \sigma C_1 \int_0^t \int \rho^k_{- \ell} (z) (\nabla A^{1 / 2} \psi (s, z))^2 \mathrm{d} z \mathrm{d} s, \end{eqnarray*}

and now we have

\(\displaystyle \frac{\nabla \rho^k_{- \ell} (z)}{\rho^k_{- \ell} (z)} \simeq \frac{\ell k | z_1 |}{1 + k | z |^2} \leqslant \ell \sqrt{k} \sup_{z \in \mathbb{R}^2} \frac{| z_1 |}{1 + | z |^2} \leqslant \sqrt{k} C_2 .\)

This yields

\begin{eqnarray*} & & \| \rho^k_{- \ell / 2} A^{1 / 2} \psi (t, \cdot) \|_{L^2}^2 + \int_0^t \left( (1 - \sigma C_1) \| \rho^k_{- \ell / 2} A^{1 / 2} \psi (s, \cdot) \|_{H^1}^2 + \left( m^2 - \sqrt{k} C_{\sigma} \right) \| \rho^k_{- \ell / 2} A^{1 / 2} \psi (s, \cdot) \|_{L^2}^2 \right) \mathrm{d} s\\ & \simeq & - \int_0^t \int B (s, z, \psi (s, z)) (A \psi (s, z)) (A^{1 / 2} (\rho^k_{- \ell} (z) A^{1 / 2} \psi (s, z))) \mathrm{d} z \mathrm{d} s\\ & & - \int_0^t \int B (s, z, \psi (s, z)) C (s, z) (A^{1 / 2} (\rho^k_{- \ell} (z) A^{1 / 2} \psi (s, z))) \mathrm{d} z \mathrm{d} s. \end{eqnarray*}

Applying Young's inequality we get

\begin{eqnarray*} & & \| \rho^k_{- \ell / 2} A^{1 / 2} \psi (t, \cdot) \|_{L^2}^2 + \int_0^t \left( (1 - \sigma C_1) \| \rho^k_{- \ell / 2} A^{1 / 2} \psi (s, \cdot) \|_{H^1}^2 + \left( m^2 - \sqrt{k} C_{\sigma} \right) \| \rho^k_{- \ell / 2} A^{1 / 2} \psi (s, \cdot) \|_{L^2}^2 \right) \hspace{1em} \mathrm{d} s\\ & \lesssim & C_{\sigma_2} \int_0^t \int \rho^k_{- \ell} (z) | B (s, z, \psi (s, z)) |^2 | A \psi (s, z) |^2 \mathrm{d} z \mathrm{d} s + \sigma_2 \int_0^t \int \rho^k_{- \ell} (z) | A^{1 / 2} \psi (s, z) |^2 \mathrm{d} z \mathrm{d} s\\ & & + C_{\sigma_3} \int_0^t \int (\rho^k_{- \ell / 2} (z) B (s, z, \psi (s, z)) C (s, z))^2 \mathrm{d} z \mathrm{d} s + \sigma_3 \int_0^t \int (\rho^k_{- \ell / 2} (z) A^{1 / 2} \psi (s, z))^2 \mathrm{d} z \mathrm{d} s\\ & \lesssim & C_{\sigma_2} \| B (\cdot, \cdot, \psi) \|_{L^{\infty} ([0, t], L^{\infty}_{- \ell} (\mathbb{R}^2))} \int_0^t \int | B (s, z, \psi (s, z)) | | A \psi (s, z) |^2 \mathrm{d} z \mathrm{d} s\\ & & + \sigma_2 \int_0^t \| \rho^k_{- \ell / 2} A^{1 / 2} \psi (s, \cdot) \|_{L^2}^2 \mathrm{d} s\\ & & + C_{\sigma_3} \int_0^t \| \rho^k_{- \ell / 2} B (s, \cdot, \psi (s, \cdot)) C (s, \cdot) \|_{L^2}^2 \mathrm{d} s + \sigma_3 \int_0^t \| \rho^k_{- \ell / 2} A^{1 / 2} \psi (s, \cdot) \|^2_{L^2} \mathrm{d} s, \end{eqnarray*}

reabsorbing the terms multiplied by \(\sigma_2\) and \(\sigma_3\) respectively, to the left-hand side and noticing that from the proof of Theorem C.1 we also get

\(\displaystyle \int_0^t \int B (s, z, \psi (s, z)) (A \psi (s, z))^2 \mathrm{d} z \mathrm{d} s \lesssim \int_0^t \| B (s, \cdot, \psi (s, \cdot)) C (s, \cdot) \|_{L^2}^2 \mathrm{d} s,\)

we have, renaming the constants and introducing \(\tilde{\sigma}\),

\begin{eqnarray*} & & \| A^{1 / 2} \psi (t, \cdot) \|_{L^2_{- \ell / 2}}^2 + \int_0^t ((1 - \sigma C_1) \| A^{1 / 2} \psi (s, \cdot) \|_{H^1_{- \ell / 2}}^2 + (m^2 - \tilde{\sigma} C_2) \| A^{1 / 2} \psi (s, \cdot) \|_{L^2_{- \ell / 2}}^2) \mathrm{d} s\\ & \lesssim & \int_0^t \| B (s, \cdot, \psi (s, \cdot)) C (s, \cdot) \|_{L^2_{- \ell / 2}}^2 \mathrm{d} s. \end{eqnarray*}

This concludes the proof.\(\Box\)

We now apply a bootstrap argument to the previous result to get the following statement.

Corollary C.3. Under the same hypotheses of Theorem C.2, we have

\(\displaystyle \| \psi \|_{B^{\beta}_{q, q, \ell'} (\mathbb{R}_+, B^{2 - 2 \beta - \delta}_{p, p, - \ell} (\mathbb{R}^2))} \lesssim \mathfrak{P}_2 (\| B \|_{L^{\infty}_{\ell'} (\mathbb{R}_+, L_{- \ell}^{\infty} (\mathbb{R}^2))}, \| C \|_{L^{\infty}_{\ell'} (\mathbb{R}_+, L_{- \ell}^{\infty} (\mathbb{R}^2))}),\)

where \(\mathfrak{P}_2\) is a second degree polynomial.

Proof. From Theorem C.2, we know that \(A^{1 / 2} \psi\) lives in \(L^2_{\ell'} (\mathbb{R}_+, H_{- \ell}^1 (\mathbb{R}^2)) \cap L^{\infty}_{\ell'} (\mathbb{R}_+, L_{- \ell}^2 (\mathbb{R}^2))\). By interpolation and using the fact that \(H^1_{- \ell} (\mathbb{R}^2) \subset B^0_{p, p, - \ell} (\mathbb{R}^2)\), for every \(p < + \infty\), we have

\(\displaystyle \| A^{1 / 2} \psi \|_{L_{\ell' / 2}^q (\mathbb{R}_+, B^0_{p, p, - \ell / 2} (\mathbb{R}^2))}^2 \lesssim \int_{\mathbb{R}_+} \rho_{\ell'} (s) \| B (s, \cdot, \psi (s, \cdot)) C (s, \cdot) \|_{L^2_{- \ell / 2}}^2 \mathrm{d} s.\)

Applying the heat kernel to the equation (C.1), we have

\(\displaystyle \psi (t, z) = \int_0^t P_{t - s} (B (s, z, \psi (s, z)) (A \psi (s, z) + C (s, z))) \mathrm{d} s,\)

and therefore, if \(1 / q + 1 / p = 1\) and \(\beta > 0\), we have by Theorem A.6,

\(\displaystyle \| \psi \|_{B^{\beta}_{q, q, \ell'} (\mathbb{R}_+, B^{2 - 2 \beta - \delta}_{p, p, - \ell} (\mathbb{R}^2))} \lesssim \| B (\cdot, \cdot, \psi (\cdot, \cdot)) (A \psi (\cdot, \cdot) + C (\cdot, \cdot)) \|_{L^q_{\ell'} (\mathbb{R}_+, B^0_{p, p, - \ell} (\mathbb{R}^2))} .\)

On the other hand, for some second degree polynomial \(\mathfrak{P}_2\), we have

\begin{eqnarray*} & & \| B (\cdot, \cdot, \psi (\cdot, \cdot)) (A \psi (\cdot, \cdot) + C (\cdot, \cdot)) \|_{L^q_{\ell'} (\mathbb{R}_+, B^0_{p, p, - \ell} (\mathbb{R}^2))}\\ & \leqslant & \mathfrak{P}_2 (\| B \|_{L^{\infty}_{\ell'} (\mathbb{R}_+, L_{- \ell}^{\infty} (\mathbb{R}^2))}, \| C \|_{L^{\infty}_{\ell'} (\mathbb{R}_+, L_{- \ell}^{\infty} (\mathbb{R}^2))}) . \end{eqnarray*}

This concludes the proof.\(\Box\)

Appendix DProof of Lemma 3.4

We give here the proof of Lemma 3.4. We only show the first part of the result, the second one following in a straightforward way. It is sufficient to prove the following property: for any \(G \in \mathcal{F}\) there exists a sequence of cylinder functions \((G_k)_{k \in \mathbb{N}}\) (here we consider a sequence \((G_{N, M})_{N, M \in \mathbb{N}}\) depending on two parameters) such that \(\mathcal{L}G_k \rightarrow \mathcal{L}G\) point-wise as \(k \rightarrow + \infty\), and we have the uniform bound

\(\displaystyle | \mathcal{L}G_k | \leqslant F_G (X), \) (D.1)

for some measurable \(F_G \in L^1 (\mu^{\operatorname{free}})\), e.g. some polynomial of \(X\). And secondly we have to show that \(\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon} G \rightarrow \mathcal{L}G\) point-wise as \(\varepsilon \rightarrow 0\) with a bound \(| \mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon} G | \leqslant F_G (X)\), for some \(F_G \in L^1 (\mu^{\operatorname{free}})\). Since we take \(\mu \in \mathcal{M}_{B_Y}\), the result follows from Lebesgue's dominated convergence theorem.

Take \(G \in \mathcal{F}\) and \(N, M, \in \mathbb{N}\), and define

\(\displaystyle G_{N, M} (X, Y) = G (f_M Q_{N, M} (f_M X), f_M Q_{N, M} (f_M Y)),\)

where \(f_M = f (\cdot / M)\), \(f : \mathbb{R}^2 \rightarrow [0, 1]\) being a compactly supported smooth function in \(\mathbb{T}^2_1\) which is identically \(1\) in a neighborhood of the origin, and \(Q_{N, M}\) is the operator defined in Section 4. We need to show that

\(\displaystyle \nabla_X G_{N, M} (X, Y)\) \(\displaystyle \rightarrow\) \(\displaystyle \nabla_X G (X, Y), \hspace{3em} \text{point-wise in } B_{1, 1, - \ell}^{2 - \delta} (\mathbb{R}^2),\) (D.2)
\(\displaystyle \nabla_Y G_{N, M} (X, Y)\) \(\displaystyle \rightarrow\) \(\displaystyle \nabla_Y G (X, Y), \hspace{3em} \text{point-wise in } B^{(2 - s) \wedge (\gamma (r - 1)) + \delta}_{l, l, - \ell} (\mathbb{R}^2) \text{ for } l \in (1, \infty),\) (D.3)
\(\displaystyle \operatorname{tr} (\nabla^2_X G_{N, M} (X, Y))\) \(\displaystyle \rightarrow\) \(\displaystyle \operatorname{tr} (\nabla^2_X G (X, Y)), \quad \text{point-wise} .\) (D.4)

We focus only on the proof of (D.2) and (D.4) since the limit (D.3) follows with similar arguments. We have,

\begin{eqnarray*} & & \nabla_X G_{N, M} (X, Y) [h]\\ & = & \nabla_X G (f_M Q_{N, M} (f_M X), f_M Q_{N, M} (f_M Y)) [f_M Q_{N, M} (f_M h)], \end{eqnarray*}

On the other hand, since the integrability parameters in the Besov spaces \(\tilde{B}_X\), \(B_Y\), \(B_{1, 1, - \ell}^{2 - \delta} (\mathbb{R}^2)\), and \(B^{(2 - s) \wedge (\gamma (r - 1)) + \delta}_{l, l, - \ell} (\mathbb{R}^2)\) are finite, the linear operator \(Z \mapsto f_M Q_{N, M} (f_M Z)\), where \(Z \in \mathfrak{B}\), where \(\mathfrak{B}\) is anyone of the Besov spaces listed before, strongly converges to the identity \(\operatorname{id}_{\mathfrak{B}}\) on \(\mathfrak{B}\). Therefore, by continuity of \(\nabla_X G\), we get that the following convergence holds as \(N, M \rightarrow + \infty\):

\(\displaystyle \nabla_X G (f_M Q_{N, M} (f_M X), f_M Q_{N, M} (f_M Y)) \rightarrow \nabla_X G (X, Y), \qquad \text{in } B_{1, 1, - \ell}^{2 - \delta} (\mathbb{R}^2) .\)

By strong convergence of the operator \(Z \mapsto f_M Q_{N, M} (f_M Z)\) in \(B_{1, 1, - \ell}^{2 - \delta} (\mathbb{R}^2)\) and the fact that the composition of strongly convergent operators is strongly convergent, we get the limit in (D.2).

Let us now prove (D.4). We have, with the notation \(p_{N, M} = (f_M Q_{N, M} (f_M X), f_M Q_{N, M} (f_M Y))\), and \(p = (X, Y)\),

\(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle \operatorname{tr} (f_M Q_{N, M} f_M \nabla^2 G (p_{N, M}) f_M Q_{N, M} f_M) -\operatorname{tr} (\nabla^2 G (p))\)
\(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle =\) \(\displaystyle \operatorname{tr} (f_M Q_{N, M} f_M \nabla^2 G (p_{N, M}) f_M Q_{N, M} f_M) -\operatorname{tr} (f_M Q_{N, M} f_M \nabla^2 G (p) f_M Q_{N, M} f_M)\) (D.5)
\(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle +\operatorname{tr} (f_M Q_{N, M} f_M \nabla^2 G (p) f_M Q_{N, M} f_M) -\operatorname{tr} (\nabla^2 G (p)) .\)

Let us deal first with the second term of equation (D.5), that is

\(\displaystyle \operatorname{tr} (f_M Q_{N, M} f_M \nabla^2 G (p) f_M Q_{N, M} f_M) -\operatorname{tr} (\nabla^2 G (p)) . \) (D.6)

We have

\begin{eqnarray*} \operatorname{tr} (f_M Q_{N, M} f_M \nabla^2 G (p) f_M Q_{N, M} f_M) & = & \operatorname{tr} (\nabla^2 G (p) (f_M Q_{N, M} f_M)^2)\\ & = & \sum_{n \in \mathbb{N}} (\nabla^2 G (p) (f_M Q_{N, M} f_M)^2 h_n, h_n)_{L^2}\\ & = & \sum_{n \in \mathbb{N}} ((f_M Q_{N, M} f_M)^2 h_n, \nabla^2 G (p) h_n)_{L^2}\\ & = & \sum_{n \in \mathbb{N}} ((f_M Q_{N, M} f_M)^2 h_n^G, \nabla^2 G (p) h_n^G)_{L^2}\\ & = & \sum_{n \in \mathbb{N}} \lambda_n ((f_M Q_{N, M} f_M)^2 h_n^G, h_n^G)_{L^2}, \end{eqnarray*}

where we use that \(f_M\), \(Q_{N, M}\), and \(\nabla^2 G (p)\) are self-adjoint operators in \(L^2 (\mathbb{R}^2)\), and \((h^G_n)_{n \in \mathbb{N}}\) is an orthonormal basis of \(L^2 (\mathbb{R}^2)\) of eigenvectors related to the eigenvalues \((\lambda_n)_{n \in \mathbb{N}}\) of the operator \(\nabla^2 G (p)\) (which exists being \(\nabla^2 G (p)\) a compact operator). Since \(\nabla^2 G (p)\) is also a trace-class operator, we have that \((| \lambda_n |)_{n \in \mathbb{N}} \in \ell^1 (\mathbb{N})\). Furthermore, we get

\(\displaystyle | \lambda_n ((f_M Q_{N, M} f_M)^2 h_n^Q, h_n^Q) | \leqslant | \lambda_n | \| (f_M Q_{N, M} f_M)^2 \|_{L (L^2, L^2)} \leqslant | \lambda_n | (\sup_{N, M \in \mathbb{N}} \| (f_M Q_{N, M} f_M)^2 \|_{L (L^2, L^2)}) \lesssim | \lambda_n | .\)

Since we have the strong convergences \(f_M \rightarrow \operatorname{id}_{L^2 (\mathbb{R}^2)}\) as \(M \rightarrow + \infty\), and \(Q_{N, M} \rightarrow \operatorname{id}_{L^2 (\mathbb{T}_M^2)}\) as \(N \rightarrow + \infty\), and so \(\sup_M \| f_M \|_{L (L^2, L^2)}, \sup_{N, M} \| Q_{N, M} \|_{L (L^2, L^2)} < + \infty\), then we have

\(\displaystyle (f_M Q_{N, M} f_M)^2 \rightarrow \operatorname{id}_{L^2 (\mathbb{R}^2)} \text{ strongly. }\)

Thus, we have \((f_M Q_{N, M} f_M)^2 h_n^G \rightarrow h_n^G\) in \(L^2\) as \(N, M \rightarrow + \infty\), and therefore

\(\displaystyle \{ \lambda_n ((f_M Q_{N, M} f_M)^2 h_n^G, h_n^G) \}_{n \in \mathbb{N}} \rightarrow \{ \lambda_n \}_{n \in \mathbb{N}} \text{ point-wise.}\)

Then the term (D.6) converges to zero by Lebesgue's dominated convergence theorem.

We now show that the first term in equation (D.5), i.e.

\(\displaystyle \operatorname{tr} (f_M Q_{N, M} f_M \nabla^2 G (p_{N, M}) f_M Q_{N, M} f_M) -\operatorname{tr} (f_M Q_{N, M} f_M \nabla^2 G (p) f_M Q_{N, M} f_M) . \) (D.7)

converges to zero. Let \(\mathfrak{M}: H^{\kappa}_{- \ell} (\mathbb{R}^2) \rightarrow L^2 (\mathbb{R}^2)\) be the natural isomorphism between the two spaces, where \(\kappa\) and \(\ell\) are the same parameter as in point iii. of Definition 3.1. The natural identification of \(L^2 (\mathbb{R}^2)\) with its dual, allows us to identify the dual map \(\mathfrak{M}^{\ast}\) of \(\mathfrak{M}\) with the natural isomorphism between \(L^2 (\mathbb{R}^2)\) and \(H^{- \kappa}_{\ell} (\mathbb{R}^2)\). We can then write

\(\displaystyle \nabla^2 G (p) = \mathfrak{M}^{- 1} \mathfrak{M} \nabla^2 G (p) \mathfrak{M}^{\ast} (\mathfrak{M}^{\ast})^{- 1},\)

and therefore, for two points \(p, p_{N, M} \in \tilde{B}_X \times B_Y\),

\begin{eqnarray*} & & \operatorname{tr} (| \nabla^2 G (p) - \nabla^2 G (p_{N, M}) |)\\ & \leqslant & \operatorname{tr} (\mathfrak{M}^{- 1} | \mathfrak{M} \nabla^2 G (p) \mathfrak{M}^{\ast} - \mathfrak{M} \nabla^2 G (p_{N, M}) \mathfrak{M}^{\ast} | (\mathfrak{M}^{\ast})^{- 1})\\ & = & \sum_{n \in \mathbb{N}} (\mathfrak{M}^{- 1} | \mathfrak{M} \nabla^2 G (p) \mathfrak{M}^{\ast} - \mathfrak{M} \nabla^2 G (p_{N, M}) \mathfrak{M}^{\ast} | (\mathfrak{M}^{\ast})^{- 1} h_n, h_n)_{L^2}\\ & = & \sum_{n \in \mathbb{N}} (| \mathfrak{M} \nabla^2 G (p) \mathfrak{M}^{\ast} - \mathfrak{M} \nabla^2 G (p_{N, M}) \mathfrak{M}^{\ast} | (\mathfrak{M}^{\ast})^{- 1} h_n, (\mathfrak{M}^{\ast})^{- 1} h_n)_{L^2}\\ & \leqslant & \| \mathfrak{M} \nabla^2 G (p) \mathfrak{M}^{\ast} - \mathfrak{M} \nabla^2 G (p_{N, M}) \mathfrak{M}^{\ast} \|_{L (L^2, L^2)} \sum_{n \in \mathbb{N}} \| (\mathfrak{M}^{\ast})^{- 1} h_n \|_{L^2}^2\\ & \leqslant & \| \mathfrak{M} \nabla^2 G (p) \mathfrak{M}^{\ast} - \mathfrak{M} \nabla^2 G (p_{N, M}) \mathfrak{M}^{\ast} \|_{L (L^2, L^2)} \operatorname{tr} (\iota_{H^{\kappa}_{- \ell} \hookrightarrow L^2} \mathfrak{M}^{- 1} (\mathfrak{M}^{\ast})^{- 1} \iota_{L^2 \hookrightarrow H^{- \kappa}_{\ell}}), \end{eqnarray*}

which is finite since point iii. in Definition 3.1 holds and the operator \(\iota_{H^{\kappa}_{- \ell} \hookrightarrow L^2} \mathfrak{M}^{- 1} (\mathfrak{M}^{\ast})^{- 1} \iota_{L^2 \hookrightarrow H^{- \kappa}_{\ell}}\) is trace class because \(\kappa > 1\) and \(\ell > 1\) (see Remark 3.2). By continuity of the map \(\nabla^2 G : \tilde{B}_X \times B_Y \rightarrow L (H^{- \kappa}_{\ell} (\mathbb{R}^2), H^{\kappa}_{- \ell} (\mathbb{R}^2))\) and the fact that, by similar arguments as the one exploited to show (D.2), \(p_{N, M} \rightarrow p\) as \(N, M \rightarrow + \infty\), we have that \(\operatorname{tr} (| \nabla^2 G (p) - \nabla^2 G (p_{N, M}) |) \rightarrow 0\) as \(N, M \rightarrow + \infty\).

The argument is then concluded by the following observation: if we take the absolute value of the difference in (D.7), then

\begin{eqnarray*} & & | \operatorname{tr} (f_M Q_{N, M} f_M (\nabla^2 G (p_{N, M}) - \nabla^2 G (p)) f_M Q_{N, M} f_M) |\\ & \leqslant & \operatorname{tr} (f_M Q_{N, M} f_M | \nabla^2 G (p_{N, M}) - \nabla^2 G (p) | f_M Q_{N, M} f_M)\\ & \leqslant & \sup_{N, M \in \mathbb{N}} \| f_M Q_{N, M} f_M \|^2 \operatorname{tr} (| \nabla^2 G (p_{N, M}) - \nabla^2 G (p) | ), \end{eqnarray*}

which converges to zero since \(\sup_{N, M \in \mathbb{N}} \| f_M Q_{N, M} f_M \|^2\) is finite.

In order to get inequality (D.1), we notice that

\(\displaystyle \| \nabla_X G_{N, M} (X, Y) \| \lesssim \| \nabla_X G (X, Y) \| \leqslant F_G (X), \) (D.8)

where \(F_G\) plays the role of \(f_{\Phi}\) in Definition 3.1, as well as similar inequalities for \(\nabla_Y G\) and \(\operatorname{tr} (\nabla^2 G)\), which is due to the fact that the norm of operator \(f_M Q_{N, M} f_M\) is uniformly bounded in \(N, M \in \mathbb{N}\).

The convergence \(\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon} G \rightarrow \mathcal{L}G\) as \(\varepsilon \rightarrow 0\) is proved in Section 4.4.3.

Appendix ETechnical results for the resolvent equation

We consider here the system of equations (3.3)–(3.4), and give a proof of Proposition 3.8. For notation simplicity, we will write \(Y\) instead of \(Y^{\varepsilon}\) when no confusion occurs. Moreover, if not explicitly specified, all the appearing parameters are assumed to be taken as in Definition 2.6.

E.1Flow equations

We start with a result about existence and uniqueness of solutions to the system of equations (3.3)–(3.4), that is the first part of the statement in Proposition 3.8.

Proposition E.1. For any \(\varepsilon > 0\), if \((X_0, Y_0) \in \hat{B}_X \times \{B_Y \cup B_{\exp}^{r, \ell} \}\), then there exists a unique solution \((X, Y)\) to equations (3.3)–(3.4) such that

\(\displaystyle (X_t, Y_t) \in \hat{B}_X \times \{B_Y \cup B_{\exp}^{r, \ell} \}, \qquad t \in \mathbb{R}_+ .\)

Proof. The unique solution to equation (3.3) is given by the explicit formula

\(\displaystyle X_t = P_t X_0 + \int_0^t P_{t - s} \xi_s \mathrm{d} s \) (E.1)

(see, e.g., Theorem 5.4 in [DZ14]). Showing existence of a solution to equation (3.4) is equivalent to showing existence for

\(\displaystyle \tilde{Y}_t = Y_t - P_t Y_0 .\)

We proceed by a Schaefer's fixed point argument (see Theorem 4 in Section 9.2.2 in [Eva10]) to get the result up to a fixed time \(T > 0\). Consider the map \(\mathcal{J}\) given by, for \(t \in [0, T]\),

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{J}_t (\tilde{Y}, Y_0, X) = - \int_0^t P_{t - s} \alpha f_{\varepsilon} : e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X_s)} : e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast \tilde{Y}_s)} e^{\alpha P_s (g_{\varepsilon} \ast Y_0)} \mathrm{d} s.\)

We need to show that \(\mathcal{J}_{\cdot} (\cdot, Y_0, X) : A \rightarrow \bar{A}\) is a continuous and bounded map, where \(A\) and \(\bar{A}\) are the convex closed subsets, for \(\kappa > 0\) small enough, \(\delta > 0\), \(\theta \in (\kappa \vee \delta, 1)\), \(\ell > 0\) large enough, consisting of non-negative functions, and such that

\(\displaystyle A \subset C^{\theta - \kappa} ([0, T], C^{2 - 2 \theta - \delta - \kappa}_{- \ell + \kappa} (\mathbb{R}^2)), \qquad \bar{A} \subset C^{\theta} ([0, T], C^{2 - 2 \theta - \delta}_{- \ell} (\mathbb{R}^2)) .\)

Exploiting the compact embedding

\(\displaystyle C^{\theta - \kappa} ([0, T], C^{2 - 2 \theta - \delta - \kappa}_{- \ell + \kappa} (\mathbb{R}^2)) \hookrightarrow C^{\theta} ([0, T], C^{2 - 2 \theta - \delta}_{- \ell} (\mathbb{R}^2)),\)

given by Besov embedding and Corollary 3 in [Sim87], we can then proceed applying Schaefer's fixed point theorem to get existence for every compact subset of \(\mathbb{R}_+\) of the form \([0, \tau]\), for some \(\tau > 0\). We have

\(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle \| \mathcal{J}_s (\tilde{Y}, Y_0, X) \|_{C^{\theta} ([0, T], C^{2 - 2 \theta - \delta}_{- \ell} (\mathbb{R}^2))}\)
\(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle \lesssim\) \(\displaystyle \| \alpha f_{\varepsilon} : e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X_s)} : e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast \tilde{Y}_s)} e^{\alpha P_s (g_{\varepsilon} \ast Y_0)} \|_{L^{\infty} ([0, T], L^{\infty} (\operatorname{supp} (f_{\varepsilon})))}\) (E.2)
\(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle \lesssim\) \(\displaystyle \| \alpha f_{\varepsilon} : e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X_s)} : e^{\alpha P_s (g_{\varepsilon} \ast Y_0)} \|_{L^{\infty} ([0, T], L^{\infty} (\operatorname{supp} (f_{\varepsilon})))} < + \infty,\)

by the regularization property of the convolution with \(g_{\varepsilon}\).

We have to prove continuity. For every \(\tilde{Y}, \tilde{Y}' \in A\), we have

\begin{eqnarray*} & & \| \mathcal{J} (\tilde{Y}, Y_0, X) -\mathcal{J} (\tilde{Y}', Y_0, X) \|_{C^{\theta} ([0, T], C^{2 - 2 \theta - \delta}_{- \ell} (\mathbb{R}^2))}\\ & \lesssim & \| \alpha f_{\varepsilon} : e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X_s)} : (e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast \tilde{Y}_s)} - e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast \tilde{Y}_s')}) e^{\alpha P_s (g_{\varepsilon} \ast Y_0)} \|_{L^{\infty} ([0, T], L^{\infty} (\operatorname{supp} (f_{\varepsilon})))}\\ & \lesssim & \| e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast \tilde{Y}_s)} - e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast \tilde{Y}_s')} \|_{L^{\infty} ([0, T], L^{\infty} (\operatorname{supp} (f_{\varepsilon})))}\\ & \lesssim & \| g_{\varepsilon} \ast \tilde{Y}_s - g_{\varepsilon} \ast \tilde{Y}_s' \|_{L^{\infty} ([0, T], L^{\infty} (\operatorname{supp} (f_{\varepsilon})))}\\ & \lesssim & \| g_{\varepsilon} \|_{L^1 (\mathbb{R}^2)} \| \tilde{Y}_s - \tilde{Y}_s' \|_{L^{\infty} ([0, T], L^{\infty} (\operatorname{supp} (f_{\varepsilon})))}\\ & \lesssim & \| \tilde{Y}_s - \tilde{Y}_s' \|_{C^{\theta - \kappa} ([0, T], C^{2 - 2 \theta - \delta - \kappa}_{- \ell + \kappa} (\mathbb{R}^2))} . \end{eqnarray*}

We are left to show uniqueness. Take two solutions \(Y\) and \(Y'\) to equation (3.4). Notice that their difference is given by \(Y - Y' = \tilde{Y} - \tilde{Y}'\) and it satisfies

\(\displaystyle (\partial_t - \Delta + m^2) (\tilde{Y}_t - \tilde{Y}'_t) = - \alpha f_{\varepsilon} : e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X)} : (e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast Y_t)} - e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast Y'_t)}) .\)

Introduce a positive function \(h : \mathbb{R} \rightarrow \mathbb{R}\) such that \(h (t) \rightarrow 0\) when \(t \rightarrow - \infty\), and with \(| h' (t) | \leqslant C h (t)\), for some constant \(C > 0\). Multiplying the previous expression by \(h (t) (\tilde{Y}_t - \tilde{Y}'_t)\) and integrating with respect to time and space, we have

\begin{eqnarray*} & & \int_0^T \int_{\mathbb{R}^2} h (t) (\tilde{Y}_t - \tilde{Y}'_t) (\partial_t - \Delta + m^2) (\tilde{Y}_t - \tilde{Y}'_t) \mathrm{d} z \mathrm{d} t\\ & = & - \alpha \int_0^T \int_{\mathbb{R}^2} h (t) (\tilde{Y}_t - \tilde{Y}'_t) f_{\varepsilon} : e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X)} : (e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast Y_t)} - e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast Y'_t)}) \mathrm{d} z \mathrm{d} t. \end{eqnarray*}

For the left-hand side, we get by integration by parts

\begin{eqnarray*} & & \int_0^T \int_{\mathbb{R}^2} h (t) (\tilde{Y}_t - \tilde{Y}'_t) (\partial_t - \Delta + m^2) (\tilde{Y}_t - \tilde{Y}'_t) \mathrm{d} z \mathrm{d} t\\ & = & h (T) \| \tilde{Y}_T - \tilde{Y}'_T \|_{L^2}^2 - \int_0^T h' (t) \| \tilde{Y}_t - \tilde{Y}'_t \|_{L^2}^2 \mathrm{d} t + \int_0^T h (t) (\| \tilde{Y}_t - \tilde{Y}'_t \|_{H^1}^2 + m^2 \| \tilde{Y}_t - \tilde{Y}'_t \|_{L^2}^2) \mathrm{d} t\\ & \geqslant & h (T) \| \tilde{Y}_T - \tilde{Y}'_T \|_{L^2}^2 - \int_0^T | h' (t) | \| \tilde{Y}_t - \tilde{Y}'_t \|_{L^2}^2 \mathrm{d} t + \int_0^T h (t) (\| \tilde{Y}_t - \tilde{Y}'_t \|_{H^1}^2 + m^2 \| \tilde{Y}_t - \tilde{Y}'_t \|_{L^2}^2) \mathrm{d} t\\ & \geqslant & h (T) \left\| \tilde{Y}_T - {\tilde{Y}'_T}' \right\|_{L^2}^2 + \int_0^T h (t) (\| \tilde{Y}_t - \tilde{Y}'_t \|_{H^1}^2 + (m^2 - C) \| \tilde{Y}_t - \tilde{Y}'_t \|_{L^2}^2) \mathrm{d} t. \end{eqnarray*}

Notice that the last line is positive. Therefore,

\begin{eqnarray*} & & h (T) \left\| \tilde{Y}_T - {\tilde{Y}'_T}' \right\|_{L^2}^2 + \int_0^T h (t) (\| \tilde{Y}_t - \tilde{Y}'_t \|_{H^1}^2 + (m^2 - C) \| \tilde{Y}_t - \tilde{Y}'_t \|_{L^2}^2) \mathrm{d} t\\ & \leqslant & - \alpha \int_0^T \int_{\mathbb{R}^2} h (t) (\tilde{Y}_t - \tilde{Y}'_t) : e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X)} : (e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast Y_t)} - e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast Y'_t)}) \mathrm{d} z \mathrm{d} t \leqslant 0, \end{eqnarray*}

which yields uniqueness.\(\Box\)

Remark E.2. The growth with respect to \(T\) of the norm of the solution \(Y\) to equation (3.4) is polynomial. Indeed, recalling that in the proof of Proposition E.1 we have

\(\displaystyle \tilde{Y}_t^{\varepsilon} = - \int_0^t P_{t - s} \alpha f_{\varepsilon} : e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X_s)} : e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast \tilde{Y}_s)} e^{\alpha P_s (g_{\varepsilon} \ast Y_0)} \mathrm{d} s,\)

we get that the time growth of \(\widetilde{Y_t}\) is determined by the growth with respect to \(s\) of the term

\(\displaystyle \alpha f_{\varepsilon} : e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X_s)} : e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast \tilde{Y}_s)} e^{\alpha P_s (g_{\varepsilon} \ast Y_0)} .\)

Now, \(\exp (\alpha e^{- (- \Delta + m^2) s} (g_{\varepsilon} \ast Y_0))\) does not increase in time, while \(\exp (\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast \tilde{Y}_s))\) is bounded since \(\tilde{Y}_s \leqslant 0\), and hence the growth with respect to \(s\) is determined only by the remaining term, that is the exponential \(f_{\varepsilon} : \exp (\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X_s)) :\).

In the next result, we prove continuity of the solutions to equations (3.3)–(3.4) with respect to the initial data.

Lemma E.3. For every \(\varepsilon > 0\), the solution \((X, Y)\) to equations (3.3)–(3.4) are continuous with respect to \(X_0\) in \(\hat{B}_X\) and with respect to \(Y_0\) in \(B_Y \cup B_{\exp}^{r, \ell}\), respectively.

Proof. As far as \(X\) is concerned, continuity follows from the linearity of its representation (E.1). Let us focus on \(Y\). Consider a sequence \((Y_0^n)_{n \in \mathbb{N}}\) converging to some limit \(Y_0\) in \(B_Y \cup B_{\exp}^{r, \ell}\). Then \((Y_0^n)_{n \in \mathbb{N}}\) is bounded in \(B_Y \cup B_{\exp}^{r, \ell}\), and, by the regularization properties of \(g_{\varepsilon}\), we have that \(\exp (\alpha P_t Y_0^n)\) is uniformly bounded with respect to \(z \in \mathbb{R}^2\), \(t\) and \(n\) on the support of \(g_{\varepsilon}\). From inequality (E.2), it follows that the solution \(\tilde{Y}_t (Y_0^{n_k})\) is bounded in the space \(\bar{A} \subset C^{\theta} ([0, \tau], C^{2 - 2 \theta - \delta}_{- \ell} (\mathbb{R}^2))\) and pre-compact in \(A \subset C^{\theta - \kappa} ([0, \tau], C^{2 - 2 \theta - \delta - \kappa}_{- \ell + \kappa} (\mathbb{R}^2))\). Thus, there exists a subsequence \(n_k\) such that \(\tilde{Y}_t (Y_0^{n_k})\) converges to some \(\tilde{Z}\) in \(A\).

On the other hand, since \(\tilde{Y}_t (Y_0^{n_k})\) solves the fixed-point equation

\(\displaystyle \tilde{Y}_t (Y_0^{n_k}) =\mathcal{J}_t (\tilde{Y}_t (Y_0^{n_k}), Y_0^{n_k}, X),\)

and since \(\mathcal{J}\) is continuous with respect to all its variables (see the proof of Proposition E.1), we have

\(\displaystyle \tilde{Z} =\mathcal{J}_t (\tilde{Z}, Y_0, X) .\)
