By uniqueness of solutions to the fixed point equation, we have that \(\tilde{Y}_t (Y_0) = \tilde{Z}\). Finally, since the solution to equation (3.4) with initial data \(Y_0^{n_k}\) is given by

\(\displaystyle Y_t (Y_0^{n_k}) = \tilde{Y}_t (Y_0^{n_k}) + P_t Y_0^{n_k},\)

by the continuity of the heat kernel and of \(\tilde{Y}_t\), we get the thesis.\(\Box\)

E.2Derivatives of the flow

In this section, we denote by \(X\) and \(Y\) the solutions to the system of equations (3.3)–(3.4) and we study their derivatives with respect to the initial data \(X_0\) and \(Y_0\).

E.2.1Existence and equations

We now show point i. in Proposition 3.8.

We have that \(X\) is differentiable and that its derivatives \(\nabla_{X_0} X_t\), \(\nabla^2_{X_0} X_t\), and \(\nabla_{Y_0} X_t\) solve equations (3.5), (3.6), and (3.7), respectively. This is because we have the following explicit representation (cf. equation (E.1)) of the solution

\(\displaystyle X_t = P_t X_0 + \int_0^t P_{t - s} \xi_s \mathrm{d} s,\)

which is linear with respect to the initial data. We immediately get that \(\nabla^2_{X_0} X \equiv 0\) and \(\nabla_{Y_0} X \equiv 0\).

We now focus on the derivatives \(\nabla_{X_0} Y\), \(\nabla_{X_0}^2 Y\), and \(\nabla_{Y_0} Y\) of \(Y\), and show that they exist and satisfy equations (3.8), (3.9), and (3.10), respectively. Furthermore, they are all continuous functions with respect to \(X_0\) and \(Y_0\).

Proposition E.4. For every \(\varepsilon > 0\), we have that that the derivatives \(\nabla_{Y_0} Y\), \(\nabla_{X_0} Y\), and \(\nabla_{X_0}^2 Y\) of the solution \(Y\) to equation (3.4) exist and satisfy equations (3.8), (3.9), and (3.10), respectively.

Proof. We only give the proof for \(\nabla_{Y_0} Y\), the other cases follows in a similar way. Consider the approximating equation (3.4), that is

\(\displaystyle (\partial_t - \Delta + m^2) Y = -\mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon} (X, Y) = - \alpha f_{\varepsilon} : e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X)} : e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast Y)}, \qquad Y (0) = Y_0, \) (E.3)

with \(Y_0 \leqslant 0\). We denote the solution to equation (E.3) as \(Y_t (Z)\), in order to stress the initial condition \(Z \in B_Y \cup B_{\exp}^{r, \ell}\). We have the integral representation of the solution \(Y\) to (E.3):

\(\displaystyle Y_t (Z) = P_t Z - \int_0^t P_{t - s} \mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon} (X_s, Y_s (Z)) \mathrm{d} s.\)

In order to compute the derivative with respect to the initial data, we need to perturb it. Therefore, taking \(\lambda > 0\) and \(h \in B_Y \cup B_{\exp}^{r, \ell}\), and considering the difference, we have

\(\displaystyle Y_t (Y_0 + \lambda h) - Y_t (Y_0) = P_t h \lambda - \int_0^t P_{t - s} (\mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon} (X_s, Y_s (Y_0 + \lambda h)) -\mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon} (X_s, Y_s (Y_0))) \mathrm{d} s.\)

But

\begin{eqnarray*} & & \int_0^t P_{t - s} (\mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon} (X_s, Y_s (Y_0 + \lambda h)) -\mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon} (X_s, Y_s (Y_0))) \mathrm{d} s\\ & = & \int_0^t \int_0^1 P_{t - s} \mathrm{D}_Y \mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon} (X_s, \varsigma Y_s (Y_0 + \lambda h) + (1 - \varsigma) Y_s (Y_0)) (g_{\varepsilon} \ast (Y_s (Y_0 + \lambda h) - Y_s (Y_0))) \mathrm{d} \varsigma \mathrm{d} s, \end{eqnarray*}

since \(\)\(\mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon}\) is differentiable in the direction \(g_{\varepsilon} \ast (Y_s (Y_0 + \lambda h) - Y_s (Y_0))\).

Define

\(\displaystyle H (s, \lambda, h) = \int_0^1 \mathrm{D}_Y \mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon} (X_s, \varsigma Y_s (Y_0 + \lambda h) + (1 - \varsigma) Y_s (Y_0)) \mathrm{d} \varsigma,\)

so that we can write

\(\displaystyle Y_t (Y_0 + \lambda h) - Y_t (Y_0) = P_t h \lambda - \int_0^t P_{t - s} H (s, \lambda, h) \cdot (g_{\varepsilon} \ast (Y_s (Y_0 + \lambda h) - Y_s (Y_0))) \mathrm{d} s.\)

Let us write down an equation for

\(\displaystyle D_{\lambda, h} Y_t (Y_0) = \frac{Y_t (Y_0 + \lambda h) - Y_t (Y_0)}{\lambda} .\)

Notice that

\(\displaystyle D_{\lambda, h} Y_t (Y_0) = P_t h - \int_0^t P_{t - s} H (s, \lambda, h) \cdot (g_{\varepsilon} \ast D_{\lambda, h} Y_t (Y_0)) \mathrm{d} s,\)

and therefore

\(\displaystyle \partial_t (D_{\lambda, h} Y_t (Y_0)) = - (- \Delta + m^2) D_{\lambda, h} Y_t (Y_0) - H (t, \lambda, h) (g_{\varepsilon} \ast D_{\lambda, h} Y_t (Y_0)), \qquad D_{\lambda, h} Y_0 (Y_0) = h. \) (E.4)

Now consider, as in the proof of Proposition E.1,

\(\displaystyle \tilde{Y}_t = Y_t - P_t Y_0 .\)

Then, we have

\(\displaystyle D_{\lambda, h} \tilde{Y}_t (Y_0) = \frac{\tilde{Y}_t (Y_0 + \lambda h) - \tilde{Y}_t (Y_0)}{\lambda} = D_{\lambda, h} Y_t (Y_0) - P_t h,\)

and such a difference satisfies the following equation

\(\displaystyle (\partial_t - \Delta + m^2) D_{\lambda, h} \tilde{Y}_t (Y_0) = - H (t, \lambda, h) (g_{\varepsilon} \ast D_{\lambda, h} \tilde{Y}_t (Y_0) + P_t (g_{\varepsilon} \ast h)), \quad D_{\lambda, h} Y_0 (Y_0) = 0. \) (E.5)

By Theorem C.1, we have then the following bound, for some constant \(K > 0\) and every \(0 < \sigma < 1\):

\(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle \| \tilde{g}_{\varepsilon} \ast D_{\lambda, h} \tilde{Y}_t (Y_0) \|_{L^2}^2 + \int_0^t (\| \tilde{g}_{\varepsilon} \ast D_{\lambda, h} \tilde{Y}_s (Y_0) \|_{H^1}^2 + (m^2 - K \sigma) \| \tilde{g}_{\varepsilon} \ast D_{\lambda, h} \tilde{Y}_s (Y_0) \|_{L^2}^2) \mathrm{d} s\)
\(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle \lesssim\) \(\displaystyle \int_0^t \| H (t, \lambda, h) P_t (g_{\varepsilon} \ast h) \|_{L^2}^2 \mathrm{d} s.\) (E.6)

Consider

\(\displaystyle \hat{Y}_t^{\lambda} (Y_0) = Y_t (Y_0 + \lambda h) - P_t h \lambda = P_t Y_0 - \int_0^t P_{t - s} \mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon} (X_s, Y_s (Y_0 + \lambda h)) \mathrm{d} s.\)

Notice that \(\hat{Y}_t^{\lambda} (Y_0)\) is negative and moreover it solves

\(\displaystyle \hat{Y}_t^{\lambda} (Y_0) = P_t Y_0 - \alpha \int_0^t P_{t - s} f_{\varepsilon} : e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X_s)} : e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast \hat{Y}^{\lambda}_s (Y_0))} e^{\lambda \alpha (g_{\bar{\varepsilon}} \ast P_s h)} \mathrm{d} s.\)

Recall that \(g_{\varepsilon}\) is such that \(g_{\varepsilon} \ast h \in L^{\infty}_{\operatorname{loc}}\) and \(f_{\varepsilon}\) is compactly supported. Therefore,

\(\exp (\alpha (1 - \varsigma) (g_{\varepsilon} \ast Y_s) (Y_0)), \exp (\alpha g_{\varepsilon} \ast \hat{Y}^{\lambda}_s (Y_0)) \in L^{\infty},\) since the exponents are negative,
\(\mathbb{I}_{\operatorname{supp} (f_{\varepsilon})} \exp (\lambda \alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast P_s h)) \in L^{\infty},\) since the exponent is negative on \(\operatorname{supp} (f_{\varepsilon})\).

We then have the uniform estimate on \(H\) given by, for any \(s > 0\) small enough and \(1 < p < + \infty\),

\(\displaystyle \| H (s, \lambda, h) \|_{B^{- s}_{p, p, - \ell}}\) \(\displaystyle \leqslant\) \(\displaystyle \| f_{\varepsilon} : e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X_s)} : \|_{B^{- s}_{p, p, - \ell}} \| e^{\alpha \varsigma (g_{\varepsilon} \ast \hat{Y}^{\lambda}_s (Y_0))} e^{\lambda \varsigma \alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast P_s h)} e^{\alpha (1 - \varsigma) (g_{\varepsilon} \ast Y_s) (Y_0)} \|_{L^{\infty} (\operatorname{supp} (f_{\varepsilon}))}\)
\(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle \leqslant\) \(\displaystyle \| f_{\varepsilon} : e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X_s)} : \|_{B_{p, p, - \ell}^{- s}} \| e^{\lambda \varsigma \alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast P_s h)} \|_{L^{\infty} (\operatorname{supp} (f_{\varepsilon}))}\)
\(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle \leqslant\) \(\displaystyle \| f_{\varepsilon} : e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X_s)} : \|_{B^{- s}_{p, p, - \ell}} \| e^{\alpha | g_{\varepsilon} \ast P_s h |} \|_{L^{\infty} (\operatorname{supp} (f_{\varepsilon}))},\) (E.7)

which is uniform both in \(\lambda\) and \(\xi\).

This gives existence of a limit for \(D_{\lambda, h} Y_t (Y_0)\) as \(\lambda \rightarrow 0\).

We are left to show that the limit satisfies equation (3.8). First, we have to prove that the following limit holds

\(\displaystyle \lim_{\lambda \rightarrow 0} H (t, \lambda, h) = \alpha^2 f_{\varepsilon} : e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X_t)} : e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast Y_t (Y_0))},\)

in some suitable space.

By Lemma E.3, we have that \(Y_t (Y_0 + \lambda h) \rightarrow Y_t (Y_0)\) as \(\lambda \rightarrow 0\) in \(B_Y \cup B_{\exp}^{r, \ell}\), and hence, thanks to the regularization properties of \(g_{\varepsilon}\), \(g_{\varepsilon} \ast Y_t (Y_0 + \lambda h)\) converges to \(g_{\varepsilon} \ast Y_t (Y_0)\) uniformly on the support of \(f_{\varepsilon}\). Thus, we have the weak convergence

\(\displaystyle H (t, \lambda, h) \rightarrow \alpha^2 f_{\varepsilon} : e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X_t)} : e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast Y_t (Y_0))}, \qquad \text{as } \lambda \rightarrow 0, \) (E.8)

and, by the uniform estimates (E.7) and the compact embedding \(B^{- s}_{p, p, - \ell} \hookrightarrow B^{- s'}_{p, p, 0}\), with \(s' > s\) (see Proposition A.2), we get the strong convergence in the space \(B^{- s'}_{p, p, 0}\).

We then have a weak limit for \(D_{\lambda, h} Y_t (Y_0)\) as \(\lambda \rightarrow 0\). Thus, proceeding as in the proof of Lemma E.3, thanks to the a priori estimate (E.6), to the uniform bound (E.7), and to the convergence (E.8), we get that the limit of \(D_{\lambda, h} Y_t (Y_0)\) as \(\lambda \rightarrow 0\) is a solution to equation (3.8).\(\Box\)

E.2.2Properties of the flow derivatives

We prove here some bounds on \(\nabla_{X_0} Y_t (Y_0)\), \(\nabla_{Y_0} Y_t (Y_0)\), and on the trace of \(\nabla^2_{X_0} Y_t (Y_0)\). Let us recall that the sets \(\tilde{B}_Y\) and \(\tilde{B}_X\) are defined as in (3.11).

Proposition E.5. For every \(\delta \in (0, 1)\), \(\theta \in (0, 1 - \delta)\), \(\ell, \ell' \geqslant 1\) and \(h \in \tilde{B}_Y\), we have the estimate

\begin{eqnarray*} & & \| \nabla_{Y_0} Y_t (Y_0) (h) \|_{C^{\theta}_{\ell'} (\mathbb{R}_+, C_{- \ell}^{2 - 2 \theta - 2 \delta} (\mathbb{R}^2)) \oplus L^{\infty} (\mathbb{R}_+, \tilde{B}_Y)}\\ & \lesssim_{g_{\varepsilon}} & \mathfrak{P}_2 (\| f_{\varepsilon} : e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X_t)} : e^{\alpha P_t (g_{\varepsilon} \ast h)} \|_{L^{\infty}_{\ell'} (\mathbb{R}_+, L^{\infty}_{- \ell} (\mathbb{R}^2))}, \| h \|_{\tilde{B}_Y}), \end{eqnarray*}

where \(\mathfrak{P}_2\) is a second degree polynomial.

Proof. We use the same notation as in the proof of Proposition E.4. Recall that

\(\displaystyle D_{\lambda, h} Y_t (Y_0) = D_{\lambda, h} \tilde{Y}_t (Y_0) + P_t h,\)

and then, since \(P_t h\) is uniformly bounded in \(\tilde{B}_Y\), in order to prove the result, it suffices to give an estimate on \(D_{\lambda, h} \tilde{Y}_t (Y_0)\) in the space \(C^{\theta}_{\ell'} (\mathbb{R}_+, C_{- \ell}^{2 - 2 \theta - 2 \delta} (\mathbb{R}^2))\).

Let us consider a time-weight \(\rho_{\ell'} (t)\) and a space-weight \(\rho_{- \ell} (x)\) defines as

\(\displaystyle \rho_l^k (z) = (1 + k | z |^2)^{- l / 2}, \qquad z \in \mathbb{R}_+, \mathbb{R}^2 .\)

Then, by Theorem C.2 we have, for some \(0 < \sigma < 1\),

\begin{eqnarray*} & & \rho_{2 \ell'} (t) \| \rho_{- \ell} (\tilde{g}_{\varepsilon} \ast D_{\lambda, h} \tilde{Y}_t (Y_0)) \|_{L^2}^2\\ & & + \int_0^t \rho_{2 \ell'} (s) (\| \rho_{- \ell} (\tilde{g}_{\varepsilon} \ast D_{\lambda, h} \tilde{Y}_s (Y_0)) \|_{H^1}^2 + (m^2 - \sigma C) \| \rho_{- \ell} (\tilde{g}_{\varepsilon} \ast D_{\lambda, h} \tilde{Y}_s (Y_0)) \|_{L^2}^2) \mathrm{d} s\\ & \lesssim & C_{\delta} \int_0^t \rho_{2 \ell'} (s) \int \rho_{- 2 \ell} (x) (\tilde{g}_{\varepsilon} \ast (H (s, \lambda, h) e^{- (- \Delta + m^2) s} (g_{\varepsilon} \ast h)))^2 \mathrm{d} x \mathrm{d} s. \end{eqnarray*}

Moreover, applying Corollary C.3 yields

\begin{eqnarray*} & & \| H (s, \lambda, h) (\tilde{g}_{\varepsilon} \ast D_{\lambda, h} \tilde{Y}_s (Y_0) + P_s (\tilde{g}_{\varepsilon} \ast h)) \|_{L^q_{\ell'} (\mathbb{R}_+, B^0_{p, p, - \ell} (\mathbb{R}^2))}\\ & \leqslant & \mathfrak{P}_2 (\| H \|_{L^{\infty}_{\ell'} (\mathbb{R}_+, L_{- \ell}^{\infty} (\mathbb{R}^2))}, \| \tilde{g}_{\varepsilon} \ast h \|_{L^{\infty}_{- \ell} (\mathbb{R}^2)}) . \end{eqnarray*}

Together with the previous estimate, this yields uniform bounds on the norm \(\| D_{\lambda, h} \tilde{Y}_t (Y_0) \|_{B^{\beta}_{q, q, \ell'} (\mathbb{R}_+, B^{2 - 2 \beta - \delta}_{p, p, - \ell} (\mathbb{R}^2))}\), and choosing \(\beta\) and \(p\) accordingly we then deduce that we have \(D_{\lambda, h} \tilde{Y}_t (Y_0) \in C_{\ell'}^{\delta} (\mathbb{R}_+, C_{- \ell}^{2 - \delta'} (\mathbb{R}^2))\), uniformly in \(\lambda\). Now, letting \(\lambda \rightarrow 0\) yields the result.\(\Box\)

Proposition E.6. For every \(\delta \in (0, 1)\), \(\theta \in (0, 1 - \delta)\), \(\ell, \ell' \geqslant 1\) and \(h \in \tilde{B}_X\), we have the estimate

\(\displaystyle \| \nabla_{X_0} Y_t (Y_0) (h) \|_{C^{\theta}_{\ell'} (\mathbb{R}_+, C_{- \ell}^{2 - 2 \theta - 2 \delta} (\mathbb{R}^2))} \lesssim_{g_{\varepsilon}} P_2 (\|f_{\varepsilon} : e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X_t)} : e^{\alpha P_t (g_{\varepsilon} \ast h)} \|_{L^{\infty}_{\ell'} (\mathbb{R}_+, L^{\infty}_{- \ell} (\mathbb{R}^2))}),\)

where \(P_2\) is a second degree polynomial.

Proof. The proof is similar to the one of Proposition E.5, the only difference is that here the initial data of \(\nabla_{X_0} Y_t\) is zero and therefore we do not need to subtract it before doing the estimates in Theorem C.2 and in Corollary C.3.\(\Box\)

We now deal with the trace term appearing in the definition of the operator \(\mathcal{L}\).

Proposition E.7. For every \(\ell, \kappa \geqslant 0\), there exist \(\beta, \delta > 0\) such that

\(\displaystyle \| \nabla_{X_0}^2 Y_t (Y_0) \|_{L (H^{- \kappa}_{\ell}, H^{\kappa}_{- \ell})} \lesssim \left( \int_{\mathbb{R}^2} \alpha^2 f_{\varepsilon} (z') : e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X_t) (z')} : e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast Y_t) (z')} e^{(\delta - m^2) t} (1 + | z' |^{\beta}) \mathrm{d} z' \right)^2 .\)

It follows that, whenever \(\ell > 1\) and \(\kappa > 1\),

\(\displaystyle \operatorname{tr} (| \nabla_{X_0}^2 Y_t (Y_0) \rho_{- \ell} |) \lesssim \left( \int_{\mathbb{R}^2} \alpha^2 f_{\varepsilon} (z') : e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X_t) (z')} : e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast Y_t) (z')} e^{(\delta - m^2) t} (1 + | z' |^{\beta}) \mathrm{d} z' \right)^2 .\)

Proof. We suppose that all the computations involving equations (3.9) and (3.10) make sense since a rigorous proof of this fact can be given in a similar way as in the proof of Proposition E.5.

Let \(\delta_{\cdot}\) be the Dirac delta distribution, and consider the map, for \(X_0 \in B_X\) and \(t \in \mathbb{R}_+\),

\(\displaystyle T_{X_0, t} : H^{- \kappa}_{\ell} (\mathbb{R}^2) \rightarrow H^{\kappa}_{- \ell} (\mathbb{R}^2), \quad h \mapsto \nabla^2_{X_0} Y_t (Y_0) (h, \delta_{\cdot}) .\)

Hereafter, we drop the dependence on \(Y_0\) in the derivatives of \(Y\). Let us then evaluate equations (3.9) and (3.10) at \(\delta_z (\cdot)\), and \((h, \delta_z (\cdot))\), respectively, to get

\(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle (\partial_t - (\Delta - m^2)) \nabla_{X_0} Y_t (\delta_z) (z')\)
\(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle =\) \(\displaystyle - \alpha^2 f_{\varepsilon} (z') : e^{\alpha g_{\varepsilon} \ast X_t (z')} : e^{\alpha g_{\varepsilon} \ast Y_t (z')} (P_t (g_{\varepsilon} \ast \delta_z) (z') + (g_{\varepsilon} \ast \nabla_{X_0} Y_t (\delta_z)) (z'))\)
\(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle =\) \(\displaystyle - \alpha^2 f_{\varepsilon} (z') : e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X_t) (z')} : e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast Y_t) (z')} (P_t g_{\varepsilon} (z - z') + (g_{\varepsilon} \ast \nabla_{X_0} Y_t (\delta_z)) (z')),\) (E.9)

and

\(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle (\partial_t - (\Delta - m^2)) \nabla^2_{X_0} Y_t (h, \delta_z) (z')\)
\(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle =\) \(\displaystyle - \alpha^2 f_{\varepsilon} : e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X_t) (z')} : e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast Y_t) (z')} (g_{\varepsilon} \ast \nabla^2_{X_0} Y_t (h, \delta_z)) (z')\)
\(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle - \alpha^3 f_{\varepsilon} : e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X_t) (z')} : e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast Y_t) (z')} (g_{\varepsilon} \ast P_t h) (z') ((g_{\varepsilon} \ast P_t \delta_z) (z') + (g_{\varepsilon} \ast \nabla_{X_0} Y_t (\delta_z)) (z'))\) (E.10)
\(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle - \alpha^3 f_{\varepsilon} : e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X_t) (z')} : e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast Y_t) (z')} (g_{\varepsilon} \ast \nabla_{X_0} Y_t (h)) (z') ((g_{\varepsilon} \ast P_t \delta_z) (z') + (g_{\varepsilon} \ast \nabla_{X_0} Y_t (\delta_z)) (z')) .\)

Using similar methods as in the proofs of Proposition E.4 and Proposition E.5, it is possible to prove that \(\nabla_{X_0} Y_t (\delta_z)\) and \(\nabla^2_{X_0} Y_t (h, \delta_z)\) are differentiable infinitely many times with respect to \(z\) and their derivatives with respect to \(z\) for any multi-index \(\beta\) satisfy

\(\displaystyle \partial^{\beta}_z (g_{\varepsilon} \ast \nabla_{X_0} Y_t (\delta_z)) (z') = \int g_{\varepsilon} (z' - y) \partial^{\beta}_z \nabla_{X_0} Y_t (\delta_z) (y) \mathrm{d} y.\)

Furthermore, by equations (E.9) and (E.10), we get

\(\displaystyle (\partial_t - (\Delta - m^2)) \partial_z^{\beta} \nabla_{X_0} Y_t (\delta_z) (z')\) \(\displaystyle =\) \(\displaystyle - \alpha^2 f_{\varepsilon} (z') : e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X_t) (z')} : e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast Y_t) (z')} P_t \partial_z^{\beta} g_{\varepsilon} (z - z')\) (E.11)
\(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle - \alpha^2 f_{\varepsilon} (z') : e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X_t) (z')} : e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast Y_t) (z')} (g_{\varepsilon} \ast \partial^{\beta}_z \nabla_{X_0} Y_t (\delta_z)) (z'),\)

and

\(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle (\partial_t - (\Delta - m^2)) \partial_z^{\beta} \nabla^2_{X_0} Y_t (h, \delta_z) (z')\)
\(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle =\) \(\displaystyle - \alpha^2 f_{\varepsilon} (z') : e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X_t) (z')} : e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast Y_t) (z')} (\partial_{\cdot}^{\beta} g_{\varepsilon} \ast \nabla^2_{X_0} Y_t (h, \delta_z)) (z')\) (E.12)
\(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle - \alpha^3 f_{\varepsilon} (z') : e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X_t) (z')} : e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast Y_t) (z')} (g_{\varepsilon} \ast P_t h) (z') ((\partial_{\cdot}^{\beta} g_{\varepsilon} \ast P_t \delta_z) (z') + (g_{\varepsilon} \ast \partial^{\beta}_z \nabla_{X_0} Y_t (\delta_z)) (z'))\)
\(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle - \alpha^3 f_{\varepsilon} (z') : e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X_t) (z')} : e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast Y_t) (z')} (g_{\varepsilon} \ast \nabla_{X_0} Y_t (h)) (z') ((\partial_{\cdot}^{\beta} g_{\varepsilon} \ast P_t \delta_z) (z') + (g_{\varepsilon} \ast \partial^{\beta}_z \nabla_{X_0} Y_t (\delta_z)) (z')) .\)

Exploiting Theorem C.2 applied to equation (E.11), we get

\begin{eqnarray*} & & \| \tilde{g}_{\varepsilon} \ast \partial_z^{\beta} \nabla_{X_0} Y_t (\delta_z) \|^2_{L^2} + \int_0^t \| \tilde{g}_{\varepsilon} \ast \partial_z^{\beta} \nabla_{X_0} Y_{\tau} (\delta_z) \|^2_{H^1} \mathrm{d} \tau\\ & \lesssim & \int \alpha^2 f_{\varepsilon} (z') : e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X_t) (z')} : e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast Y_t) (z')} | P_t \partial_z^{\beta} g_{\varepsilon} (z - z') |^2 \mathrm{d} z' =: F_{\beta} (z) . \end{eqnarray*}

By a bootstrap argument, i.e. applying Corollary C.3, we can conclude that

\(\displaystyle \| \partial_z^{\beta} \nabla_{X_0} Y_t (\delta_z) \|^2_{B^s_{2, 2}} \leqslant \rho_{- \ell'} (t) F_{\beta} (z) q_s (F_{\beta} (z)),\)

where \(q_s\) is an \(s\)-dependent polynomial, because of the relation of the heat kernel with the support of \(f_{\varepsilon}\).

By a similar method as above, we get the following estimate concerning the second derivative

\(\displaystyle \| \partial_z^{\beta} \nabla^2_{X_0} Y_t (h, \delta_z) \|_{B_{2, 2}^s} \leqslant \| h \|_{H^{- \kappa}_{\ell}} F_{\beta} (z) \tilde{q}_s (F_{\beta} (z)),\)

where \(\tilde{q}_s\) is another \(s\)-dependent polynomial. Taking \(s\) large enough, we have

\(\displaystyle \| \partial_z^{\beta} \nabla^2_{X_0} Y_t (h, \delta_z) \|_{L^{\infty}} \leqslant \| h \|_{H^{- \kappa}_{\ell}} F_{\beta} (z) \tilde{q}_s (F_{\beta} (z)) .\)

This proves that the map is linear with respect to \(h\) as a map from \(L^2\), moreover, if \(s \in \mathbb{N}\) and fixing \(z'\) and \(h\), the norm with respect to \(z\) is given by

\(\displaystyle \| \nabla^2_{X_0} Y_t (h, \delta_{\cdot}) (z') \|_{B_{2, 2, - \ell}^s (\mathrm{d} z)}^2\) \(\displaystyle =\) \(\displaystyle \sum_{| \beta | \leqslant s} \int \rho_{- \ell}^2 (z) (\partial_z^{\beta} \nabla^2_{X_0} Y_t (h, \delta_z) (z'))^2 \mathrm{d} z\)
\(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle \lesssim\) \(\displaystyle \sum_{| \beta | \leqslant s} \int \rho_{- \ell}^2 (z) \| \partial_z^{\beta} \nabla^2_{X_0} Y_t (h, \delta_z) (z') \|^2_{L^{\infty} (\mathrm{d} z')} \mathrm{d} z\)
\(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle \lesssim\) \(\displaystyle \| h \|_{H^{- \kappa}_{\ell}} \sum_{| \beta | \leqslant s} \int \rho_{- \ell}^2 (z) | F_{\beta} (z) \tilde{q}_s (F_{\beta} (z)) |^2 \mathrm{d} z.\) (E.13)

We are left to show that the last integral is finite. Recall

\(\displaystyle F_{\beta} (z) = \int \alpha^2 f_{\varepsilon} (z') : e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X_t) (z')} : e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast Y_t) (z')} | P_t \partial_z^{\beta} g_{\varepsilon} (z - z') |^2 \mathrm{d} z' .\)

Since we have to show that the integral is finite for some polynomial, we multiply (and divide) the heat kernel by some weight \(\rho_{- 2 \ell}\), to get

\(\displaystyle F_{\beta} (z) \rho_{- 2 \ell} (z) = \int \alpha^2 f_{\varepsilon} (z') : e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X_t) (z')} : e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast Y_t) (z')} | \rho_{- \ell} (z) P_t \partial_z^{\beta} g_{\varepsilon} (z - z') |^2 \mathrm{d} z' .\)

By inequality (6.2) in Section 6 of [Tri06], we have, for some \(\kappa > 0\),

\(\displaystyle \rho_{- \ell} (z) \leqslant \rho_{- \ell} (z - \tilde{z}) (1 + | \tilde{z} |)^{\kappa} .\)

Therefore,

\begin{eqnarray*} | \rho_{- \ell} (z) P_t \partial_z^{\beta} g_{\varepsilon} (z - z') | & = & \left| \int_{\mathbb{R}^2} \frac{1}{2 \pi t} e^{- \frac{| \tilde{z} |^2}{2 t} - m^2 t} \partial_z^{\beta} g_{\varepsilon} (z - z' - \tilde{z}) \rho_{- \ell} (z) \mathrm{d} \tilde{z} \right|\\ & \leqslant & \int_{\mathbb{R}^2} \frac{1}{2 \pi t} e^{- \frac{| \tilde{z} |^2}{2 t} - m^2 t} | \partial_z^{\beta} g_{\varepsilon} (z - z' - \tilde{z}) | \rho_{- \ell} (z) \mathrm{d} \tilde{z}\\ & \leqslant & \int_{\mathbb{R}^2} \frac{1}{2 \pi t} e^{- \frac{| \tilde{z} |^2}{2 t} - m^2 t} | \partial_z^{\beta} g_{\varepsilon} (z - z' - \tilde{z}) | \rho_{- \ell} (z - z' - \tilde{z}) (1 + | z' + \tilde{z} |)^{\kappa} \mathrm{d} \tilde{z} . \end{eqnarray*}

By the compact support of \(g_{\varepsilon}\), we have

\begin{eqnarray*} | \rho_{- \ell} (z) P_t \partial_z^{\beta} g_{\varepsilon} (z - z') | & \leqslant & C_{\ell, g, \varepsilon, \beta} \int_{\mathbb{R}^2} \frac{1}{2 \pi t} e^{- \frac{| \tilde{z} |^2}{2 t} - m^2 t} (1 + | z' + \tilde{z} |)^{\kappa} \mathrm{d} \tilde{z}\\ & = & C_{\ell, g, \varepsilon, \beta} e^{- m^2 t} \mathbb{E}_W [(1 + | z' + W_t |)^{\kappa}], \end{eqnarray*}

where \(W\) is some two-dimensional Brownian motion. Thus, if we take \(\kappa\) large enough, we get

\begin{eqnarray*} | \rho_{- \ell} (z) P_t \partial_z^{\beta} g_{\varepsilon} (z - z') | & \leqslant & C_{\kappa, \ell, g, \varepsilon, \beta} e^{- m^2 t} (1 + | z' |^{\kappa} +\mathbb{E} [| W_t |^{\kappa}])\\ & \leqslant & C_{\kappa, \ell, g, \varepsilon, \beta, \delta} e^{(\delta - m^2) t} (1 + | z' |^{\beta}) . \end{eqnarray*}

Then,

\(\displaystyle F_{\beta} (z) \rho_{- 2 \ell} (z) \leqslant C_{\kappa, \ell, g, \varepsilon, \beta, \delta} \int \alpha^2 f_{\varepsilon} (z') : e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast X_t) (z')} : e^{\alpha (g_{\varepsilon} \ast Y_t) (z')} e^{(\delta - m^2) t} (1 + | z' |^{\kappa}) \mathrm{d} z',\)

which is finite, since \(f_{\varepsilon}\) is compactly supported.\(\Box\)
